# Prominent English Study Bibles

This section profiles 27 study Bibles available in English, chosen for a combination of reach (sales and general use), denominational/traditional breadth (so the list is not accidentally dominated by one publisher or tradition), and representation of the 27 Bible translations tracked throughout this book (see "Popular Bible Translations" and "Histories of English Bible Translations"). Entries are presented in an estimated order of overall reach -- combining known sales figures where available, general cultural and denominational reach, and the underlying translation's own popularity as a secondary factor. This ordering is necessarily an estimate rather than a precise, uniformly sourced ranking: a handful of entries near the top (the NIV Study Bible, the Life Application Study Bible, the ESV Study Bible, the Scofield Reference Bible) have real, checkable sales figures or well-documented historical influence behind their position, while entries further down the list, especially the last several, are ordered by genuinely weaker and more inferential evidence, and this section says so directly rather than presenting false precision.

A number of the 27 tracked translations (WEB, Darby, YLT, Smith's Literal Translation, Webster's Bible, the AKJV, the ERV, the CPDV, TRB, and, so far as this research could confirm, the NRSV-CE and the BSB specifically) do not appear to have a comparable, widely available dedicated study Bible edition, typically because they are older public-domain works, specialty/niche translations, or free digital-first texts not usually paired with a full study apparatus; this section says so directly rather than manufacturing an entry that does not exist. The ASV is the one apparent exception among the otherwise-unrepresented older translations: a dedicated ASV-based study Bible was published in 1972, described below, though it was never followed by a second edition and today survives mainly as a historical curiosity.

For each entry, this section documents: the translation the study Bible is built on; the general editor's (or lead commentator's) biography, independently verified rather than assumed from the volume's own promotional material; the volume's denominational leaning, cross-referenced to the fuller doctrinal and ethical treatment in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations" where a direct parallel exists; and three tables in the same form used for the denominations, 31 positions in all: a doctrinal grid (view of Scripture, salvation, baptism, the Lord's Supper, church government, eschatology, spiritual gifts, women's ordination); an ethical and lifestyle grid (abortion, homosexuality, alcohol, divorce and remarriage, contraception, gambling, dancing, premarital sex and cohabitation, war and pacifism, the death penalty); and a grid of other divisive positions (Scripture and Tradition; the Trinity and the person of Christ; predestination and free will; sanctification and holiness; hell and the fate of the lost; Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead; the day, style and liturgy of worship; church and state; race and the church; creation and the age of the earth; tithing and the prosperity gospel; Bible translations and KJV-onlyism; and marriage roles). For a study Bible the last grid records what the notes themselves say or assume on each question -- most visibly at Genesis 1, Romans 9, Ephesians 5, Matthew 25 and Revelation 20 -- and, for the translations row, the volume's own base text and what its preface claims for it. A study Bible is a commentary, not a confession, so the tables describe the position its notes take or, where the notes do not address a topic, the position of the editorial tradition the volume belongs to; the Source column says which, and "general pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes" means exactly that. The abortion, homosexuality, and alcohol rows are the most fully researched, and distinguish the editor's personal published views elsewhere from the content of the volume's own verse notes, which are related but not the same kind of evidence.

**A note on confidence:** for several widely used, doctrinally explicit study Bibles (the Ignatius Catholic Study Bible, the Orthodox Study Bible, the Catholic Study Bible/NABRE, the Complete Jewish Study Bible), the volume's own stated method ties it directly to its tradition's official teaching, so its ethical positions can be stated with real confidence. For others (several entries in the second half of this list, and to some degree the ESV Study Bible and MacArthur Study Bible specifically), this section distinguishes the editor's own well-documented personal position from independently confirmed content of the volume's own verse notes, and says plainly where only the former, not the latter, has been verified. A few entries (the Cultural Backgrounds Study Bible) are academic or historical-critical in orientation and generally do not offer direct ethical application to contemporary questions at all -- this is stated as such rather than papered over.

**A note on alcohol specifically:** as in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations," entries distinguish moderate use (variously permitted or discouraged depending on tradition) from drunkenness/overuse, which is treated as sinful essentially universally.

Several entries in this list (particularly the Life Application Study Bible, the NIV Study Bible, the Open Bible, and the Baker CSB Illustrated Study Bible) are institutional, multi-scholar projects credited to a publisher's editorial team rather than one or two named individuals; this section says so plainly rather than force a "biography" onto a work that does not have one identifiable author.

---

## 1. NIV Study Bible

**Translation used:** New International Version (2011 text).
**Publisher:** Zondervan; study material © 1985, 1995, 2002, 2008, 2011, 2020. First published 1985; the edition read for this entry is the Fully Revised Edition of 2020 (ebook, August 2020), in which the editors "conducted a thorough review and revision of all the notes and study tools."
**Scale:** "Over 21,000 study notes," "over 130 articles," introductions and outlines to every book, NIV text notes, a 100,000-entry cross-reference system, a 35,000-reference concordance, nearly 70 charts and models, 90 maps, a Gospel harmony and a notes index (the volume's own "Introduction to the NIV Study Bible").

### Editorial credit
**Kenneth L. Barker** has been General Editor through every edition since 1985 ("for the past thirty-five years," the 2020 tributes say). The 1985 Associate Editors were Donald W. Burdick, John H. Stek, Walter W. Wessel and Ronald F. Youngblood; Mark L. Strauss joined in 2008, and the 2020 team added Craig L. Blomberg, Jeannine K. Brown and Michael J. Williams. The Contributors page lists some fifty original contributors by book — among them Youngblood (Genesis, Exodus, Jeremiah), Walter C. Kaiser Jr., R. Laird Harris, Ronald B. Allen, J. Robert Vannoy, Raymond Dillard, Edwin Yamauchi, Elmer Smick, Stek (Psalms), Derek Kidner, Gleason Archer, Thomas McComiskey, Leon Morris (John, Thessalonians), W. Harold Mare, Philip E. Hughes, Robert Mounce, Walter Liefeld, Richard Gaffin, Gerald Hawthorne, George W. Knight III and D. Edmond Hiebert — while noting that "the General Editor and the Associate Editors extensively edited the notes on most books" and "alone are responsible for their final form and content."

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical by design. The introduction describes "a transdenominational team of biblical scholars" who "all confess the authority of the Bible as God's infallible word," and states the editorial rule that governs every contested cell below: "Doctrinally, the NIV Study Bible reflects traditional evangelical theology. Where editors were aware of significant differences of opinion on key passages or doctrines, they tried to follow an evenhanded approach by indicating those differences (e.g., see article on Rev 20:2–7 about the 'thousand years')." In practice the notes rule firmly where evangelicals agree (justification, the deity of Christ, sexual ethics, eternal punishment, the Lord's Day) and set out the options in an article where they do not (baptism, the millennium, women's authority, the warning passages of Hebrews). The 2020 revision added articles on war and genocide, slavery, head coverings and the household codes.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | "God's infallible word" (introduction); at 2 Timothy 3:16 "God-breathed" "affirms God's active involvement in the writing of Scripture, an involvement so powerful and pervasive that what is written is the infallible and authoritative word of God." The word "inerrant" does not occur in the volume; "infallible" is its term. |
| Salvation | Justification by faith, forensic and imputed: the article "Justification" says God "declares them to be not guilty… declares them to be righteous. He cancels the guilt of their sin and credits righteousness to them," because "Christ died to pay the penalty for our sin and lived a life of perfect righteousness that can in turn be imputed to believers"; "no human effort can contribute to our salvation" (Ephesians 2:8), and James 2:24 is explained "to use John Calvin's words, that people are justified… by faith alone, but not by a faith that is alone." On perseverance the article "Can Christians Lose Their Salvation?" sets out the face-value reading of Hebrews 6 and the view that "a faith that does not endure was not ever a true faith," leaning to the latter; John 10:28 is "a strong denial that the sheep will ever perish." |
| Baptism | Deliberately undecided: the article "Views on Baptism" presents credobaptists ("only those who profess their faith") and paedobaptists ("children of believing parents as members of God's covenant community") with their proof-texts, and immersion, affusion and aspersion as all "practiced in the church," concluding that such differences "should not be allowed to deflect attention from the union with Christ that such baptism signifies." The notes themselves say "not that baptism effects forgiveness. Rather, forgiveness comes through the rebirth symbolized by baptism" (Acts 2:38) and "it is not the act of washing that saves a person, but faith" (1 Peter 3:21). |
| The Lord's Supper | Memorial with real participation, the mode left open: "the bread is a symbol of Christ's body… the Lord's Supper is a memorial supper" (1 Corinthians 11:24), "is. Represents or signifies" (Luke 22:19), yet "those who eat the bread and drink the wine participate mysteriously but truly in the body and blood of Christ (1Co 10:16)… Exactly how Jesus is present in the bread and wine has understandably been the subject of much debate… What is clear, however, is that the bread and wine are a means of grace" ("'This Is My Body'… 'This Is My Blood'"). John 6:54 is "metaphorical for believing in Jesus." |
| Church government | Two local offices, no polity prescribed: "the only two local church offices mentioned in the NT are those of overseer (also called elder…) and deacon" (1 Timothy 3:8); "the same church leaders could be called 'elders,' 'overseers' or 'pastors'" (Acts 20:28). The article "Jesus Builds His Church" surveys the Protestant, Eastern, Augustinian and Petrine readings of Matthew 16:18 and notes that papal succession "only became official Roman Catholic doctrine after the time of the Reformation"; the keys are "not authority to determine, but to announce" (16:19). |
| Eschatology | The named test case of the volume's evenhandedness: "The Thousand Years" sets out amillennialism, premillennialism (dispensationalist and historic) and postmillennialism without choosing; "The Church and the Tribulation" gives pre-, mid- and post-tribulation views; 1 Thessalonians 4:17 notes "Christians debate whether this meeting of believers and Christ will take place before, after or in the middle of a future period of tribulation"; Matthew 24:30 offers the second coming or AD 70; Daniel 9:24 offers 490 years or a symbolic period; "All Israel Will Be Saved" gives three readings. |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuationist in the notes' own reasoning: at 1 Corinthians 13:10 the view that "the perfect" is the canon so that "tongues ceased when the canon was complete" is called "unlikely… since the close of the canon did not result in perfect knowledge nor a face-to-face encounter with Christ," adding "cf. also 1:7, where believers have all the gifts until Christ returns"; "Paul was not opposed to speaking in tongues if it was practiced properly" (14:5); tongues may be "earthly and heavenly languages, including ecstatic languages of praise and prayer" (12:10). |
| Women's ordination | Three readings, none chosen: "Women and Authority in the Church" gives the universal prohibition (no woman "functioning as an overseer"), the Ephesian-situation reading and the domineering-teaching reading — "Each of these positions is held by a segment of the church and claims Scriptural support" — and closes by urging believers "not to judge others but to follow their own conscience." "Women Remaining Silent" judges 1 Corinthians 14:34–35 situational, allowing that Paul "may have envisioned only male elders (based on one interpretation of 1Ti 2:12)" but "this last point will have to be determined based on other texts." Phoebe is "a female deacon" (Romans 16:1); Junia "a feminine name" (16:7); 1 Timothy 3:1 "refers to a man." |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed as a question; the notes index has an "Abortion" entry pointing to Psalm 139:13–16 and Jeremiah 1:5, where the notes say "God himself put David together in the womb and ordained the span of David's life before he was born," and Exodus 21:23's "serious injury" is "either to mother or to child." The word appears in the notes only at 1 Corinthians 15:8 (lexical). | Volume's notes index "Abortion"; notes at Psalm 139:13–16, Jeremiah 1:5, Exodus 21:22–25, Luke 1:44 |
| Homosexuality | Sinful in act, not in inclination: Romans 1:27 is "one of the clearest evidences that homosexual practice is sinful in God's eyes… But it is the act, rather than the inclination, that is sinful"; the claim that Paul means "only abusive, exploitative or oppressive homosexual behavior" is answered by the lesbian reference of 1:26; the Corinthian terms refer to "the active or male role… and the passive… or female role," such persons being "explicitly excluded from God's kingdom (but see next note)" on washing, sanctification and justification (1 Corinthians 6:9–11); Genesis 19:5 is "homosexual assault; other forms of homosexual practice are condemned elsewhere." | Volume's notes at Romans 1:26–27, 1 Corinthians 6:9–11, Genesis 19:5, Leviticus 18:22, 20:13 |
| Alcohol | Moderation: "Scripture does not forbid the moderate use of alcohol" (1 Timothy 5:23); "those who overindulge become mockers and brawlers" (Proverbs 20:1); Proverbs 23:29–35 is "a vivid description of the physical and psychological effects of drunkenness." | Volume's notes at 1 Timothy 5:23, Proverbs 20:1, 23:29–35, Ephesians 5:18, Titus 2:3 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Two exceptions, with reconciliation the ideal: "Jesus clearly took the side of Shammai (v. 9), but only after first pointing back to God's original ideal for marriage" (Matthew 19:3); Mark and Luke "probably presuppose" the exception (5:32); "1Co 7:15 contains another exception" (Mark 10:11); "the believer is not under obligation to try to continue living with the unbeliever" (1 Corinthians 7:15); "the separated or divorced couple ought to seek reconciliation. Clearly the ideal is the restoration and renewal of the marriage covenant" (7:11). "Does God Hate Divorce?" defends the NIV's "the man who hates and divorces" while insisting it "still strongly condemns divorce as contrary to God's will." | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:32, 19:3–9, Mark 10:11–12, 1 Corinthians 7:10–15, Deuteronomy 24:1–4; article "Does God Hate Divorce?" |
| Contraception | Not addressed: Onan's sin is "his refusal to perform his levirate duty" (Genesis 38:10); "children are God's gift and a sign of his favor" (Psalm 127:3); the words do not occur. | Volume's notes at Genesis 38:8–10, Psalm 127:3–5, 1:28 (no position stated) |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the word does not occur in the notes. | Whole-text search (no entry) |
| Dancing | Not addressed as a question. | Whole-text search (no position stated) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Reserved for marriage: Paul "refutes" the Corinthian slogan "by stating that sexual relations have their proper expression in marriage" (1 Corinthians 7:1–2); sex is "appointed for use in the man-woman relationship in marriage" (6:13); "in the first century Greco-Roman world, moral standards were generally very low, and chastity was regarded as an unreasonable restriction. Paul, however, would not compromise God's clear and demanding standards" (1 Thessalonians 4:3). Cohabitation as such is not discussed. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:13–18, 7:1–2, 1 Thessalonians 4:3, Hebrews 13:4 |
| War/pacifism | Open, with a limit on both sides: the article "The Conquest, War, and Genocide" gives twelve reasons Joshua's war was not genocide, calls it "the only war in the Bible that God ever commanded," and concludes "Christians may debate pacifism vs. just war, but they have no right to claim they know God instituted any war they may fight"; Romans 13:4 — "governments may have to use force to maintain good order… Here we find the biblical principle of using force for the maintenance of good order"; "Violence in Revelation": "nowhere in Revelation are believers called to imitate the violence of God… there is no battle cry resembling 'onward, Christian soldiers.'" | Volume's articles "The Conquest, War, and Genocide" (Joshua 6), "Violence in Revelation"; notes at Romans 13:1–5, Matthew 5:9, 5:39, Luke 22:36 |
| Death penalty | The Mosaic penalty explained, the state's sword affirmed in principle: "in the later theocracy, those guilty of premeditated murder were to be executed… In killing a human being, a murderer demonstrates contempt for God" (Genesis 9:6, cross-referring Romans 13:3–4 and 1 Peter 2:13–14); "Jesus did not condone what the woman had done" (John 8:11). No contemporary verdict is stated. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:5–6, Exodus 21:12–14, Numbers 35:33, Romans 13:4, John 8:7–11 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone binding: "God's commands are found in Scripture and are binding; the traditions of the elders are not in Scripture and therefore not authoritative or binding" (Mark 7:8); apostolic "teachings. Or 'traditions'" were "passed on in fairly fixed form" "until the NT was written" (2 Thessalonians 2:15). | Volume's notes at Mark 7:8–13, 2 Thessalonians 2:15 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Fully orthodox and explicit: "the doctrine of the Trinity means that there is one true God, existing eternally as three distinct persons: Father, Son and Holy Spirit" (Matthew 28:19); "Jesus was God in the fullest sense" and the prologue "begins and ends with a ringing affirmation of his deity" (John 1:1, 18); "in very nature God. Affirming that Jesus is fully God" (Philippians 2:6); "firstborn… does not mean that Christ was God's first created being" (Colossians 1:15); "the two are one in essence or nature, will and purpose, but they are not identical persons" (John 10:30). Genesis 1:26's "us" is read of the heavenly court, not the Trinity. | Volume's notes at Matthew 28:19, John 1:1–18, 10:30, Philippians 2:6–7, Colossians 1:15, Genesis 1:26; article "Jesus as the Word (Logos)" |
| Predestination and free will | Election affirmed, the Reformed and Arminian readings both given: at Romans 8:29 "some maintain" foreknowledge means "choosing us by his grace, before the foundation of the world," while "others believe that Paul here refers to the fact that in eternity past God knew those who by faith would become his people"; 9:11 "based on sovereign freedom, not on the fulfillment of any prior conditions," yet 9:16 "the basis for Israel's rejection was their unbelief"; John 6:44 "the Father draws them. But neither does he compel them against their will"; "to focus exclusively on God's gracious and sovereign activity in salvation is to ignore human free will and responsibility. How these two factors coexist is… perhaps ultimately beyond human comprehension" ("Will 'All People' Be Saved?"). | Volume's notes at Romans 8:29–30, 9:11–22, Ephesians 1:4–11, John 6:37, 44; article "Will 'All People' Be Saved?" |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive, inseparable from justification: "those who have been justified will surely give evidence of that fact by the presence of holiness in their lives" (Romans 6:22); "work out your salvation… not a reference to the attempt to earn one's salvation by works, but a reference to the expression of one's salvation in spiritual growth and development… an ongoing process in which the believer is strenuously involved" (Philippians 2:12); the filling of the Spirit "is not a once-for-all experience" (Ephesians 5:18). No second work of grace. | Volume's notes at Romans 6:22, Philippians 2:12–13, Ephesians 5:18, 1 Corinthians 1:2, 1 Thessalonians 5:23 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment, annihilationism rejected: "the parallelism between these two phrases prevents any weakening of the former" (Matthew 25:46); "Revelation offers no support for the doctrine of the annihilation of the wicked… On the other hand… there is profound symbolism here, since no object can literally burn forever without being consumed. What is absolutely clear, however, is that they have no rest" (14:11); "the annihilation of the whole self cannot be in mind here… This eternal separation is the penalty of sin and the essence of hell" (2 Thessalonians 1:9); universalism "is contradicted by many passages of Scripture" ("Will 'All People' Be Saved?"). | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:46, Revelation 14:10–11, 2 Thessalonians 1:9, Mark 9:43–48, Luke 16:23; article "Will 'All People' Be Saved?" |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Jesus's brothers — the Epiphanian and Hieronymian views are named, but "the most natural conclusion (suggested by Helvidius) is that they were the sons of Joseph and Mary, younger half brothers of Jesus" (Luke 8:19; Matthew 13:55 "sons born to Joseph and Mary after the virgin birth"); "Jesus' virgin birth is an essential truth of the Christian faith" (Matthew 1:25); John 19:27 is John taking "responsibility for her." Purgatory, the Immaculate Conception and the invocation of saints are not mentioned. | Volume's notes at Luke 8:19, Matthew 1:25, 12:46–50, 13:55, Mark 6:3, John 19:26–27 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday as the Lord's Day, the Sabbath no longer binding: "it is remarkable that Paul identifies one of the Ten Commandments (Ex 20:1–17) as no longer binding on Christians… the OT law has been fulfilled in Christ" (Romans 14:5); "the Lord's Day. A technical term for the first day of the week — so named because Jesus rose from the dead on that day" (Revelation 1:10); "every Sunday believers were to bring what they had set aside" (1 Corinthians 16:2); Acts 20:7's "first day" is "Sunday," and breaking bread there "indicates the Lord's Supper… observed regularly." Style is not discussed. | Volume's notes at Romans 14:5, Colossians 2:16–17, Acts 20:7, 1 Corinthians 16:2, Revelation 1:10, Hebrews 4:9 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission bounded by allegiance to Christ: "Paul is encouraging Christians to be good citizens as long as it doesn't conflict with their allegiance to Christ… Even the possibility of a persecuting state did not shake Paul's conviction that civil government is ordained by God" (Romans 13:1); "obedience to the emperor must never be in violation of the law of God" (1 Peter 2:13); "this democratizing of honor (from emperor to people in the lowest of positions) would have been quite striking" (2:17). No political program. | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–5, 1 Peter 2:13–17, Matthew 22:21, Acts 5:29 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity affirmed, racist proof-texting repudiated: the article "Slavery and the Bible" calls the use of Noah's curse "to justify the enslavement of persons of color… an absolutely illegitimate application of the biblical text"; "all people are of one family" (Acts 17:26); "God does not favor individuals because of… their nationality, their ethnicity" (10:34); "unity in Christ transcends ethnic, social and gender distinctions" (Galatians 3:28); Moses' Cushite wife is probably Zipporah ("Moses' Cushite Wife"). | Volume's article "Slavery and the Bible" (Genesis 9:25); notes at Acts 10:28–34, 17:26, Ephesians 2:14–15, Galatians 3:28; article "Moses' Cushite Wife" |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Creation ex nihilo affirmed, chronology left open — the old cell's three named readings are not what the volume lists: "the Bible is clear from its very first verse that God created all things out of nothing (ex nihilo)… Attempts at gaining further specificity regarding timing and methodology, however, encounter difficulties"; Genesis 1 is "a highly stylistic literary work, perhaps even to be considered poetry," which "calls into question the legitimacy of efforts to read the biblical creation account as though it were intended to recount the methodology of creation with scientific precision"; day-age (allowing "various permutations of the modern theory of evolution") and gap readings are described, "but ultimately all such attempts are unnecessary… the biblical creation account is concerned not to describe the chronology or methodology of God's activity, but rather to relate the fact that God created" ("The Biblical Account of Creation"). "Some say that the creation days were 24-hour days, others that they were indefinite periods" (1:5); the flood may be "worldwide" or "regional" ("The Flood"); Genesis 5's numbers may be "literal or… conventional" ("Lifespan Before the Flood"). | Volume's articles "The Biblical Account of Creation," "Creation in the Bible and the Ancient Near East," "The Flood," "Lifespan Before the Flood"; notes at Genesis 1:1–5, 2:4–7 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithe as Israel's law ("Israel actually had three tithes… The total adds up to 23 1/3 percent," Leviticus 27:30; Jesus affirms it "while the law is still in force," Matthew 23:23), New Testament giving as proportionate and generous ("an amount proportionate to their income," 1 Corinthians 16:2). Prosperity teaching named and rejected: "the so-called prosperity gospel today often appeals to this Deuteronomistic theology in promising health and wealth to faithful Christians without recognizing that it was a unique arrangement with ancient Israel" ("The Deuteronomistic History"); Psalm 1:1's blessedness is "not first of all to health and wealth"; Proverbs gives "rewards, not promises." | Volume's articles "The Deuteronomistic History," "Does the Psalmist Promise Prosperity?", "Rewards, Not Promises"; notes at Leviticus 27:30, Deuteronomy 14:22–29, Matthew 23:23, 1 Corinthians 16:2, 1 Timothy 6:5–10 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the NIV, whose preface the volume prints; the introduction calls the NIV "unsurpassed in accuracy, clarity and literary grace"; text notes record variants (Mark 16:9–20, John 7:53–8:11, the "missing verses" at Matthew 17:21 and 18:11 are cited as examples). KJV-onlyism has no standing. | Volume's front matter (NIV Preface; Introduction, "Text Notes") |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Deliberately bilateral: Ephesians 5:22 is "an aspect of the mutual submission taught in v. 21," and "head" "has been understood to indicate either authority or source (see article)"; 5:25 "shows that this is not a one-sided submission but a reciprocal relationship"; the 1 Corinthians 11:3 note gives the source, honour and authority readings in turn; "Household Expectations in the First Century" says the New Testament codes "imply that Christian households were to be less autocratic and patriarchal than their pagan counterparts," and 1 Peter 3:7 "women experience the saving grace of God on equal terms with men." "Suitable Helper" says *'ezer* "does not… communicate the idea of inferiority." | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:21–33, 1 Corinthians 11:3–10, 1 Peter 3:1–7, Genesis 2:18–24, 3:16; articles "Household Expectations in the First Century," "Suitable Helper," "God's Image and Glory in Men and Women" |

## 2. NLT Life Application Study Bible

**Translation used:** New Living Translation (the Life Application notes are also published with the NIV, KJV, NKJV and other texts)
**Publisher:** Tyndale House Publishers; Third Edition, 2019 (notes © 1988–2019; New Testament notes first © 1986). The copyright page acknowledges "the role of Youth for Christ/USA in preparing the Life Application notes and Bible helps," and the third-edition revisions were produced by The Livingstone Corporation.
**Scale:** Application and explanatory notes on nearly every page, book introductions (timeline, vital statistics, overview, blueprint, megathemes, map), nearly 150 Personality Profiles, hundreds of charts, a Harmony of the Gospels, a dictionary/concordance and "A Christian Worker's Resource"; the front matter says "over 75 percent of this Bible's features are application oriented."

### General editor / project leadership
Ronald A. Beers is credited as General Editor, with a Senior Editorial Team of Bruce B. Barton, Beers, James C. Galvin, LaVonne Neff, Linda Chaffee Taylor and David R. Veerman (Veerman wrote the book introductions, Galvin the outlines and Harmony, Barton the Megathemes, Neil S. Wilson the Personality Profiles). The work was reviewed by a panel of theological reviewers under General Theological Reviewer Kenneth S. Kantzer of Trinity Evangelical Divinity School, including Barry Beitzel, Edwin A. Blum, Geoffrey W. Bromiley, L. Russ Bush, Walter A. Elwell, Gerald F. Hawthorne, Howard G. Hendricks and Grant R. Osborne. No single scholar's name is attached to the notes in the manner of the ESV Study Bible or MacArthur Study Bible.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and deliberately interdenominational: the front matter describes the volume as "developed by an interdenominational team of pastors, scholars, family counselors, and members of a national organization dedicated to promoting God's Word," and lists among the questions a buyer should ask of any study Bible, "Do the notes avoid denominational bias?" The reviewers were drawn from Trinity Evangelical Divinity School, Dallas Theological Seminary, Wheaton, Fuller, Bethel and Southwestern Baptist. On the doctrines that divide evangelicals (baptism, the Lord's Supper, the millennium, election, women's teaching) the notes lay out the competing views and decline to rule; on the ethical questions of the day (abortion, homosexuality, same-sex marriage, premarital sex) they take firm conservative-evangelical positions and apply them directly to the reader.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Fully inspired and authoritative: "The Bible, however, is given by God; the words are God's words… they wrote exactly what God wanted them to write. We can completely trust Scripture because God was in control of its writing. The words of the Bible are entirely authoritative for your faith and life" (2 Timothy 3:16 note); 2 Peter 1:20–21 note says God "cooperated with the writers in such a way as to ensure that the message he intended was faithfully communicated in the very words they wrote." The word "inerrancy" is not used. |
| Salvation | Justification by grace through faith alone: "God declares us not guilty when we trust Jesus Christ to take away our sins" (Romans 3:21–29); "We are saved only by trusting in what God has done for us" (3:27–28); "Why does God save us by faith alone?" (3:28); "We become Christians through God's unmerited grace, not as the result of any effort, ability, intelligent choice, or act of service on our part" (Ephesians 2:8–10). Substitutionary atonement is named at 1 Peter 2:22–25. Old Testament believers "looked forward in faith to Jesus' coming and were saved" (Romans 3:25–26). |
| Baptism | A symbol and sign of faith, not a saving act: at Acts 2:38–39 "Baptism identifies us with Jesus and with the community of believers. It is a condition of discipleship and a sign of faith"; at Matthew 28:19 baptism "symbolizes submission to Jesus as Lord"; at Romans 6:1–4 "immersion was the usual form of baptism" in Paul's day. Infant baptism is neither taught nor discussed; the Acts 16:31–34 note says of the jailer's household, "The jailer's faith is not what saved them; they all needed to come to Jesus in faith." |
| The Lord's Supper | The three views are set out without ruling: at 1 Corinthians 11:24–25 "(1) Some believe that the bread and wine actually become Christ's physical body and blood. (2) Others believe that the bread and wine remain unchanged but that Christ is spiritually and uniquely present… (3) Still others believe that the bread and wine symbolize Christ's body and blood. Christians generally agree, however… that Christ's presence, however we understand it, strengthens us spiritually." The notes call it "a memorial feast" (11:25) and "a sacrament given by Jesus to help strengthen our faith" (11:30); the John 6:56 note says Jesus "was not talking about literal blood, of course." |
| Church government | No polity endorsed; "elder and pastor are synonymous, not names for separate offices," and the term "can refer to a pastor, elder, bishop, or presiding overseer" (1 Timothy 3:1); leaders "should be chosen by the church because of their respect for the truth." Deacons "were leaders in the church, and their qualifications resemble those of elders" (3:8–13). |
| Eschatology | Deliberately uncommitted: the Revelation 20:2–4 note defines postmillennialism, premillennialism and amillennialism and concludes, "These different views about the Millennium need not cause division and controversy in the church… we should not let this issue divide us"; 20:5–6 gives the two views of the first resurrection. "It is good that we don't know exactly when Jesus will return" (Matthew 24:36); the timing of the rapture "is not as important as knowing why Paul wrote these words" (1 Thessalonians 4:15–18). The second death is "everlasting separation from God" (20:6). |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuationist in practice: tongues "is a legitimate gift of the Holy Spirit" and "a desirable gift even though it isn't a requirement of faith"; "no one should put down Christians who speak in tongues, and those who speak in tongues should not disparage those who do not" (1 Corinthians 14:2–5); the gift is "most likely" an ecstatic or heavenly language rather than earthly languages (12:10); and tongues "can be used in public worship if" interpreted (14:13–20). Gifts end only "in eternity" (13:10–12). Spirit baptism is received "when we receive Jesus Christ" (Acts 1:5), not as a second experience. |
| Women's ordination | Leans toward permitting women to teach, while reporting both views: at 1 Timothy 2:12 "Paul did not forbid women from ever teaching… Paul was very likely prohibiting the Ephesian women, not all women, from teaching"; at 2:13–14 the two interpretations (local problem versus "God's design for his created order") are given side by side; at 1 Corinthians 14:34–35 "women have much to contribute and should be encouraged to participate in worship services"; Phoebe "was known as a deacon, or servant" (Romans 16:1–2); of Deborah, "God can choose anyone to lead his people, young or old, man or woman" (Judges 4:4–24). The 1 Timothy 3:11 "women" are more probably deacons' wives. The word "ordination" is not used of the question. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Opposed, explicitly: "David would be shocked at how easily the unborn are destroyed today. Millions of abortions occur every year… Pray and work for the protection of these lives" (Psalm 139:13–16); "Millions of unborn children have also been sacrificed on the altar of convenience through abortion. God sees these evil practices and will exact his justice" (Ezekiel 20:31–32). No note on Exodus 21:22. | Volume's notes at Psalm 139:13–16 and Ezekiel 20:31–32 |
| Homosexuality | Homosexual practice is sin; same-sex attraction is distinguished from behaviour: "Homosexual activity is strictly forbidden in Scripture (Leviticus 18:22) because it goes against God's design for marriage… But society does not set the standard for God's law" (Romans 1:26–27); "We must be careful, however, to disapprove of the practice but not condemn individuals who experience same-sex attraction… The church must love those who are gay without compromising the biblical stance against homosexual sexual behavior" (1 Corinthians 6:9); on same-sex marriage, "At Creation he approved one kind of marriage bond, man to woman" (Matthew 19:4–6). | Volume's notes at Romans 1:26–27, 1 Corinthians 6:9–11, Matthew 19:4–6, Leviticus 18:6–27 |
| Alcohol | Moderation, not abstinence: the Proverbs 23:29–30 note says "These verses are not trying to make a blanket statement about the evils of alcohol but rather the abuse of alcohol. Like any gift from God (food, sex, work, money), using it as he intended brings happiness and blessing, but abusing it brings great sorrow"; "Drinking too much leads to alcoholism" (1 Corinthians 6:12–13); 1 Timothy 5:23 "is not an invitation to overindulgence in alcohol"; the Romans 14 notes warn against adding "rules and regulations" to Scripture on disputable matters. | Volume's notes at Proverbs 23:29–35, 1 Corinthians 6:12–13, 1 Timothy 5:23, Romans 14:10–23 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Marriage is "indissoluble" by design; divorce permitted only for persistent unfaithfulness or desertion by an unbeliever, and never as a first resort: "Jesus said that divorce is not permissible except for in cases of unfaithfulness… The word translated 'unfaithful' implies a sexually immoral lifestyle, not a confessed and repented act of adultery" (Matthew 5:32); "God designed marriage to be indissoluble" (19:7–8); 1 Corinthians 7:15 "is misused by some as a loophole to get out of marriage," but "if… an unbelieving spouse insisted on leaving, Paul said to let him or her go." Remarriage is not discussed. | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:31–32, 19:3–12, Mark 10:5–9, 1 Corinthians 7:12–15, Malachi 2:14–16 |
| Contraception | Not addressed: the Genesis 38:8–10 note says "God killed Onan because he refused to fulfill his obligation to his brother and to Tamar," with no application to contraception; no note anywhere discusses birth control. | Volume's note at Genesis 38:8–10; full-text search of the notes |
| Gambling | Not treated as a doctrine; gambling appears only as a vice to be given up — "If you have a secret 'weight' such as pornography, gambling, painkillers, or alcohol, admit your need and get help today" (Hebrews 12:1) — and in the observation that someone "may be a great gambler or a skillful liar" (Psalm 52:1); the soldiers "gambled for Jesus' clothes" (Luke 23:34). | Volume's notes at Hebrews 12:1, Psalm 52:1, Luke 23:34, Joshua 18:2–10 |
| Dancing | Approved as worship: "David's exuberance as he worshiped God with dancing and music is approved of in Scripture" (1 Chronicles 13:8); praise "by dancing, singing, and playing musical instruments. God enjoys his people" (Psalm 149:3–5); Michal's contempt for David's dancing is the fault (2 Samuel 6:16–23). Social dancing is not discussed. | Volume's notes at 1 Chronicles 13:8, Psalm 149:3–5, 2 Samuel 6:14–23 |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sex is reserved for marriage: "Paul clearly states that premarital sex, adultery, and anything that qualifies as lust is a rejection of God himself" (1 Thessalonians 4:8); sexual union "is reserved for marriage" (Genesis 2:24); "Many unmarried people want to experience all the delights and comforts of marriage—like living together and having sex—before they are married… Remain pure and wait patiently" (Song of Songs 8:4); "If we indulge in sex before marriage… we are trying to satisfy God-given desires in wrong ways" (Matthew 4:3–4); the Hebrews 13:4 note also urges readers to resist "political pressure to recognize and legalize marriages that are not between one man and one woman." | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3–8, Genesis 2:24, Song of Songs 8:4, 1 Corinthians 6:13–20, Hebrews 13:4 |
| War/pacifism | Both positions reported, neither endorsed: the Romans 13:1–14 note gives three Christian views of the state, the first holding that Christians "should not work for the government, vote in elections, or serve in the military," and concludes "None of these views advocate rebelling against or refusing to obey the government's laws." The Matthew 5:38–44 notes are about personal non-retaliation ("Instead of demanding rights, give them up freely!"), and the John 2:15–16 note says "the authority to use weapons and restrain people is granted to police officers, but not to the general public." | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–14, Matthew 5:38–44, John 2:15–16 |
| Death penalty | Not ruled on: at Genesis 9:5 "We cannot harm or kill another human being without answering to God… A penalty must be paid. Justice will be served," and 9:5–6 explains "why murder is so wrong" without commending execution; at John 8:7 "Jesus upheld the legal penalty for adultery, stoning" while highlighting "the importance of mercy and forgiveness"; Matthew 5:38 reads the lex talionis as a law "given to judges" to "make the punishment fit the crime." | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:5–6, John 8:3–11, Matthew 5:38 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone is the standard; tradition is useful but subordinate: "Jesus wasn't against all tradition, but he was against those who made their traditions as important, if not more important, than God's Word… Change your traditions if they become more important to you than God's Word" (Mark 7:8–9); "Traditions should help us understand God's Word better, not have equal status with it" (Matthew 15:1–2); "It is arrogant to assume that your interpretation of God's Word is as important as God's Word itself" (Mark 7:8–9); the Bible "remains our only source of knowledge about how we can be saved" (2 Timothy 3:16–17). | Volume's notes at Mark 7:1–13, Matthew 15:1–9, 2 Timothy 3:16–17, 2 Thessalonians 2:15 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Fully orthodox and insisted upon: Matthew 28:19 "affirm[s] the reality of the Trinity… the concept comes directly from Jesus himself"; Jesus "was not part human and part God; he was completely human and completely divine" (John 1:14); "Because Christ created all and rules all creation… we must believe in the deity of Jesus Christ (that Jesus is God). Without this core belief, our Christian faith becomes hollow" (Colossians 1:15–16); "Christ is equal to God because he is God" (Philippians 2:5–11); "But Jesus is God" (John 8:58). | Volume's notes at Matthew 28:19, John 1:1–18, 8:58, Colossians 1:15–16, Philippians 2:5–11 |
| Predestination and free will | Both views reported without a verdict, with a lean toward human responsibility: at Romans 8:29–30 "Some believe these verses mean that before the beginning of the world, God chose certain people to receive his gift of salvation… Others believe that God knew in advance who would respond to him"; at 9:17–18 hardening "most likely means that God allows those who willfully rebel against him to continue to do so"; at Ephesians 1:4 "salvation depends totally on God… we cannot take credit for our salvation or take pride in making the right decision." | Volume's notes at Romans 8:29–30, 9:11–21, Ephesians 1:4–11 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive, lifelong, Spirit-wrought: holiness "is a lifelong process (called sanctification) as the Holy Spirit works in you, conforming you to the image of Jesus Christ" (1 Thessalonians 4:3); Romans 6–8 "deals with sanctification—the change God makes in our lives as we grow to be more like him"; the Spirit "begins a lifelong process of change to make us more like Jesus" (Acts 1:5). No second work of grace is taught. | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3, Romans 6:1–8:39, Acts 1:5 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment: Gehenna "is the final and eternal state of the wicked after the resurrection and the Last Judgment. Jesus' warnings against unbelief are meant to save us from agonizing, eternal punishment" (Matthew 25:46, with a survey of Sheol, Hades and Gehenna); "If by faith we have not identified with Christ… we will have no hope, no second chance, no other appeal" (Revelation 20:14); the second death is "everlasting separation from God" (20:6). Annihilationism is not mentioned. | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:46, Revelation 20:6, 20:12–15 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary is honored as "the only human who was with Jesus from birth to death" whose "submission was part of God's plan to bring about our salvation" (Luke 1:30–31; profile), with no veneration, intercession or perpetual virginity — Joseph "honored her virginity until after the baby was born," and the Joseph profile lists her children as "Jesus, James, Joseph, Judas, Simon, and daughters." Nothing on invoking saints, purgatory or prayer for the dead. The 1 Peter 3:18–20 note reports three views of Christ's preaching "to the spirits in prison." | Volume's notes at Luke 1:26–48, Matthew 12:46–50; profiles of Mary and Joseph |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sabbath-keeping not binding, weekly rest and worship commended: Old Testament "holidays, and festivals pointed toward Christ… When Christ came, he dispelled the shadows" (Colossians 2:17), and believers should "never criticize fellow Christians whose traditions and ceremonies differ from ours" (2:16–17); "Observing a regular time of rest and worship… demonstrates how important God is to us" (Exodus 20:8–11); on style, "True Christianity is neither barren intellectualism nor thoughtless emotionalism" (1 Corinthians 14:15), order "should not… rule out God's creativity, joy, and unpredictability" (14:40), and expressive worship is defended at 2 Samuel 6:20–22. | Volume's notes at Colossians 2:16–17, Exodus 20:8–11, 1 Corinthians 14:15–40, 2 Samuel 6:20–22, Hebrews 10:25 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Three views set out without endorsement at Romans 13:1–14 (separation, two spheres, Christian influence on the state), with the common ground that Christians obey the law "unless those laws clearly require the believer to violate the moral standards revealed by God"; "We should never allow the government to force us to disobey God" (13:1); "All are commanded to cooperate with those in authority as far as conscience will allow" (1 Peter 2:13–17). Readers are urged to "pray and work" against abortion and to resist "political pressure" on marriage. | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–7, 1 Peter 2:13–17, Acts 5:29 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Racial prejudice condemned: Genesis 9:25 "has been wrongfully used to support racial prejudice and even slavery. Noah's curse, however, wasn't directed toward any particular race"; "Jesus created a new, multiethnic family of faith… Make a point to seek out and appreciate people who are not just like you" (Galatians 3:28); barriers of "race, theological perspective" are among those Christ has removed (Ephesians 2:14–22); "Whole nations can be caught up in immorality (racial hatred, slavery, prison cruelty)" (Exodus 1:17–21). | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:25, Galatians 3:28, Ephesians 2:11–22, Acts 10:34–35, Exodus 1:17–21 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Deliberately open on the length of the days, firm on divine creation: "Some believe God created it in seven, 24-hour days. Others believe it happened over millions of years under God's direction… The Bible doesn't tell us how it all happened" (Genesis 1:1–31); "The Bible does not say how long these days were… The important thing is not how long it took God to create the world, whether a few days or a few billion years, but that he created it" (1:3–2:7). "To say that the universe 'just happened' or 'evolved' requires more faith than to believe that God is behind these amazing statistics" (1:1–31). | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1–2:7 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithing commended as a baseline, prosperity teaching rejected: "are you willing to return at least 10 percent for helping to advance God's Kingdom?" (Malachi 3:8–12); "the blessings God promises are not always material and may not be experienced completely here on earth" (3:8–12); "Wealth is not a sign of faith or of partiality on God's part. Faith does not guarantee that we'll be wealthy" (Mark 10:26); "Tithing is important, but giving a tithe does not exempt us from fulfilling God's other directives" (Matthew 23:23–24); a "Giving a Tenth" chart accompanies Malachi 3. | Volume's notes at Malachi 3:8–12, 2 Corinthians 9:6–13, Matthew 23:23–24, Mark 10:26; "Giving a Tenth" chart |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the New Living Translation, whose introduction explains the translators' aim "to render the message of the original texts of Scripture into clear, contemporary English" keeping "the concerns of both formal-equivalence and dynamic-equivalence in mind"; the Life Application notes themselves are published across several translations, and the notes repeatedly commend reading "the whole Bible" in a plan (2 Timothy 3:16–17). KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's "Introduction to the New Living Translation"; copyright page |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Soft complementarian framed by mutual submission: "In a marriage, both husband and wife should mutually submit to each other. For the wife, this means willingly following her husband's leadership in Christ. For the husband, it means putting aside his own interests in order to care for and serve his wife" (Ephesians 5:21–22); "According to the Bible, the man is the responsible spiritual head of the family, and his wife should acknowledge his leadership. But real spiritual leadership involves loving service" (5:22–25); "while wives have to submit, husbands have to die" (5:22–25); "Paul stressed complete equality in sexual relationships" (1 Corinthians 7:4); "A man who honors his wife as his equal will protect, respect, help, and stay with her" (1 Peter 3:7). | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:21–33, 1 Corinthians 7:3–4, 11:3, 1 Peter 3:1–7, Genesis 2:18–24 |
