## 3. ESV Study Bible

**Translation used:** English Standard Version (ESV)
**Publisher:** Crossway, first published 2008 (ECPA Christian Book of the Year, 2009)
**Scale:** 93 evangelical scholars and teachers from 9 countries; over 20,000 study notes, 80,000 cross-references, 200+ charts, 240 full-color maps.

### General editor
**Wayne A. Grudem** (b. 1948) — B.A., Harvard University; M.Div., Westminster Theological Seminary (also awarded a D.D.); Ph.D., University of Cambridge (dissertation: *The Gift of Prophecy in 1 Corinthians*, under C.F.D. Moule). Distinguished Research Professor of Theology and Biblical Studies at Phoenix Seminary (previously 20 years at Trinity Evangelical Divinity School). A former president of the Evangelical Theological Society and a member of the ESV's own Translation Oversight Committee. Best known for his widely used *Systematic Theology* (1994) and as co-founder of the Council on Biblical Manhood and Womanhood, the leading organization advocating complementarian (as opposed to egalitarian) gender roles in the home and church. Ordained in the Baptist General Conference.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and Reformed-leaning, though the volume draws contributors from multiple evangelical traditions rather than representing one denomination exclusively. Closest in orientation to the Southern Baptist Convention and other confessionally evangelical bodies profiled in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations," though not formally affiliated with any single one.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed explicitly, in the terms of the Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy (1978). |
| Salvation | Reformed/Calvinist: unconditional election, effectual grace, and perseverance of the saints, stated explicitly in the notes on Romans 9, Ephesians 1, and John 6. |
| Baptism | Covenantal infant baptism (paedobaptism) is the editors' confessional position, though the notes acknowledge the Baptist reading. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Amillennial or historic premillennial; the notes read Revelation 20 within covenant theology and reject dispensational distinctions between Israel and the Church. |
| Spiritual gifts | Mildly cautious; the notes do not affirm the continuation of the sign gifts as normative. |
| Women's ordination | Complementarian; the notes read 1 Timothy 2:12 as reserving the office of pastor/elder to men. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Grudem has written extensively and specifically against abortion in his own separate works (*Christian Ethics*, 2018; *What the Bible Says about Abortion, Euthanasia, and End-of-Life Medical Decisions*), grounding opposition in the doctrine that human life bears God's image from conception. The ESV Study Bible's own notes are understood to reflect this same view, consistent with its broadly complementarian-evangelical editorial team, though the specific wording of individual verse notes was not checked. | Editor's own published works (Grudem, *Christian Ethics*); notes not independently checked |
| Homosexuality | Grudem's own published position, argued at length in his other works, holds same-sex sexual conduct to be sinful (citing Leviticus 18:22, Romans 1:26–27, 1 Corinthians 6:9–11) while distinguishing this from compassionate pastoral care toward those experiencing same-sex attraction. The study Bible's editorial team and stated theological orientation are consistent with this position, though specific verse-note wording was not checked. | Editor's own published works (Grudem, *Christian Ethics*); notes not independently checked |
| Alcohol | Moderate use is not treated as sinful, in contrast to the Southern Baptist Convention's confessional abstinence position; drunkenness, distinct from use, is treated as sinful, consistent with the wider evangelical and Reformed tradition. | Editor's own published works (Grudem, *Christian Ethics*); notes not independently checked |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone is the final authority; the volume's doctrinal articles treat the ecumenical creeds and Reformation confessions as faithful summaries subordinate to Scripture. | Volume's articles ("Biblical Doctrine: An Overview"; "The Bible and Christian Theology") |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and Chalcedonian, set out at length in the doctrinal overview article and the notes on John 1 and Philippians 2. | Volume's articles and notes |
| Predestination and free will | Reformed-leaning: the notes on Romans 9 and Ephesians 1 read election as unconditional while acknowledging the Arminian alternative; the general editor's own systematic theology is Calvinist. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (Grudem, *Systematic Theology*) |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification that is never complete in this life, as the doctrinal overview states; no second-blessing teaching. | Volume's article ("Biblical Doctrine: An Overview") |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment, defended explicitly against annihilationism in the doctrinal overview and the notes on Matthew 25:46 and Revelation 20:10–15. | Volume's articles and notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary is honored, not venerated; the notes read Matthew 12:46 as literal siblings and reject invocation of saints and prayers for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday assumed as the Lord's Day; the notes on Colossians 2:16 treat the Sabbath as fulfilled in Christ, and the article on worship favors Word-centered, reverent worship without a fixed liturgy. | Volume's notes and article on worship |
| Church and state / political engagement | An article on "The Bible and Government" teaches limited government, the legitimacy of civil authority (Romans 13) and Christian participation in public life; the general editor's later *Politics According to the Bible* (2010) takes conservative positions on specific issues. | Volume's article; editor's own published works (Grudem) |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The notes on Genesis 10–11, Acts 17:26 and Galatians 3:28 affirm the unity of humanity and the church across ethnic lines; no social program is advocated. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Creation and the age of the earth | The Genesis notes (C. John Collins) read the "days" as God's workdays analogically, treat the age of the earth as a question Scripture does not settle, and the article "The Bible and Science" argues that old-earth views are compatible with inerrancy while rejecting naturalistic evolution. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1 and article ("The Bible and Science") |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | The notes treat the tithe as Mosaic law and New Testament giving as generous, cheerful and proportionate; the article on wealth and poverty rejects prosperity teaching. | Volume's notes and article on wealth and poverty |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the ESV, an "essentially literal" revision of the RSV; the preface and the article on Bible translation defend formal-equivalence translation from the critical texts. KJV-onlyism is rejected by implication. | Volume's preface and article ("The Bible and Translation") |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Complementarian: the notes on Ephesians 5, 1 Corinthians 11 and 1 Timothy 2 teach male headship in home and church; the general editor co-founded the Council on Biblical Manhood and Womanhood. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (Grudem) |

---

## 4. MacArthur Study Bible (LSB edition)

**Translation used:** Legacy Standard Bible (LSB)
**Publisher:** Thomas Nelson, in association with The Master's Seminary and The Lockman Foundation; second edition released 2024. Now the official current edition of the MacArthur Study Bible, since the LSB itself was produced in association with The Master's Seminary, part of the John MacArthur Trust. The MacArthur Study Bible as a whole (across all its translation editions -- NKJV, ESV, NASB, NIV, and LSB) has reached more than 4 million readers since 1997, a figure this entry's position in the list reflects even though it applies to the full family of editions rather than the LSB edition specifically.
**Scale:** Nearly 25,000 verse-by-verse study notes, 190 in-text maps, charts, and diagrams; the same underlying study-note content found in the NKJV and NASB MacArthur Study Bible editions, adapted to the LSB's own text.

### General editor
**John F. MacArthur Jr.** (1939–2025) — graduated Talbot Theological Seminary, 1964. Pastor-teacher of Grace Community Church in Sun Valley, California, from 1969 until his death in 2025 (56 years); chancellor of The Master's University and Seminary; longtime featured teacher on the internationally syndicated radio and media ministry *Grace to You*. Known worldwide for verse-by-verse expository preaching and for a substantial body of published work (nearly 400 books and study guides over his ministry). MacArthur personally wrote more than half of the original 20,000 study notes in longhand and reworked many of the rest; MacArthur died on July 14, 2025, at age 86, following health complications dating to 2023.

### Denominational leaning
Independent Baptist/non-denominational, conservative evangelical, explicitly Reformed (Calvinist) in soteriology, dispensationalist in eschatology, and cessationist regarding the miraculous spiritual gifts — a distinctive combination, since dispensationalism and cessationism are each more commonly paired with non-Reformed evangelical traditions than with Calvinism. Closest among the Denominations entries in this book to the doctrinal orientation of conservative, confessional Southern Baptist and independent Reformed Baptist churches, though Grace Community Church itself was independent rather than formally affiliated with the SBC. The specific pairing with the LSB reflects the translation's own direct institutional connection to MacArthur's own ministry (The Master's Seminary and University), making this edition, rather than the older NKJV edition, the one MacArthur's own institutions currently promote as standard.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed explicitly, in the terms of the Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy (1978). |
| Salvation | Reformed/Calvinist: unconditional election, effectual grace, and perseverance of the saints, stated explicitly in the notes on Romans 9, Ephesians 1, and John 6. |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism by immersion, in the Baptist tradition of the editor. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Dispensational premillennialism: a pretribulational rapture, a literal seven-year tribulation, and a literal thousand-year reign, with Israel and the Church distinguished throughout. |
| Spiritual gifts | Cessationist: the notes hold that the sign gifts (tongues, prophecy, healing) ceased with the apostolic age. |
| Women's ordination | Complementarian; the notes read 1 Timothy 2:12 as reserving the office of pastor/elder to men. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | MacArthur was a prominent, public, and unambiguous voice against abortion throughout his ministry, consistent with his broader conservative evangelical framework; his notes and associated preaching ministry treat the sanctity of unborn human life as a settled biblical position rather than a debatable one. | Editor's own published works (MacArthur); notes not independently checked |
| Homosexuality | MacArthur was likewise a public and outspoken voice holding same-sex sexual conduct to be sinful, a position argued at length in his preaching ministry (available via Grace to You) and consistent with his broader complementarian, conservative evangelical framework. | Editor's own published works (MacArthur); notes not independently checked |
| Alcohol | MacArthur's own ministry and broader independent-Baptist-adjacent tradition generally treats moderate use as a matter of Christian liberty rather than sin, distinct from the Southern Baptist Convention's formal confessional abstinence position; drunkenness itself is treated as sin, a distinction MacArthur's own preaching ministry addresses directly. | Editor's own published works (MacArthur); notes not independently checked |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone; the notes and the introductory articles treat creeds and tradition as having no authority over the text. | Volume's notes and introductory material |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and Chalcedonian; the notes defend Christ's full deity at John 1, Colossians 1 and Hebrews 1. | Volume's notes |
| Predestination and free will | Reformed: unconditional election and the perseverance of the saints taught explicitly in the notes on Romans 8–9 and Ephesians 1, with free will described as bound by sin. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (MacArthur) |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification as the necessary fruit of saving faith ("lordship salvation"); the notes deny any second work of grace and reject perfectionism. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (MacArthur, *The Gospel According to Jesus*) |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment, taught unambiguously in the notes on Matthew 25:46, Luke 16 and Revelation 20; annihilationism rejected. | Volume's notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: no veneration of Mary or the saints and no prayer for the dead; the notes on Luke 1 and Matthew 12:46 reject perpetual virginity and Marian intercession. | Volume's notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday as the Lord's Day; the notes on Colossians 2:16 treat the Sabbath as abrogated. Worship is to be Word-centered and expository; the notes on 1 Corinthians 14 commend order and reject charismatic practice. | Volume's notes |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission to government (Romans 13) and a deliberately non-political pulpit; the editor's own writings argue the church's task is the gospel rather than political activism. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (MacArthur, *Why Government Can't Save You*) |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The notes affirm the unity of all people in Christ (Galatians 3:28; Ephesians 2); the editor was the lead signer of the 2018 *Statement on Social Justice and the Gospel*, which rejects social-justice framing of racial questions. | Volume's notes; editor's own public statements (2018) |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Literal six-day, young-earth creation and a global Flood, stated in the Genesis 1–11 notes and defended against old-earth readings. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1–11; editor's own published works (MacArthur, *The Battle for the Beginning*) |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithing is not a New Testament requirement; giving is to be free, proportionate and generous (2 Corinthians 9). The prosperity gospel is condemned in the notes on the relevant passages. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (MacArthur) |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the Legacy Standard Bible, a formal-equivalence revision of the NASB from the critical texts; earlier editions used the NKJV and NASB. The editor rejects KJV-onlyism. | Volume's preface; editor's own published statements |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Firmly complementarian: male headship in the home (Ephesians 5), male-only elders and pastors (1 Timothy 2–3), and submission of wives taught in the notes. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (MacArthur) |

---

## 5. Scofield Reference Bible / Scofield Study Bible

**Translation used:** Originally King James Version (1909; revised 1917); a "New Scofield" revision appeared in 1967 and 1986, and a further revision ("Scofield III") in 2005.
**Publisher:** Oxford University Press.

### General editor
**Cyrus Ingerson Scofield** (1843–1921), a pastor and Bible teacher whose extensive interpretive notes — printed directly alongside the biblical text rather than segregated as a separate commentary — are credited with popularizing dispensationalist theology (including the framework of successive biblical "dispensations" and the doctrine of a pretribulational rapture) more broadly and durably within American evangelicalism than any other single work, arguably making it the single most historically influential study Bible ever published in English even though its raw sales figures are less precisely documented than several more recent entries in this list.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical, foundational to 20th-century American dispensationalism specifically; historically influential on institutions including Dallas Theological Seminary. Later revisions (1967, 1986, 2005) softened or removed some of Scofield's own most historically controversial original notes while retaining the overall dispensationalist framework.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Divine inspiration affirmed: the Introduction calls the Bible's doctrinal harmony "the unanswerable proof of the divine inspiration of the Bible" and insists "the Bible is one book," "a progressive unfolding of truth"; the notes read Jesus' words as "confirmation of the Genesis narrative of creation" (Matthew 19:4) and as "confirming the Mosaic authorship of Deuteronomy" (19:8), and treat Paul's rulings as "the inspired words of an apostle" (1 Corinthians 7:12). The 1917 notes have no entry on inspiration at 2 Timothy 3:16, and the terms "verbal, plenary" and "inerrancy" belong to the tradition rather than to the notes read. |
| Salvation | Free grace, faith alone: salvation "is by grace through faith, is a free gift, and wholly without works," in three tenses — the believer saved from "the guilt and penalty of sin" "is safe," is being saved from sin's dominion, and will be conformed to Christ (Romans 1:16 note); justification is "the judicial act of God whereby He justly declares righteous one who believes on Jesus Christ," "by faith, not works" (Romans 3:28); eternal security follows from the 1 Peter 1:2 note on election. Not the full Calvinist scheme. |
| Baptism | Barely addressed: the Matthew 28:19 note treats the baptismal formula as revealing "the final name of the one true God," the Romans 6:3 note reads only "All we who were baptized," and there is no note on baptism at Acts 2:38 or Romans 6:4 — nothing on mode, subjects or efficacy. Believer's baptism is the assumption of the editorial tradition, not a teaching of the notes. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not treated as doctrine: the Matthew 26:20 note lists "the institution of the supper" among the events of the Passover night, the 26:28 note explains "remission" as forgiveness grounded in sacrifice, and the 1 Corinthians 11 notes gloss "unworthily" as "in an unworthy manner" and "damnation" as "judgment"; no note states a theology of Christ's presence, which the tradition leaves within the broad evangelical (memorial) range. |
| Church government | Not addressed as polity: the 1 Timothy 3:15 note distinguishes the visible church — "that visible body of professed believers called, collectively, 'the Church,'" existing "under many names and divisions based upon differences in doctrine or in government" — from the true church, and the Ephesians introduction says the epistle concerns the true church, not local church order; there are no notes on bishops or deacons at 1 Timothy 3:1–13. |
| Eschatology | Dispensational premillennialism: the Matthew 24 outline runs from "the great tribulation" of three and a half years, ending at Armageddon, to "the glorious appearing of the Lord, visible to all nations, and the regathering of Israel" (24:29–31), with "generation" at 24:34 read as "nation, or family of Israel"; the 1 Thessalonians 4:17 note makes the catching-up "peculiarly the 'blessed hope' of the Church" and includes "all bodies of the saved, of whatever dispensation" in the first resurrection; Revelation 20 is read as a literal millennium. The pretribulational timing is the system's, stated elsewhere in the notes than the passages read. |
| Spiritual gifts | Cessationist in principle: "Tongues and the sign gifts are to cease, and meantime must be used with restraint, and only if an interpreter be present" (1 Corinthians 14:1 note); yet "to each believer is given a spiritual enablement and capacity for specific service" (12:1), and the New Testament prophet "is not ordinarily a foreteller, but rather a forth-teller" (12:10). |
| Women's ordination | Women excluded from public teaching: the 1 Corinthians 14:1 summary holds that when "the whole church" met "in one place" "women were required to keep silence" (citing 14:34–35, 11:3–16 and 1 Timothy 2:11–14), and the 11:3 and 11:10 notes call the veil "a symbol of this subordination" and "the sign of the husband's authority"; there is no note at 1 Timothy 2:11–12 itself. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed: the 1917 notes have no entry at Exodus 21:22–25 (the chapter's only note, at 21:24, contrasts law and grace), reflecting the volume's early-twentieth-century composition; later revisions were not checked. | 1917 notes at Exodus 21 (none on the subject); later editions not checked |
| Homosexuality | Not addressed: the 1917 notes have no entry at Romans 1:26–27 or 1 Corinthians 6:9–10 (the only note in that passage, at 6:15, concerns fornication); later revisions were not checked. | 1917 notes at Romans 1 and 1 Corinthians 6 (none on the subject); later editions not checked |
| Alcohol | Not addressed: the 1917 notes have no entry on wine or drunkenness at Ephesians 5:18 or Genesis 9:20–21; the volume takes no position. | 1917 notes at Ephesians 5 and Genesis 9 (none on the subject); John 2, Proverbs and Leviticus 10 not checked |
| Divorce and remarriage | Not addressed: the 1917 notes at Matthew 19:3–9 concern only the Genesis narrative (19:4) and Mosaic authorship (19:8), and the 1 Corinthians 7 notes (7:12, 7:14) say nothing about divorce or remarriage; the permissive majority-evangelical reading often attributed to the volume is the tradition's, not the notes'. | 1917 notes at Matthew 19 and 1 Corinthians 7 (none on divorce) |
| Contraception | Not addressed: the 1917 notes have no entry anywhere in Genesis 38 (the chapter carries no notes), and the evangelical tradition the volume reflects treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | 1917 notes (Genesis 38 has none); the conscience position is the tradition's, not stated in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage: the one note touching the subject says Paul, against fornication, "appeals to the believer's sacredness as a member of Christ" rather than to the seventh commandment (1 Corinthians 6:15); there is no note at 1 Corinthians 7:2 or Hebrews 13:4. | 1917 note at 1 Corinthians 6:15; 1 Corinthians 7 and Hebrews 13 silent |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine: the Genesis 9:1 note lists "human government is established" (citing 9:1–6) among the elements of the Noahic covenant, and the Romans 13 notes are brief glosses (13:2 "damnation" as "judgment by the magistrate"); the just-war rather than pacifist orientation is the tradition's. | 1917 notes at Genesis 9:1 and Romans 13; the just-war orientation is a general pattern, not stated in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine: there is no separate note at Genesis 9:6; the 9:1 note's statement that "human government is established" cites 9:1–6, the passage containing the blood-for-blood command, and the Romans 13 notes are brief glosses — capital punishment is thereby left within the state's authority without advocacy. | 1917 notes at Genesis 9:1 and Romans 13 (no note at 9:6 itself) |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone, read dispensationally: "the Bible is one book," "a progressive unfolding of truth" whose harmony is "the unanswerable proof of the divine inspiration of the Bible" (Introduction); apostolic teaching is "the inspired words of an apostle" (1 Corinthians 7:12 note); church tradition is given no authority anywhere in the notes read. | Volume's Introduction and notes (1917) |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian: "Father, Son, and Holy Spirit is the final name of the one true God" — "(1) That God is one. (2) That He subsists in a personality which is threefold … (3) The conjunction in one name of the Three affirms equality and oneness of substance" (Matthew 28:19 note); orthodox on the person of Christ. | Volume's note at Matthew 28:19 |
| Predestination and free will | Moderately Calvinist: the 1 Peter 1:2 summary defines election as God's sovereign choice of persons for himself and for service, "according to the foreknowledge of God" and "wholly of grace, apart from human merit," both corporate (Israel, the church) and individual, while the notes preserve human responsibility; eternal security is taught firmly. | Volume's notes at 1 Peter 1:2 ("Elect") and Romans 9 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Positional and progressive: the 1 Corinthians 1:2 note contrasts the believer's position, which "is the result of the work of Christ," with his walk, which God conforms to that position through chastening, the Spirit and trials — "he grows in grace, not into grace"; the Keswick "deeper life" influence is evident. (The 1 Thessalonians 5:23 note concerns the trichotomy of spirit, soul and body, not sanctification.) | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 1:2 and 1 Thessalonians 5:23 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment: the Revelation 20:14 note holds that "'the second death' and the 'lake of fire' are identical terms" and "that the second death is not annihilation"; the 20:10 note adds that the notion the devil reigns in hell "is Miltonic, not biblical"; the Matthew 25:46 note observes that "'eternal' and 'everlasting' are the same word." | Volume's notes at Revelation 20:10–14 and Matthew 25:46 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant by silence: the 1917 notes have no entry on Mary at Luke 1 (the notes at 1:28 and 1:30 gloss only "angel"), none on the saints, and none on prayer for the dead; the volume neither venerates nor discusses them. | 1917 notes at Luke 1 (none on Mary); the Protestant position is the tradition's, not argued in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday distinguished sharply from the Jewish Sabbath: the Matthew 12:1 "Sabbath" summary holds that "the sabbath commemorates God's creation rest, the first day Christ's resurrection," that "the sabbath was a day of legal obligation, the first day one of voluntary worship and service," and that the Sabbath appears outside Acts only at Colossians 2:16 and Hebrews 4:4 as a type of the believer's rest; worship style is not addressed. | Volume's note at Matthew 12:1 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Pessimistic premillennialism: civil government and culture are expected to decline until Christ's return, and political reform is not presented as the church's task; the Romans 13 notes themselves are brief (13:2 "damnation" is glossed as "judgment by the magistrate"), the submission to government being assumed rather than argued. | Volume's eschatological notes; Romans 13:2 note |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The 1909 and 1917 editions' note on the Noahic Covenant at Genesis 9:1 declared that "from Ham will descend an inferior and servile posterity" (with Japheth's descendants the "enlarged" races and Shem's in "a peculiar relation to Jehovah"), language later used to defend segregation and removed in the 1967 New Scofield; current editions carry no such note. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:1 (1917 edition); removal in 1967 edition |
| Creation and the age of the earth | The 1917 notes teach a gap: at Genesis 1:1 "the first creative act refers to the dateless past, and gives scope for all the geologic ages," and at 1:2 the earth "had undergone a cataclysmic change as the result of divine judgment" (citing Jeremiah 4:23–27, Isaiah 24:1, 45:18); at 1:5 the note allows that "evening" and "morning" "may be held to limit 'day' to the solar day" but also that each creative day may be "a period of time, long or short." The 1967 and later editions soften this and leave the age of the earth open. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1, 1:2, 1:5 (1917 edition) and 1967 edition |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Christian giving is "a disposition created by the Spirit," "voluntary, and a test of sincerity and love," universal and "proportioned to income"; the Old Testament proportion was the tithe, "a proportion which antedates the law" (2 Corinthians 8:1 note), so the tithe is a guide rather than a law for the church. No prosperity teaching. | Volume's note at 2 Corinthians 8:1 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Published on the KJV (1909, 1917, and the lightly modernized 1967 New Scofield); later editions appear in the NIV, NASB, NKJV and ESV. The notes never claim the KJV as the only valid translation. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Traditional headship, stated briefly: the 1 Corinthians 11:3 and 11:10 notes call the woman's head-covering "a symbol of this subordination" and "the sign of the husband's authority"; the Ephesians 5 notes treat the church as bride (5:25, 5:32) without comment on wives and husbands, and 1 Timothy 2:11–15 has no note. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 11:3, 11:10; Ephesians 5 and 1 Timothy 2 silent on headship |

---

## 6. NLT Study Bible

**Translation used:** New Living Translation, second edition (text © 1996, 2004, 2007 Tyndale House Foundation).
**Publisher:** Tyndale House Publishers (features and Bible helps © 2008); the ebook edition read here carries the 2008 apparatus.
**Scale:** "A team of sixty scholars, writers, reviewers, editors, and designers" (the editors' introduction); verse-by-verse study notes for every chapter, nearly three hundred theme notes, about 220 Greek and Hebrew word studies, some 120 profiles of biblical people, book and section introductions ("Introduction to the Old Testament," "Introduction to the Pentateuch," "Introduction to the Letters of Paul," "The Time after the Apostles" and others), charts, timelines, maps, a subject index and the NLT concordance.

### General editor
**Sean A. Harrison** is General Editor, with **Mark D. Taylor** as Executive Editor. The contributors page names six general reviewers — Daniel I. Block (Genesis–Deuteronomy), Barry J. Beitzel (Joshua–Esther, maps), Tremper Longman III (Job–Song of Songs), John N. Oswalt (Isaiah–Malachi), Grant R. Osborne (Matthew–Acts) and Norman R. Ericson (Romans–Revelation) — and some fifty contributing scholars, among them Allen P. Ross and Andrew Schmutzer (Genesis), Eugene H. Merrill (Deuteronomy), Willem VanGemeren (Psalms, Isaiah), Iain Duguid (Ezekiel), Scot McKnight (Matthew), Robert Stein (Mark), Mark Strauss (Luke), Gary M. Burge (John), Douglas J. Moo (Romans, Colossians, 1–2 Peter, Jude), Roger Mohrlang (1 Corinthians, Ephesians, Philippians, Philemon), Ralph P. Martin (2 Corinthians), Gene L. Green (1–2 Thessalonians), Jon Laansma (1–2 Timothy, Titus), George Guthrie (Hebrews), Philip W. Comfort (1–3 John) and Gerald Borchert (Revelation). Kenneth N. Taylor, the Living Bible's author, is listed as special reviewer (deceased).

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and cross-denominational (the contributors page gives names only, no affiliations); the notes aim, in the editors' words, "to help people understand the Bible clearly and accurately" and leave applications to the Holy Spirit. Where a passage divides evangelicals the volume characteristically lays out the options in a theme note — three views of the millennium, three readings of 1 Timothy 2 — and declines to rule; where the Bible's own position is uncontested among evangelicals (sexual ethics, the deity of Christ, eternal punishment) it states it plainly. It is not confessionally Reformed, dispensational or charismatic.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inspired, "true, reliable, authoritative, permanent, and powerful because it comes from God himself": at 2 Timothy 3:16 the note says inspiration ("literally God-breathed") "does not negate the active involvement of the human authors. But it does affirm that God is fully responsible for his word," and that Paul's statement "applies to all Scripture, including the NT." The word "inerrant" is not used anywhere in the notes or introductions; the Genesis Introduction defends the book as history ("Genesis is not myth… The stories of Genesis are not fictional tales"). |
| Salvation | Justification by faith alone, with works as its fruit: Ephesians 2:8–9 is "a concise summary of how a person is saved… Salvation is for those who trust Christ alone to save them," and "good works are the result, not the cause, of salvation" (2:10); the "Righteousness by Faith" theme note concludes that "both Jews and Gentiles are accepted by God and made righteous before him on the basis of faith alone," while "Faith and Works" (Matthew 25) warns that "faith that does not result in works is not saving faith… Salvation is not achieved by works, but neither is it without works." Romans 3:25 reads *hilastērion* as Jesus "satisfies, or absorbs in himself, the anger of God." Perseverance: John 6:37–40 "those who come to him are secure," but Hebrews 6:4–8 is read as "a harsh warning about those who have left the Christian faith" whose apostasy "results in devastating judgment" — the Calvinist–Arminian question is not adjudicated. |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism as the sign of conversion, without ruling on infants: baptism "signifies a Christian's spiritual union with Christ… the rite of initiation in the Christian community," and "many interpreters see baptism as the Christian equivalent of circumcision" ("Baptism" theme note, Acts 2:38); it "indicates public identification with, and surrender to, Jesus" (Matthew 28:19); "baptism has no value apart from faith" (Romans 6:3), and 1 Peter 3:21 "does not mean that water baptism saves a person regardless of that person's heart… only people exercising faith toward God will benefit from baptism." Mode is not discussed. |
| The Lord's Supper | Memorial and spiritual participation, with the sacramental reading noted as others': Jesus "instituted the communion service that the disciples were to practice in remembrance of his death," the bread and cup being "symbols of his body and blood" (Luke 22:19–20); "believers share in the blood and body of Christ" and "sharing one loaf unites believers as one body" (1 Corinthians 10:16–18); John 6:53–58 observes that "some Christian traditions see the bread as Christ's literal flesh… Receiving communion means no less than embracing Jesus' life-giving sacrifice"; unworthy eating "treats his sacrificial death as trivial" and "may be expressed in infirmities, sickness, and even death" (11:27–30). |
| Church government | Local elders/overseers and deacons, with no polity prescribed: "overseer" and "elder" are "the same office" (1 Timothy 3:1); Paul "exercised influence and control over his entire mission field, but there is no evidence that he intended to build or leave a regional or mission-wide infrastructure of governance," and the Pastorals "are more concerned with ensuring that the Good News be faithfully transmitted than with perpetuating a form of government" ("Church Leaders" theme note); at Matthew 16:18 "it is not necessary to conclude that later church leaders must derive their authority from Peter." "There is no evidence of a paid clergy at this time" (1 Timothy 5:17–18). |
| Eschatology | Deliberately undecided: "The Thousand Years (The Millennium)" theme note sets out premillennialism, amillennialism and postmillennialism as views "held by Christian groups with an equally high regard for Scripture," adding that "true Christian humility and love… would never let such differences disrupt Christian fellowship" and that all agree "Christ will visibly return and rule in an actual new heaven and earth." Matthew 24 offers the AD 70 and second-coming readings side by side; the 144,000 "probably represent all faithful Christians" (Revelation 7:4–8); "rapture" appears at 2 Thessalonians 2:1 as "the resurrection and rapture of the church," and 666 "probably stood for Nero" ("The Antichrist"). |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuation assumed, with order and testing: the Spirit "remains with God's people today" ("Spirit Empowerment"); "in the early church, the gift of prophecy was not uncommon," given "to both men and women," "always in need of testing" ("The Gift of Prophecy"); tongues at 1 Corinthians 12:10 is "spiritual language that requires the spiritual gift of interpretation," placed "near the bottom of the list"; "none of the spiritual gifts gives us full understanding" (13:9). No cessationist statement appears, and the word "cessation" occurs only of the Sabbath and death. |
| Women's ordination | Three readings laid out, none chosen: "Women's Roles in the NT Church" (1 Timothy 2:11–15) presents the universal interpretation ("women in all times and places… must not teach men or exercise authority over men in the church"), the polemical (Paul "is merely to silence the false teachers in Ephesus") and the cultural ("an egalitarian culture… would see men and women as sharing" leadership); the 2:12 note says "there are no clear NT examples or endorsements of women teaching men in church meetings," while 14:34–35 "is to be understood in light of 11:5, which clearly implies that women are permitted to pray and prophesy publicly," Junia "was probably a woman" (Romans 16:7) and 1 Timothy 3:11 allows that "there might have been women deacons." Titus 1:5–9 "assumes that the elders will be male." |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed as such: Exodus 21:22–25 is read that "if a child's birth was caused prematurely and the child died… the penalty for murder was to be enacted," and Psalm 139:13 that "even when hidden in his mother's womb, the psalmist was guided by the Lord"; the words abortion and unborn occur only in the concordance's definitions. | Volume's notes at Exodus 21:22–25, Psalm 139:13 (no position stated) |
| Homosexuality | Condemned as "a perversion of God's intentions for men and women": "though homosexual activity was accepted in Greco-Roman culture, it is condemned throughout the Bible," and "those who freely give themselves to illicit sexual relations, whether homosexual or heterosexual, will not inherit the Kingdom of God… forgiveness and cleansing are available for those who turn to Christ" ("Holiness and Sexual Purity," 1 Corinthians 6:9–20); "engaging in homosexual activity is a violation of God's creative intention" (Romans 1:26); "homosexuality is but one example of sinful violation of God's order" (Leviticus 18:22). | Volume's theme note "Holiness and Sexual Purity"; notes at Romans 1:26–27, 1 Corinthians 6:9, Leviticus 18:22 |
| Alcohol | Moderation, not abstinence: "The OT is not against drinking wine in moderation (Prov 3:10; 9:5; Ps 104:14–15), but it adamantly opposes excessive drinking" (Proverbs 20:1); "the OT does not prohibit drinking alcohol, but many pious Jews… did abstain" (Colossians 2:16); "good wine symbolized God's blessing" (John 2:9–10); "the Holy Spirit, not wine, should dominate believers' lives" (Ephesians 5:18). | Volume's notes at Proverbs 20:1, Colossians 2:16, John 2:9–10, Ephesians 5:18 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Permanence, with the two exceptions: "Jesus permits only one legitimate reason for a man to divorce: if his wife has been unfaithful… Jesus affirmed the indissolubility of marriage" (Matthew 19:6, 9); the exception clause "permits but does not demand divorce," and remarriage after an unlawful divorce "is adulterous" (5:32; 19:9); "Paul also allows an exception if an unbelieving partner deserts the marriage" (Mark 10:11–12; 1 Corinthians 7:15); "the estrangement of divorce is a violent and cruel social crime" (Malachi 2:16). Ezra's dissolutions are "not prescriptive for believers today" ("Marriage and Divorce"). | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:31–32, 19:3–9, Mark 10:11–12, 1 Corinthians 7:10–15, Malachi 2:16; theme note "Marriage and Divorce" |
| Contraception | Not addressed: Onan's sin is that he "was not willing to provide an heir for his brother" (Genesis 38:9–10); children are "one of God's blessings" (Psalm 127:3–5); the words do not occur. | Volume's notes at Genesis 38:8–10, Psalm 127:3–5 (no position stated) |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the only notes touching it say the lots of Proverbs 16:33 "were not for gambling but a means commissioned by God to determine his will," and Job 6:27 "literally even gamble over an orphan." | Volume's notes at Proverbs 16:33, Job 6:27 (no position stated) |
| Dancing | Not addressed: no note treats dancing as a question. | Whole-text search (no position stated) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Reserved for marriage: "sexual relationships are to be restricted to marriage alone… It is therefore inappropriate for anyone who is united to Christ to have sexual relations with anyone outside of marriage" ("Holiness and Sexual Purity"); *porneia* is "any sexual union outside marriage" (1 Thessalonians 4:3); "sexual intimacy apart from marital commitment perverts the order that God intended for creation" ("Human Sexuality," Genesis 1:27–28); "immoral refers to all sexually illicit behavior" (Hebrews 13:4). Celibacy is commended as Paul's ideal (1 Corinthians 7:1, 36–38). | Volume's theme notes "Holiness and Sexual Purity," "Human Sexuality"; notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3, Hebrews 13:4, 1 Corinthians 7 |
| War/pacifism | Neither pacifist nor crusading: at Matthew 5:9 "Jesus is not calling for pacifism per se, but rather a different kind of activism"; Luke 22:36's sword is "metaphorical"; the Canaanite war "was limited historically and theologically to its OT setting," so that "the Crusades… or the more recent jihads of Islamic terrorism cannot be justified based on OT practice. … No justification for such action exists in the modern world" ("God's War," Deuteronomy 1:30); a biblical holy war "is a conflict where God fights for his people" ("Holy War"). Just-war theory is not expounded; the subject index lists "pacifism vs. activism." | Volume's theme notes "God's War," "Holy War"; notes at Matthew 5:9, Luke 22:36, Romans 13:1–5 |
| Death penalty | Theologically grounded for murder in the OT, left open for the state today: "The death penalty has a theological basis… Since murder destroys a person made in God's image, the ultimate penalty must be imposed on a murderer… We are not to pursue personal revenge, but are to uphold the justice of the 'governing authorities'" (Genesis 9:6); at Romans 13:4 the sword "might simply be a metaphor for punishment of any kind, but some interpreters believe it suggests that human governments… have the right to carry out capital punishment"; "death is the severest penalty in human justice" ("Death"). | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:5–6, Numbers 35:31–33, Romans 13:4; theme note "Death" |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture supreme, early tradition valued: "The Time after the Apostles" says "the church fathers of the earliest centuries can be considered authors and exponents of a founding tradition. Protestants might insist that tradition is not revelation, yet they will agree that the early tradition was an element of the Holy Spirit's providential working to define and preserve the Christian faith"; Mark 7:13 records that the Pharisees' traditions served "to cancel the word of God." The Apocrypha is not discussed in the OT Introduction's canon section. | Volume's section introduction "The Time after the Apostles"; "Introduction to the Old Testament," canon; note at Mark 7:9–13 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Fully orthodox: "Jesus Christ, the Word… not only revealed God but was God" (John 1:1); "Jesus as 100-percent divine and 100-percent human—form the bedrock of a Christian understanding of Christ" (1:14); "John explicitly affirms Christ's deity. Jesus shares the substance of God's being" (1:18); "Firstborn does not mean he was created" (Colossians 1:15); "the Father and the Son are two separate persons with one purpose and nature" (John 10:30); Genesis 1:26's "us" is judged unlikely to intend the Trinity, which "was revealed at a later stage in redemptive history." | Volume's notes at John 1:1–18, 10:30, Philippians 2:6–11, Colossians 1:15–19, Genesis 1:26 |
| Predestination and free will | Both affirmed, neither system adopted: "the sovereign God chooses his own people" and "God's choice is not unfair because he owes nothing to his sinful creatures" (Romans 9:13–16); "God hardened Pharaoh's heart… and Pharaoh hardened his own heart" (9:18); "God is in no way responsible for our sin… The remarkable fact is not that God allows some sinners to persist in their chosen delusions, but that he saves sinners" ("Divine Sovereignty and Human Responsibility," Ezekiel 14); "by his sovereign initiative, God embraces and blesses as his children those who trust in Christ" (Ephesians 1:5). | Volume's notes at Romans 8:30, 9:13–18, Ephesians 1:4–11; theme note "Divine Sovereignty and Human Responsibility" |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive, Spirit-wrought, no second blessing: "God enables Christians to live a holy life. His sanctifying work extends to the spirit and soul and body" (1 Thessalonians 5:23); believers "are being sanctified by the transforming work of the Holy Spirit" (Philippians 1:1); "both the desire and the power to do what pleases him come from God" (2:13); "God has made Christians holy by the work of his Holy Spirit… and they are therefore called to live as his own holy people" ("Holiness and Sexual Purity"). | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 5:23, Philippians 1:1, 2:12–13; theme note "Holiness and Sexual Purity" |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment, annihilationism noted and rejected: at Matthew 25:46 "some hold out hope that the punishment of the damned will, after 'an age,' be ended by annihilation or forgiveness. The parallel between eternal life and eternal punishment suggests strongly that it means 'lasting forever'"; "while the concept of unending punishment repulses some, it is the message of Revelation and of Scripture generally" (Revelation 20:10); "the eternality of hell is emphasized" (Mark 9:48); "a person's state after death is permanent" (Luke 16:26); "Hell, like heaven, is real" ("The Thousand Years"). | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:46, Mark 9:43–48, Luke 16:23–26, 2 Thessalonians 1:9, Revelation 20:10–15 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Jesus's brothers "are normally understood to have been children born to Joseph and Mary after Jesus' birth… some interpreters instead believe that they were either Joseph's children by an earlier marriage or Jesus' cousins, due to the Roman Catholic belief that Mary was perpetually a virgin" (Mark 3:31–35); "until probably implies that Joseph had sexual relations with Mary after the birth of Jesus" (Matthew 1:25); Luke 1:28's "Blessed are you among women" is a textual addition; John 19:27 is "a Jewish family law" and the birth of the church, not Mary's motherhood of believers. Purgatory and the invocation of saints are not mentioned. | Volume's notes at Mark 3:31–35, 6:3, Matthew 1:25, Luke 1:28, 48, John 19:27 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Lord's Day, Sabbath not binding: "Christians could continue to observe the Sabbath if they wanted to, but Paul insists that Christians have liberty on this matter… it is wrong for anyone to insist on Sabbath observance as a necessary expression of Christian piety" (Colossians 2:16); "observance of the Sabbath in its original form is not required of Christians" (Romans 14:5); "Christians met on the first day of each week… the day of the Lord's resurrection" (1 Corinthians 16:2; Acts 20:7; Revelation 1:10). Style and liturgy are not discussed; "God's Sabbath Rest" (Hebrews 4) is read as rest in Christ's finished work. | Volume's notes at Colossians 2:16, Romans 14:5, Acts 20:7, 1 Corinthians 16:2, Revelation 1:10; theme note "God's Sabbath Rest" |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission under God's prior claim: "our submission to governing authorities must always be in terms of our ultimate submission to God (see Acts 4:19–20)"; "whether they know it or not, governing authorities are serving God when they administer justice" (Romans 13:1–4); Jesus "was acknowledging two dominions, Caesar's and God's, the latter having priority" (Matthew 22:21); 1 Peter 2:13 is set against Nero's persecution. No political program is commended. | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–5, Matthew 22:21, 1 Peter 2:13–17, Colossians 3:18 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity in Christ, ethnic hatred condemned: "the church is one new people, a community where love and acceptance are prized and ethnic distinctions are no longer a source of division" (Ephesians 2:15); "everyone comes to Christ and receives God's promises in exactly the same way" (Galatians 3:28); "God disapproves of such hatred against any ethnic or religious group," with the Inquisition and the Holocaust named ("Ethnic Hatred," Esther 3); Noah's curse falls on Canaan's line, not on a race (Genesis 9:25), and Moses' Cushite wife is probably Zipporah (Numbers 12:1). | Volume's notes at Ephesians 2:14–15, Galatians 3:28, Acts 10:34–35, Genesis 9:25, Numbers 12:1; theme note "Ethnic Hatred" |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Historical creation by God, the length of the days left open: "the Hebrew *yom* can refer to daylight (1:5a), to a 24-hour period (1:5b), or to an unspecified time period (2:4b)" (Genesis 1:5); "the creation account describes the appearance of things from a human perspective" (1:6–8); Genesis 2 "is not a second creation account; rather, it is a theological and historical expansion"; the flood "covered the entire planet" and "was a true historical event" (6:17; 7:11–12); the Introduction insists "Genesis is not myth" and that "conservative scholars find it plausible that the biblical material accurately records actual events." The age of the earth and evolution are not discussed. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1–2:4, 6:17, 7:11–12; Genesis Introduction, "Literary Character"; theme note "The Creation" |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithe as Israel's "tax or tribute" (Deuteronomy 14:22–27), New Testament giving as grace: "Christian Giving" (2 Corinthians 8–9) summarizes Paul — giving "should spring from a willing mind and heart… is a joy… is marked by generosity and may entail sacrifice," and "the portion… is not specified" (1 Corinthians 16:2); Jesus at Matthew 23:23 speaks "without denying the validity of the tithe." Prosperity teaching is rejected: "Material Reward" (Job 4) calls the belief that "righteous people would always prosper materially" a view that "God's analysis of Job's life proved… to be false," "Faith" dismisses "a ticket to health and wealth," and 1 Timothy 6:5's false teachers were perhaps "espousing some version of a prosperity gospel." | Volume's theme notes "Christian Giving," "Material Reward," "Faith"; notes at Deuteronomy 14:22–27, Leviticus 27:30, Matthew 23:23, 1 Corinthians 16:2, 1 Timothy 6:5 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the NLT second edition, "the world-class scholarship of the ninety scholars who worked on the translation" (editors' introduction), with the "Note to Readers" and NLT Introduction explaining its "dynamic-equivalence" or "thought-for-thought" method and the claim that "all translations contain a mixture of these two philosophies" and the textual notes recording manuscript variants (e.g., Mark 16:9–20, John 7:53–8:11, Luke 1:28). KJV-onlyism has no standing in the volume. | Volume's front matter (NLT Introduction, Note to Readers, Translation Team); textual notes |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Headship as authority, framed by mutual submission and equality: "submit to one another: This general instruction… applies to all three relationships" (Ephesians 5:21); "to be the head is to have authority" (5:23–24) and "husbands are to love their wives just as Christ loved the church—that is, sacrificially" (5:25–33); "the husband's headship is balanced and informed by the equal status that men and women have before God" (1 Peter 3:7); the woman is "his perfect complement, made in the same image of God… given the same commission" (Genesis 2:18); the "NT Household" theme note explains the household codes as adaptations of Greco-Roman norms that would "result in the significant modification of cultural norms." | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:21–33, 1 Peter 3:1–7, Colossians 3:18, Genesis 2:18, 3:16; theme note "The NT Household" |

---

## 7. NASB Study Bible

**Translation used:** New American Standard Bible
**Publisher:** The Lockman Foundation (which also holds the NASB translation copyright) / Zondervan.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical, cross-denominational; the NASB itself is widely regarded as one of the most formally literal English translations, and this study Bible's notes are generally oriented toward close textual and grammatical study rather than one denomination's specific confessional framework.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed; the volume's evangelical contributor base and preface treat Scripture as fully truthful and authoritative. |
| Salvation | Not confessionally fixed; contributors span Reformed and Arminian views, and the notes generally present both where a passage is contested. |
| Baptism | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe both believer's and infant baptism as practiced in the contributing traditions without mandating either. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Not confessionally fixed; premillennial, amillennial, and postmillennial readings are noted where relevant, with a general evangelical premillennial lean in the tradition. |
| Spiritual gifts | Not confessionally fixed; the notes neither deny nor emphasize the continuation of the miraculous gifts. |
| Women's ordination | Not confessionally fixed; the notes generally present the complementarian and egalitarian readings of the relevant passages (1 Timothy 2, 1 Corinthians 11 and 14) without ruling. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Consistent with broad evangelical Protestantism; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Consistent with broad evangelical Protestantism; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | Consistent with broad evangelical Protestantism; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture is the final authority and historic creeds are respected but not treated as a second source; the notes do not appeal to church tradition to settle readings. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and orthodox on the deity and humanity of Christ throughout the notes on the relevant passages (John 1, Philippians 2, Colossians 1). | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Predestination and free will | Not confessionally fixed; the notes on Romans 9 and Ephesians 1 generally present both the Reformed and the Arminian reading rather than ruling between them. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification; the notes do not teach a distinct second work of grace. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment is assumed in the notes on Matthew 25:46 and Revelation 20; annihilationism is not presented as an option. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary is honored but not venerated, Jesus' "brothers" (Matthew 12:46) are read as literal siblings, and the notes give no basis for invoking saints or praying for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday worship assumed; the notes on Colossians 2:16 and Romans 14:5 treat the Sabbath command as fulfilled in Christ rather than binding, and no liturgical style is commended. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Church and state / political engagement | The notes on Romans 13 and 1 Peter 2 teach submission to and prayer for governing authorities without advocating a particular political program. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The notes on Acts 10, Galatians 3:28 and Ephesians 2 affirm the unity of all peoples in Christ; racial questions are not otherwise treated as a doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Notes adapted from the NIV Study Bible: the Genesis 1 notes present the literal-day, day-age and framework readings and decline to rule. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1, adapted from the NIV Study Bible (general pattern; wording not independently verified) |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | The notes treat the tithe as Israel's law and New Testament giving as generous and proportionate (2 Corinthians 8–9) without mandating a tenth; prosperity teaching is not present. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the New American Standard Bible; the preface commends a committee translation from the original languages. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | The notes on Ephesians 5 present the husband's headship as loving, sacrificial leadership and the wife's submission within mutual submission (5:21); complementarian in tone without pressing the question. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
