## 8. Perry Stone Hebraic Prophetic Study Bible

**Translation used:** King James Version — "the traditional 1611 KJV translation of the Bible, the version I personally have used the most throughout my ministry" (the author's letter).
**Publisher:** Perry Stone Ministries (Voice of Evangelism), copyright 2019, "published in association with Fedd Books, a division of The Fedd Agency, Inc."; issued in two volumes, Old Testament (first edition 2019; brown and navy editions) and New Testament, each carrying the author's letter.
**Scale:** "More than one million words" of commentary in all — "nearly 500,000 words" in the Old Testament volume and "over 300,000 words" in the New Testament volume — made up of verse notes, boxed "nuggets" and "In Depth" articles at the beginning and end of books, with the author's own Hebrew and Greek word studies, Jewish traditional sources (Mishnah, Midrash, Talmud) and prophetic patterns running throughout.

### General editor
**Perry Stone** (born 1959, Parsons, West Virginia), the author of the whole commentary, describes himself as "raised in a pastor's home and having three generations of ministers in my lineage," called to preach at sixteen. He founded Voice of Evangelism Outreach Ministries in Cleveland, Tennessee, in 1985 and has hosted the prophecy-centered television program *Manna-Fest* since 2000; he attended Lee University and holds a B.A. in theology from Covenant Life Christian College. His formative influence was the Pentecostal minister T. L. Lowery of the Church of God (Cleveland, Tennessee). One signed contribution is by another hand: the article "A Biblical Study of Strong Drink" (Proverbs in Depth) is by French Arrington, Ph.D., the Church of God theologian.

### Denominational leaning
Pentecostal, of the classical (Church of God, Cleveland, Tennessee) kind, with a strong "Hebraic roots" and prophecy emphasis: the notes teach the baptism in the Holy Spirit as distinct from conversion, nine present-day gifts of the Spirit, a prayer language, a fivefold ministry, a pretribulation rapture and a literal thousand-year reign, and are written in the first person as one minister's convictions rather than as a committee's consensus. The volume does not name a denomination.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | "The Bible is the inspired Word of God" (Genesis in Depth), "God's inspired Word" that "gives us promises, prophecies, and practical teachings" (Job in Depth); the New Testament introduction defends the manuscript reliability of the text and the Dead Sea Scrolls' confirmation of the Hebrew text. The words "inerrant" and "infallible" are not used of Scripture in the material read; the approach is literal, typological and prophetic. |
| Salvation | By grace through faith, with works rewarded: "A person is saved through God's grace, but each one will be rewarded at the judgment seat of Christ for our works and deeds" (Ephesians 2:8); "'works' to create righteousness are useless since genuine righteousness is imputed by the blood of Christ" (2:9); Luther is credited with seeing "that justification from sin takes place through the blood of Christ and faith alone, not through works" (Galatians in Depth). Salvation can be forfeited: Hebrews 10:29 is read of "a backslider and his severe punishment" (Exodus in Depth), and the Spirit "will depart forever if a person blasphemes Him." |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism in water following repentance — "in the early church, water baptism always followed repentance" (Acts 9:17–20) — distinct from "the baptism in the Holy Spirit" (John 3:22; Acts 9:17); the Trinitarian formula of Matthew 28:19 is defended against the "Jesus' name" reading. Infants are dedicated, not baptized: the Leviticus article "Incorporating Jewish Traditions into a Christian Dedication" gives a pattern for "a Christian infant dedication." Mode is not discussed in the notes read. |
| The Lord's Supper | Memorial and symbol: "eating His flesh and blood was a reference to receiving Holy Communion, or the Lord's Supper … in which the fruit of the vine represents Christ's blood and the bread represents His body" (John 6:53–58); "as we receive the fruit of the vine and the bread, we are showing forth the Lord's death until He comes" (Exodus in Depth); foot-washing "is still at times practiced among believers in various denominations after they celebrate the Lord's Supper" (John 13:14). Arrington's essay notes that Scripture never calls the cup "wine." |
| Church government | Not treated as polity; offices described: "qualifications for ordained elders and bishops" (Titus 1:5–9), elders "appointed at the churches to oversee various responsibilities and to assist in teaching" (Acts 20:17), deacons and deaconesses (1 Corinthians in Depth, "The Deaconess"), and "a fivefold ministry in the Church (see Eph. 4:11), which guides the Church in all spiritual truth" (Genesis 9; Exodus in Depth), with the apostle as "one sent forth with unique spiritual authority." |
| Eschatology | Dispensational premillennial, pretribulational: "After years of research, I lean toward a pre-tribulation catching away of the saints … The pre-tribulation rapture occurs prior to the seven-year Tribulation" (Ecclesiastes in Depth); the three timing views are set out at Zephaniah in Depth; the fall feasts prefigure the rapture, "the beginning of the Tribulation" and "the beginning of the coming thousand-year reign on earth (see Rev. 20:4)" (Leviticus 23); Israel's land promise "will be fulfilled in the millennial reign of Christ" (Genesis 15). |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuationist, Pentecostal: "Paul teaches that the nine gifts listed in 1 Cor. 12:7-10 will be operative in the Church until the return of the Lord … Thus, the gifts will continue until the return of Christ" ("Coming Behind in No Gift," 1 Corinthians 1:7–8); cessationists "emphasize" the textual doubt about Mark 16:16–20; "speaking in tongues" is "a prayer language" (Isaiah 28; Jeremiah in Depth), and the Holy Spirit "directs the church through prophetic utterance and words of knowledge" (Acts 13:1–5). |
| Women's ordination | Women in "all forms of ministry": at 1 Corinthians 14:34–35 "Paul is not dealing with the subject of women preachers, but of discipline and order during a religious service," women "were permitted to pray and prophesy and were active in ministry," 1 Timothy 2:12 forbids only that they "usurp authority," and the article concludes, "Thus God has and will continue to use women in all forms of ministry" ("Let the Women Keep Silence in the Church," 1 Corinthians in Depth); Phoebe's title diakonos is "one who assists or is a teacher or pastor" ("The Deaconess"). |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Opposed from conception: "if the soul and spirit enter the fetus at the moment of conception, then the willful termination of the fetus would equate to taking an actual life," and the article concludes from Psalm 139, Jeremiah 1:5 and Luke 1:41–44 that "the eternal soul and spirit are a part of the development of the child in the womb"; the "pro-choice" theory "cannot be accurate" ("Known Before You Were Born," Jeremiah 1:5); abortion is "the shedding of innocent blood" (Habakkuk 1:3–4; 1 Kings 21). | Volume's articles at Jeremiah 1:5 and 1 Kings 21; notes at Habakkuk 1:3–4, 1 Samuel 15 |
| Homosexuality | Sinful: Romans 1:24–27 is read as "a reference to same-sex relationships" that "will bring the judgment of God and eventual spiritual death to those who know they are doing wrong, and refuse to turn from these sins and repent" (Romans in Depth); reading David and Jonathan as a homosexual relationship "is truly a twisted and perverted interpretation" (1 Samuel 18:1–4); Leviticus 18:22 is listed among the family codes. | Volume's article "The Wrath of God Unleashed" (Romans 1); notes at 1 Samuel 18:1–4, Judges 1:17, Leviticus 18 |
| Alcohol | Abstinence: the signed article "A Biblical Study of Strong Drink" (French Arrington) concludes, "I feel the best choice for a follower of Christ is total abstinence from all strong drink," with Proverbs 23:30–31 "not to [be used] as license for moderate drinking"; Stone's own notes say "when people who once abstained from alcohol begin to drink, they often end up in adulterous situations" (Proverbs 23:29–32) and list "drinking alcohol" among costly sins (29:3); Philippians 4:5 does not "justify 'moderation' in drinking alcoholic beverages," and Ephesians 5:18, which "has been interpreted to mean that Paul is permitting drinking wine as long as it is in moderation," is rendered instead "Do not be drunk with wine wherein there is debauchery" (Arrington). | Volume's article at Proverbs in Depth; notes at Ephesians 5:18, Proverbs 23, 29:3, Philippians 4:5, 1 Timothy 6:9–10 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Permitted for sexual unfaithfulness only: "Christ, however, aligned with the conservative view—that the only reason for a divorce was marital unfaithfulness (fornication and adultery, v. 9)" (Matthew 19:9); marriage is "one man … becoming one flesh … living as husband and wife until death separated them" (19:5–6); the Pauline case at 1 Corinthians 7 is not annotated. | Volume's note at Matthew 19:3–9 |
| Contraception | Not addressed: the Genesis 38 note says only that Onan "refuses to carry on the name and was slain," and no note or article treats birth control. | Volume's note at Genesis 38:8–10 (none on contraception) |
| Gambling | Discouraged as a vice: "the vices of gambling, selling drugs, consuming alcoholic beverages, participating in prostitution, and viewing pornography are all based on the lust for money" (1 Timothy 6:9–10); "cunning craftiness is the trickery gamblers use" (Ephesians 4:14); Philippians 4:5 is not a license for "'moderation' … in gambling." | Volume's notes at 1 Timothy 6:9–10, Ephesians 4:14, Philippians 4:5 |
| Dancing | Dancing in worship affirmed; social dancing not addressed: David "would dance, worship, and rejoice in God regardless of her criticism" (2 Samuel 6:20–22), and "Praise him with … the dance" is applied to the restored believer ("Dancing with Broken Bones," Psalm 51:8). | Volume's notes at 2 Samuel 6:20–22; article at Psalm 51:8 |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Condemned: Leviticus 18:20 is summarized "You were not to have sexual relations outside of marriage" (Leviticus in Depth family codes); Hebrews 13:4 teaches that "marriage relations (the bed) between a husband and wife are honorable, but those adulterers and whoremongers" face judgment; "sexual sins" are the strongest bondages of the last days (Ecclesiastes in Depth). | Volume's notes at Hebrews 13:4, Leviticus 18; Ecclesiastes in Depth |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the state's sword accepted: "if they resist, the ruler bears the sword, meaning that he has the ability to execute judgment upon the resisters" (Romans 13:4); Israel's wars since 1948 are described approvingly as self-defense (Isaiah 13). No note discusses a Christian's own participation in war. | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–8 and Isaiah 13 (none on pacifism) |
| Death penalty | Described, not advocated: Genesis 9:5–6 "is the first reference to a death penalty for anyone who would purposely kill another human being," and "a premeditated slaying … if not repented of and the criminal dealt with, the land where the blood was shed could eventually become cursed"; "particular transgressions carried the death penalty" under Moses (Deuteronomy 17:1–7). | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:5–6, Deuteronomy 17:1–7, Romans 13:4 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture as the standard, with Jewish tradition used freely for illumination: the New Testament introduction explains the canon as fixed by the "Rule of Faith," and the commentary draws constantly on the Mishnah, Midrash and "church tradition" for background while treating only "the inspired Word of God" as authoritative; Roman practice such as indulgences and purgatory is rejected (Galatians in Depth; Revelation 17). | Volume's Introduction to the History of the Bible; notes at Revelation 17:8–18; Galatians in Depth |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian: the Holy Spirit "is the third person in the Godhead; He is not an 'it' or a 'thing'" (Judges 3:10); against those who "reject the triune Godhead of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit," John 14:7–12 shows Christ is "in the Father" and going "back to the Father" (John 14:5–12); Christ is "the Son of God and in Him dwelt the fullness of the Godhead" (1 Kings in Depth); Isaiah 7:14 "is a direct prediction of Christ's virgin birth." The Comma Johanneum is cited without comment (Ecclesiastes 12:1). | Volume's notes at Judges 3:10, John 14:5–12, Isaiah 7:14; 1 Kings in Depth |
| Predestination and free will | Foreknowledge, not predestination to damnation: Romans 8:29 "has been a source of debate for centuries, as some use this verse as 'proof' that God has pre-ordained some people to be saved and others to be eternally lost. God determined beforehand … that He would send Christ to suffer … He also foreknew that some individuals would choose Christ and others would not. He pre-planned that those who do choose this New Covenant would be conformed to His Son" (Romans 8:29); at Ephesians 1:4–5 "all human existence was previously foreknown and foreordained." | Volume's notes at Romans 8:29, 9:6–16, Ephesians 1:4–5 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive and ongoing: "sanctification is both an act and a continual process, a daily renewing of the mind (see Rom. 12:2)"; "through justification the spirit is delivered, through sanctification the mind is delivered, and through glorification the body is finally delivered" (Titus in Depth; Romans 8:30–32). No second definite work is taught in the material read. | Volume's article at Titus in Depth; note at Romans 8:30–32 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment; annihilation denied: "some people teach that at some point in time God will destroy the spirits of those in hell … The main point that counters this theory are the phrases 'eternal damnation' or 'everlasting fire' … Everlasting means 'everlasting'; nothing more and nothing less" (Isaiah in Depth, with a list of the "eternal" texts); Jude 7's "eternal fire" (Judges in Depth). | Volume's article at Isaiah in Depth; Judges in Depth |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: "Joseph and Mary had other children besides Christ (see Mark 6:3)" (John 2:1–11); Mary is "blessed" as the virgin who bore the Savior (Jeremiah in Depth); purgatory is "one of the conflicting doctrines that the Reformers resisted," under which relatives "could pray that person out of purgatory through the prayers of the priest" (Revelation 17:8–18), and Mark 9:49 "is not a reference to some form of purgatory." Veneration of saints is not discussed. | Volume's notes at John 2:1–11, Revelation 17:8–18, Mark 9:49–50 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday worship by apostolic precedent, with the Jewish feasts and Sabbath valued as patterns: "Instead of ministering on Saturday (the Jewish Sabbath) in synagogues, Paul is now ministering on Sunday, the 'first day of the week' … the Gentiles began meeting on the first day of the week, the time when the women saw Christ alive" (Acts 20:7–12); Christians "can … bless our children each week during the Jewish Sabbath or the traditional Christian Sabbath" (Leviticus in Depth); the "Lord's day" of Revelation 1:10 is read as the Day of the Lord, not Sunday. Style is charismatic and expressive (David's dance, Psalm 150). | Volume's notes at Acts 20:7–12, Revelation 1:10; Leviticus in Depth; Psalm 51 article |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission to government: "Paul instructs believers to be subject to the human government leaders. The word subject is a military word referring to soldiers marching in order," and "resisting legal authority causes displeasure with God and leads to punishment from human leaders" (Romans 13:1–8); Proverbs 29:2 "would be an appropriate verse to read from pulpits before national elections"; the nation is warned of judgment for "shedding innocent blood" (1 Kings 21). | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–8, Proverbs 29:2; article at 1 Kings 21 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity in Christ: "Under the New Covenant, the blood of Christ makes all believers one in faith and one family of God. There are no racial or ethnic divisions. All men and women are formed in God's image" (1 Corinthians in Depth, on Galatians 3:28); Acts 17:26 ("of one blood all nations") is printed without a note. | Volume's article at 1 Corinthians in Depth |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Literal days, a young humanity, with the Gap Theory presented as an option: "The Hebrew word for day here in Genesis is yom, and throughout the Scriptures this word refers to a literal period from sunrise to sunset, or a twenty-four-hour day" (Genesis 1:5, after listing the day-age views); "Biblical research indicates that the earth is approximately six thousand years old," and the Gap Theory "is one way some scholars explain the scientific criticisms of a six-thousand-year-old earth … however, the creation of Adam only goes back about six thousand years" ("The Gap Theory and the Creation of Hell," Genesis 1:1–2, citing the 1917 Scofield notes and Finis Dake). Evolution is treated as a secular theory. | Volume's note at Genesis 1:5 and article at Genesis 1:1–2 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithing taught as a standing principle, prosperity as covenant blessing: "Some 'tithe bashers' point out that nowhere in the New Testament does God ask for 10 percent. This is correct, but it is correct because the principle was already established … Under the New Covenant, we should not limit God or ourselves to just 10 percent" ("The Truth Behind Tithing and Giving," Malachi 3); "many believers are skeptical of a minister when receiving an offering" (Leviticus); at 3 John 2, to deny "that it is God's will for all believers to have a successful journey, be healthy, and have souls that are well, would do an injustice to … the spiritual, emotional, physical, and financial blessings of every believer" — a defense of the verse against critics of "the so-called 'prosperity message.'" | Volume's articles at Malachi 3 and Leviticus; note at 3 John 2 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | KJV by conviction, short of KJV-onlyism: the 1611 text is "my preferred translation," kept because "many of the other translations leave out important words contained in the text" (the author's letter); the New Testament introduction traces the KJV to Erasmus's Greek text and says that "though these translations may differ, we rest assured that the manuscripts the divine text is based on has been preserved and translated by scholars and men of God for over two millennia"; the notes themselves freely correct KJV renderings from the Hebrew and Greek. | Volume's letter from the author; Introduction to the History of the Bible |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Headship with mutual love in the home, open ministry in the church: "The husband is the head of the wife as Christ is head of the church (v. 23). The wife must submit to her husband as the church must submit to Christ (v. 24). There must be mutual love between the husband and wife (v. 25)" (Ephesians 5:22–33); in ministry, women are to be used "in all forms," 1 Timothy 2:12 forbidding only "usurp[ing] authority" (1 Corinthians in Depth). | Volume's note at Ephesians 5:22–33; article "Let the Women Keep Silence in the Church" |

---

## 9. Baker CSB Illustrated Study Bible

**Translation used:** Christian Standard Bible (CSB)
**Publisher:** Baker Books, a division of Baker Publishing Group, Grand Rapids (study material © 2018 Baker Publishing Group; CSB text © 2017 Holman Bible Publishers, used under license); the ebook edition was created in 2018 (ISBN 978-1-4934-1528-1).
**Scale:** Section-by-section study notes (about 730,000 words, drawn from *The Baker Illustrated Bible Commentary*, 2012), book introductions with outlines, "more than 150 articles," "more than 450" definitions (from *The Baker Compact Dictionary of Theological Terms* and the Baker Bible dictionaries), "more than 100 full-color maps," artists' reconstructions, time lines, charts and a concordance — in all roughly a million words of study material alongside the CSB text.

### Editorial credit
Produced by Baker's editorial team with five contributing editors — Gary M. Burge (Calvin Theological Seminary), J. Scott Duvall and J. Daniel Hays (Ouachita Baptist University), Andrew E. Hill (Wheaton College) and Tremper Longman III (Westmont College) — and consulting editor Adam Barr; the study notes and introductions are credited book by book to some fifty named scholars (Victor P. Hamilton on Genesis, Walter C. Kaiser Jr. on Deuteronomy, Daniel J. Estes on Job, Willem A. VanGemeren on Isaiah and Daniel, among others), "edited and in some cases abridged, so that the final form of the notes are a collaborative product," with more than 200 further contributors listed for articles, definitions and captions. An institutional, multi-scholar project in the same category as the Life Application Study Bible and NIV Study Bible profiled elsewhere in this section.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and deliberately cross-denominational: the contributors come from Baptist (Ouachita, Southwestern, Truett), Reformed (Calvin), Wesleyan (Asbury), Adventist (Andrews), Mennonite Brethren and non-denominational evangelical institutions, and the notes regularly set out the competing evangelical readings of a contested passage rather than rule between them. Its Southern Baptist connection is only through the CSB text licensed from Holman (B&H's own text-focused *CSB Study Bible* is a separate volume not profiled here).

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inspired and wholly trustworthy: "The Scriptures are entirely trustworthy. They are the very breath of God (3:16a)" (2 Timothy 3:15–17 note); the definition of inspiration calls Scripture "the product of the creative breath of God" and "the superintending work of the Spirit bearing along the biblical authors as they wrote." The word "inerrant" appears only in the licensed CSB translators' introduction ("God's inspired Word, inerrant in the original manuscripts"), not in Baker's own introduction or definitions. |
| Salvation | Justification by faith alone, Reformation-style, with works as its fruit: the article "Justification by Faith" says the confession "that 'faith alone' makes a person right with God became a central element of the Reformation and continues to distinguish Protestant Christianity from Roman Catholicism," justification being "the act of God's saving judgment … in which the fallen human being is both forgiven and created anew"; James and Paul "are like soldiers in the same army standing back to back" ("Faith and Works in James 2:24 and Galatians 2:15–16"). Perseverance is affirmed at Hebrews 6:4–8: apostates "were never born again or made genuine partakers of the redemption purchased by Christ." |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism assumed, immersion noted: at Romans 6:3–4 "the term baptizō ('baptize') refers not to a particular ritual but to the act of immersion," so that "sinners who have come to faith were 'immersed into' the Messiah Jesus"; at Acts 2:38 "those who are baptized in his name will receive the eschatological Spirit." Infant baptism is not discussed, and no note argues the mode or subjects as a doctrine. |
| The Lord's Supper | Memorial and proclamation, with the realist dispute left open: "whatever the relationship established between the body and the bread, both are given for the benefit of all … that all may share in one body and celebrate their unity together in this memorial" (1 Corinthians 11:23–26); the definition calls it "the meal of remembrance instituted by Jesus," whose observance "proclaims the gospel, fosters remembrance of Christ's sacrifice, signals the new covenant relationship, unites Christians with Christ, and portrays unity in the church." John 6 is given a "eucharistic application." |
| Church government | Two offices, elders in plurality, no polity prescribed: "scholars commonly agree that [elder and overseer] refer to the same office … There are then two main offices of church leaders: overseers/elders and deacons" ("Church Leaders in the New Testament"); "Elders apparently always function in a plurality in Scripture, never as solo leaders" (definition "Elder"); deacons "assisted the elders/overseers in practical ways" (definition "Deacon"). Episcopal, presbyterian and congregational systems are not compared. |
| Eschatology | Deliberately uncommitted: the article "Millennialism" sets out premillennialism, postmillennialism and amillennialism in turn without a verdict, and the definition "Rapture" says "there is disagreement as to the timing of the rapture," describing the pretribulational and the posttribulational views side by side. |
| Spiritual gifts | Both sides stated, neither adopted: "'Cessationists' believe such miraculous activities ceased at the end of the apostolic age … 'Continuationists' agree that miracles confirmed the message in the first century but ask whether there is no need for confirmation now, particularly since nowhere is it written in the Bible that miracles will cease," adding that "many recent cessationists and continuationists agree that 1 Co 13:10 does not speak to this issue because the second coming of Christ is in view" (article "Spiritual Gifts"). |
| Women's ordination | Three readings presented, the third given most room: at 1 Timothy 2:12–14 "some believe the prohibition is absolute" (but "Paul seems to endorse women ministering through speech in the congregation in 1 Co 11:5"), others hold it "secondary or temporary" (but "Paul's argument is not primarily situational but theological"), and "still other commentators believe that Paul extends to women permission to participate in the ministry of the Word in the congregation … except in a mixed-gender setting (1 Co 14:31–35) or a formal teaching of the church, in which cases Paul stipulates male leadership"; Junia is "likely … a feminine name" of uncertain role (definition). Not the Baptist Faith and Message's flat reservation of the pastorate to men. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed as such: the Exodus 21:22–25 note reads the injury as "that which occurs to the woman," with "the fine [applying] to the prematurely born or miscarried children"; Pharaoh's order is called "outright infanticide" (Exodus 1:22); Jeremiah 1:5 and Psalm 139:13–16 carry no application; the only occurrence of the word is descriptive, among first-century Roman women "practicing contraception and abortion" (1 Timothy 2:9–10). | Volume's notes at Exodus 21:22–25, 1:22, Jeremiah 1:4–10, Psalm 139:13–24, 1 Timothy 2:9–10 (no position stated) |
| Homosexuality | Same-sex acts judged sinful: "God created man and woman, male and female (Gn 1:27) to become 'one flesh' (Gn 2:24). Paul's language denies same-sex sexual activity as right or good" (Romans 1:25–27); "the nontechnical language of 18:22 is unambiguous: for a man to sexually lie with another man as with a woman is abominable to God. Censured here is not simply homosexual tendency but acting on it" (Leviticus 18:22); the Sodom article notes "homosexual practice was not one singular sin there" but that the narrative "centers on the perversion of sexuality." | Volume's notes at Romans 1:25–27, Leviticus 18:22; article "The Detestable Acts of Sodom and Gomorrah" |
| Alcohol | Drunkenness condemned, use not forbidden: Proverbs 23 "condemn[s] (1) gluttony and especially drunkenness as leading to rags, not riches"; Ephesians 5:18 contrasts the Spirit-filled with "those drunk with wine"; Paul "prescribes 'a little wine'" for Timothy (1 Timothy 5:23); John the Baptist's "abstinence from alcohol" is explained by the Nazirite vow (Luke 1:15). No note calls for abstinence. | Volume's notes at Proverbs 20:1, 23:20–35, Ephesians 5:18, 1 Timothy 5:23, Luke 1:14–25 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Unfaithfulness and an unbeliever's departure as grounds: Jesus's teaching "allows for it only in cases of unfaithfulness, fitting the more conservative interpretation of Deuteronomy" (Matthew 19:9), and "affirms both lifelong marriage and singleness"; Paul, "as a realist and not a legalist," provides for separation (1 Corinthians 7:11) and tells the believer with an unbelieving spouse not "to seek divorce" while the spouse is willing (7:12–16). | Volume's notes at Matthew 19:3–12, 1 Corinthians 7:10–16, Deuteronomy 24:1–4 |
| Contraception | Not addressed: Onan "refuses to exercise his responsibility, most likely in order to guarantee he will get his hands on his older brother's inheritance" (Genesis 38:8–11), and contraception is mentioned only as a first-century Roman practice (1 Timothy 2:9–10). | Volume's notes at Genesis 38:8–11 and 1 Timothy 2:9–10 (no position stated) |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the casting of lots is explained historically (Urim and Thummim; Acts 1), and "gamble" occurs only figuratively (Rehoboam "gambles away the kingdom"; Job's friends "gamble for orphans"). | Volume's notes at Exodus 28:30, 1 Samuel 10:20–21, Job 6:27 (no position stated) |
| Dancing | Not addressed: dancing appears only in narrative description (Jephthah's daughter, the women of Shiloh, Israel's victory songs, David before the ark). | Volume's notes at Judges 11:34, 21:19–21, 1 Samuel 18:6, 2 Samuel 6:5 (no position stated) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sex reserved for marriage: "sanctification, here understood as sexual purity, is the will of God" and "this text is one of the many that call Christians to self-control" (1 Thessalonians 4:3–5); sexual impurity is a sin "of dissatisfaction with God's provision" whose antidote is "contentment and fulfillment in what God has given" (Hebrews 13:4); Proverbs 5 praises "sexual pleasure within the context of marriage" while "sex outside of marriage is discouraged." | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3–8, Hebrews 13:4, Proverbs 5:15–23, Leviticus 18:20 |
| War/pacifism | Both Christian positions named, neither adopted: "Some Christians believe that involvement in government should be avoided because it can exercise deadly force (in war and capital punishment), which violates the law of love. Others believe that such involvement is demanded by the mandate to build civilization" (definition "Government"); Matthew 5:38–42 "emphasizes nonresistance as a nonnegotiable for Jesus's disciples" in personal insults; the article "Holy War in the Old Testament" treats Israel's wars historically. | Volume's definition "Government"; notes at Matthew 5:38–42; article "Holy War in the Old Testament" |
| Death penalty | Described, not advocated: the sixth commandment "prohibits murder and refers only to illegal killing … The primary concerns here are not the death penalty or warfare," and "the punishment for murder is the death penalty (21:12)" under the torah (Exodus 20:13); Genesis 9:5–6 is noted only as holding "even animals" accountable; the "Government" definition records that some Christians object to the state's "deadly force (in war and capital punishment)." | Volume's notes at Exodus 20:13, Genesis 9:1–7; definition "Government" |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture as the norm, with the creeds and the Reformation read approvingly: the Chalcedonian Formula is quoted as the church's statement of "the very heart of the Christian faith" ("The Deity and Humanity of Christ"); "faith alone" is upheld against Roman Catholicism ("Justification by Faith"); Mary's perpetual virginity is attributed to "ecclesial or confessional needs" rather than the text ("The Family of Jesus"). Tradition is used as historical evidence, never as a second authority. | Volume's articles "Justification by Faith," "The Deity and Humanity of Christ," "The Family of Jesus" |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Chalcedonian: "Christians believe Jesus Christ is a person who is both human and divine. Existing as one person, he nonetheless possesses two 'natures,' and, in the words of the Chalcedonian Formula (451), he has these natures 'without confusion, without change, without division, without separation'" ("The Deity and Humanity of Christ"); incarnation "moves faith from the realm of mythology to the realm of history" ("The Incarnation"); the Baker introduction opens with "God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit." | Volume's articles "The Deity and Humanity of Christ," "The Incarnation"; Introduction |
| Predestination and free will | Held in tension, with the notes diverging: the definition "Election" sets the foreknowledge view against Romans 9:16 and concludes that "the proper balance in Scripture seems to involve both God's sovereignty and human involvement," citing Spurgeon's two rails; "Predestination" says God "determined that some individuals would be the recipients of his salvation" yet "this determination does not rule out the necessity of human choice"; the Romans 9 note says election is "the result of God's mercy, not the result of human desire or effort," while the Ephesians 1:4–6 note reads that "this predestination applies to all humanity, not to some elect portion." | Volume's definitions "Election," "Predestination"; notes at Romans 9:6–21, Ephesians 1:4–6 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Relational and ongoing, no second work: "'To be holy' (sanctified) means 'to belong to God'" and "sanctification centers on deepening the relationship between God and the Spirit-filled Christian" (definition "Sanctification"); "justification and sanctification are different dimensions of the one saving act of God in Christ, and not distinct events that may be placed in a logical order" ("Justification by Faith"); believers "are consistently devoted to righteousness and holy living" (Romans 6:1–2). | Volume's definition "Sanctification"; article "Justification by Faith"; note at Romans 6:1–2 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment: hell is "the place where the lost are assigned by God to eternal punishment of both body and soul (Mt 10:28). This agony of eternal torment in hell is the greatest of all possible tragedies," and "Revelation describes how their torment is eternal (Rv 14:11)" (definition "Hell"). Annihilationism and universalism are not discussed. | Volume's definition "Hell" |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Jesus's brothers and sisters were "'blood' brothers and sisters born to Mary and Joseph after the birth of Jesus," the rival theories being "an attempt to defend the developing tradition of Mary's perpetual virginity," for which "there is no reason apart from ecclesial or confessional needs" ("The Family of Jesus"); Mary is "typically cast as lacking insight into the nature of Jesus's mission." Invocation of saints and prayer for the dead are not discussed. | Volume's article "The Family of Jesus"; note at Matthew 12:46–50 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday by apostolic practice, the Sabbath question left open: Revelation 1:10's Lord's Day is glossed "(i.e., Sunday)," and Acts 20:7 notes meeting "on the first day of the week"; "Gentiles therefore need not … observe the Sabbath day, since these are merely shadows of the reality—which is Christ himself" ("Jewish Sabbath Observances"), though the definition "Sabbath" records that "some Christians understand this as denying continuity of the Sabbath principle of a weekly day of rest. Others understand it" more narrowly. Style and liturgy are not addressed. | Volume's notes at Revelation 1:10, Acts 20:7; article "Jewish Sabbath Observances"; definition "Sabbath" |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission with a limit: "believers obey the edicts, rules, and regulations issued by government officials" and "must pay taxes," because "God has ordained and appointed all governing authorities," yet "Romans 13 (submission to the state as a God-given authority) therefore must be balanced by Rv 13 (obeying God rather than the state when the state becomes an agent of evil)" (Romans 13:1–7); the midwives' defiance is "the first recorded instance in the Bible of civil disobedience in the face of an immoral law" (Exodus 1:17–21). Whether Christians should hold office is left as a disagreement (definition "Government"). | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–7, Exodus 1:17–21; definition "Government" |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity in Christ: in Galatia, "where distinctions of national origin, gender, and economic status were the defining tools for human interaction, Paul declares the inauguration of a new paradigm of human value," and "once they have been joined to Christ, all temporal distinctions become meaningless" (Galatians 3:26–29); Miriam and Aaron's complaint may be "a racial slur against Zipporah … for the darker color of her Midianite skin" (Numbers 12:1). Racism is not otherwise treated. | Volume's notes at Galatians 3:26–29, Numbers 12:1, Acts 10:34–36 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Literary and theological reading, age not fixed: "in the beginning" is "the Hebrew way of saying, 'a long time ago,' without stipulating how long ago it was" (Genesis 1:1–2); the account "is presented not mechanically but poetically. It has structure and symmetry … God is not following a step-by-step instruction manual" and runs "in two interrelated cycles" of domains and inhabitants ("Parallel Days of Creation in Genesis 1"); the notes observe "sources of light in the universe (day one) besides sunlight (day four)." The length of the days and the age of the earth are not ruled on. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1–13; article "Parallel Days of Creation in Genesis 1" |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithe as an Old Testament principle, New Testament giving generous and free: "the OT concept of the tithe is assumed by Jesus in Mt 23:23 but otherwise is not frequently mentioned in the NT. The NT instead focuses on the attitude of the believer, who should give sacrificially … and in love with generosity (2 Co 9:6–8), trusting that God will provide" (definition "Stewardship"); "each person should feel free to decide in faith on the amount of a gift" (2 Corinthians 9:6–11). Prosperity teaching is absent. | Volume's definitions "Stewardship," "Tithe"; note at 2 Corinthians 9:6–11 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the Christian Standard Bible (2017), whose licensed introduction gives its textual base as the Nestle-Aland 28th edition, UBS 5th and Biblia Hebraica Stuttgartensia 5th and its philosophy as "optimal equivalence"; Baker's introduction calls the CSB "a highly readable, highly reliable text." KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's Introduction to the Christian Standard Bible; Baker's Introduction |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Mutual submission stressed: Ephesians 5:22–6:9 is headed "Wisdom as mutual submission"; the husband's headship is "standard cultural wisdom," yet "the entire section is governed by 5:21: husbands are to submit to their wives," the text having "with tragic irony … served for centuries to sanctify the abuse of women" when interpreters "stop interpreting at verse 24"; at 1 Corinthians 11:3 "head" may mean "rule and authority" or "source and origin," the second having "the advantage"; the household codes "uphold, but at the same time subvert, the first-century social status quo" (article). Closer to the egalitarian pole than any other evangelical volume in this section. | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:22–33, 1 Corinthians 11:3–5; article "Household Codes" |

---

## 10. NLT Illustrated Study Bible

**Translation used:** New Living Translation
**Publisher:** Tyndale House Publishers, first published 2015 (winner, 2016 ECPA Christian Book Award, Bible of the Year).
**Scale:** Over 1,000 photos, illustrations, and infographics; more than 25,000 study notes; over 120 profiles of biblical people and empires; 150+ full-color maps and photos; 300 theme notes; 200 Hebrew and Greek word studies.

### Editorial credit
Produced as an institutional, multi-scholar Tyndale House project distinct from the earlier, text-focused NLT Study Bible (entry 6 above); this edition's own distinguishing feature is its extensive visual apparatus (full-color photographs, artistic reconstructions, and infographic-style presentation of background material) rather than a different underlying theological orientation.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical, cross-denominational, in the same general category as the standard NLT Study Bible; reviewers have specifically noted the volume's conservative interpretive stance on questions where its notes engage historical-critical objections to traditional authorship or historicity.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed; the volume's evangelical contributor base and preface treat Scripture as fully truthful and authoritative. |
| Salvation | Not confessionally fixed; contributors span Reformed and Arminian views, and the notes generally present both where a passage is contested. |
| Baptism | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe both believer's and infant baptism as practiced in the contributing traditions without mandating either. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Not confessionally fixed; premillennial, amillennial, and postmillennial readings are noted where relevant, with a general evangelical premillennial lean in the tradition. |
| Spiritual gifts | Not confessionally fixed; the notes neither deny nor emphasize the continuation of the miraculous gifts. |
| Women's ordination | Not confessionally fixed; the notes generally present the complementarian and egalitarian readings of the relevant passages (1 Timothy 2, 1 Corinthians 11 and 14) without ruling. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture is the final authority and historic creeds are respected but not treated as a second source; the notes do not appeal to church tradition to settle readings. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and orthodox on the deity and humanity of Christ throughout the notes on the relevant passages (John 1, Philippians 2, Colossians 1). | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Predestination and free will | Not confessionally fixed; the notes on Romans 9 and Ephesians 1 generally present both the Reformed and the Arminian reading rather than ruling between them. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification; the notes do not teach a distinct second work of grace. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment is assumed in the notes on Matthew 25:46 and Revelation 20; annihilationism is not presented as an option. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary is honored but not venerated, Jesus' "brothers" (Matthew 12:46) are read as literal siblings, and the notes give no basis for invoking saints or praying for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday worship assumed; the notes on Colossians 2:16 and Romans 14:5 treat the Sabbath command as fulfilled in Christ rather than binding, and no liturgical style is commended. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Church and state / political engagement | The notes on Romans 13 and 1 Peter 2 teach submission to and prayer for governing authorities without advocating a particular political program. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The notes on Acts 10, Galatians 3:28 and Ephesians 2 affirm the unity of all peoples in Christ; racial questions are not otherwise treated as a doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Creation and the age of the earth | The Genesis 1 notes and infographics present the literal-day and figurative readings without ruling; the volume defends the historicity of Genesis 1–11 against critical objections. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1 (general pattern; wording not independently verified) |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | The notes treat the tithe as Israel's law and New Testament giving as generous and proportionate (2 Corinthians 8–9) without mandating a tenth; prosperity teaching is not present. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the New Living Translation; the preface commends a committee translation from the original languages. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | The notes on Ephesians 5 present the husband's headship as loving, sacrificial leadership and the wife's submission within mutual submission (5:21); complementarian in tone without pressing the question. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
Consistent with broad evangelical Protestantism generally; specific verse-note wording on abortion, homosexuality, or alcohol has not been independently confirmed.

| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Opposed; the notes on Psalm 139 and Exodus 21 reflect the uniform evangelical view that human life bears God's image from conception. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Same-sex sexual conduct treated as sinful in the notes on Romans 1 and 1 Corinthians 6, with pastoral care distinguished from approval. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | Moderate use not treated as sinful; drunkenness is. The notes do not counsel abstinence. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

## 11. Reformation Study Bible

**Translation used:** English Standard Version (current edition; earlier editions used the New King James Version)
**Publisher:** Reformation Trust Publishing (the publishing arm of Ligonier Ministries)

### General editor
**R.C. Sproul** (1939–2017) — B.A., Westminster College; Ph.D., Free University of Amsterdam. Founder of Ligonier Ministries (1971) and a leading 20th-century popularizer of Reformed theology in America, known for his teaching ministry (*Renewing Your Mind* radio broadcast) and his book *The Holiness of God*. Sproul died in 2017; subsequent editions have been overseen by Ligonier's continuing editorial team.

### Denominational leaning
Explicitly and confessionally Reformed/Calvinist — reflects confessional covenant theology (in contrast to MacArthur's Calvinist-but-dispensationalist combination), closest among Denominations entries to the Presbyterian Church in America's theological orientation, though Sproul himself and Ligonier Ministries are not formally part of the PCA.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed explicitly, in the terms of the Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy (1978). |
| Salvation | Reformed/Calvinist: unconditional election, effectual grace, and perseverance of the saints, stated explicitly in the notes on Romans 9, Ephesians 1, and John 6. |
| Baptism | Covenantal infant baptism (paedobaptism) is the editors' confessional position, though the notes acknowledge the Baptist reading. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Amillennial or historic premillennial; the notes read Revelation 20 within covenant theology and reject dispensational distinctions between Israel and the Church. |
| Spiritual gifts | Mildly cautious; the notes do not affirm the continuation of the sign gifts as normative. |
| Women's ordination | Complementarian; the notes read 1 Timothy 2:12 as reserving the office of pastor/elder to men. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Consistent with confessional Reformed evangelicalism generally: opposed; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Consistent with confessional Reformed evangelicalism generally: considered sinful; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | No prohibition; drunkenness alone condemned, consistent with confessional Reformed tradition generally. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone is the final authority; the Reformed confessions (Westminster, Belgic, Heidelberg) are cited in the theological notes as faithful subordinate summaries. | Volume's theological notes and articles |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and Chalcedonian, set out in theological notes on the doctrine of God and the person of Christ. | Volume's theological notes |
| Predestination and free will | Confessionally Reformed: unconditional election and reprobation (Romans 9; Ephesians 1), total depravity and the bondage of the will, taught explicitly in the notes and theological articles. | Volume's notes and theological articles; editor's own published works (Sproul, *Chosen by God*) |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification as the fruit of justification, imperfect in this life; perfectionism rejected. | Volume's theological notes |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment, taught in the notes on Matthew 25 and Revelation 20 and in the theological notes on hell; annihilationism rejected. | Volume's notes and theological notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: invocation of Mary and the saints and prayer for the dead are rejected, consistent with the Westminster Standards the notes follow. | Volume's theological notes (Reformed confessional position) |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday as the Christian Sabbath, in Westminster's terms; the Regulative Principle of worship is commended in the theological notes. | Volume's theological notes; editor's own published works (Sproul) |
| Church and state / political engagement | Romans 13 read as establishing legitimate civil authority under God; Christians are to engage public life as citizens, and the editor's own writings opposed abortion and defended religious liberty. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (Sproul) |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity of humanity in Adam and of the church in Christ affirmed (Acts 17:26; Galatians 3:28); racial questions are not treated as a separate doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Creation and the age of the earth | The notes on Genesis 1 present the literal-day and other confessionally permitted readings; the editor publicly held a six-day view later in life while treating the age of the earth as a secondary matter. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1; editor's own public statements |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | The tithe is treated as Old Testament law and Christian giving as proportionate and generous; prosperity teaching is rejected. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the ESV (earlier editions on the NKJV and, as the *New Geneva Study Bible*, the NKJV); the preface commends formal-equivalence translation. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Complementarian: male headship in the home and male-only ordained office, in line with the Westminster tradition the notes follow. | Volume's notes on Ephesians 5 and 1 Timothy 2; Reformed confessional position |

## 12. Ryrie Study Bible

**Translation used:** Available in KJV, NASB, and NIV editions.
**Publisher:** Moody Publishers, first published 1976 (revised 1994, 1995).

### General editor
**Charles C. Ryrie** (1925–2016) — Th.D., Dallas Theological Seminary; Ph.D., University of Edinburgh. Longtime professor of systematic theology at Dallas Theological Seminary, widely regarded as one of the most influential dispensationalist theologians of the 20th century.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical, explicitly dispensationalist in eschatology — the Ryrie Study Bible is one of the two most historically significant dispensationalist study Bibles alongside the Scofield Reference Bible (entry 5 above), and shares its general theological framework with the MacArthur Study Bible's own dispensationalist orientation, though Ryrie's own moderate ("normative") dispensationalism differs in some particulars from other dispensationalist schools.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Verbal, plenary inspiration and inerrancy affirmed explicitly. |
| Salvation | Broadly evangelical with a free-grace, faith-alone emphasis; eternal security affirmed, but not the full Calvinist scheme. |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism assumed; not emphasized in the notes. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Dispensational premillennialism: the notes divide history into dispensations, distinguish Israel from the Church, and expect a pretribulational rapture followed by a literal millennium. |
| Spiritual gifts | Cessationist by tradition; the notes treat tongues and prophecy as belonging to the apostolic age. |
| Women's ordination | Complementarian by tradition; the notes assume male pastoral leadership. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Consistent with conservative evangelical Protestantism; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Consistent with conservative evangelical Protestantism; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | Consistent with conservative evangelical Protestantism; specific verse-note wording not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone, read dispensationally; the notes and the appended *Synopsis of Bible Doctrine* give tradition no authority. | Volume's notes and *Synopsis of Bible Doctrine* |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and Chalcedonian, set out in the *Synopsis of Bible Doctrine*. | Volume's *Synopsis of Bible Doctrine* |
| Predestination and free will | Moderately Calvinist (election affirmed, limited atonement denied) with firm eternal security; the editor's "free grace" position opposes making ongoing obedience a condition of assurance. | Volume's notes and *Synopsis*; editor's own published works (Ryrie, *So Great Salvation*) |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification; the editor argued against both perfectionism and "lordship salvation," holding that a believer may be carnal yet saved. | Volume's notes; editor's own published works (Ryrie, *Balancing the Christian Life*) |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment, taught in the notes and the *Synopsis*. | Volume's *Synopsis of Bible Doctrine* |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: no veneration of Mary or the saints and no prayer for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday as the Lord's Day; the Sabbath is treated as part of the Mosaic economy no longer binding (notes at Colossians 2:16); worship style not addressed. | Volume's notes |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission to government (Romans 13); the church's calling is spiritual rather than political, consistent with dispensational expectations of cultural decline. | Volume's notes |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes affirm the unity of believers in Christ. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Creation and the age of the earth | The Genesis notes favor a recent creation in six literal days while noting that the text does not date the earth; the editor's own works defend a young earth. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1; editor's own published works (Ryrie) |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | The tithe belongs to the law; Christian giving is grace giving, proportionate and voluntary. | Volume's notes |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Published in the KJV, NASB, NIV and ESV; the notes make no claim for any one translation. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Traditional male headship in home and church assumed in the notes on Ephesians 5 and 1 Timothy 2. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

## 13. Ignatius Catholic Study Bible

**Translation used:** Revised Standard Version, Second Catholic Edition (RSV2CE)
**Publisher:** Ignatius Press
**Format:** Originally released in individual-book volumes beginning in the late 1990s (New Testament complete in one volume, 2010); the complete Old and New Testament edition, with introductions, essays, commentary and notes by Scott Hahn and Curtis Mitch, was published by Ignatius Press in 2024 (RSV2CE text, revised "according to *Liturgiam Authenticam* 2001"; imprimatur for the notes, Archbishop Salvatore Cordileone, 8 May 2024).

### General editor
**Scott W. Hahn** (b. 1957) — B.A. in Theology, Philosophy, and Economics, Grove City College (1979); M.Div., Gordon-Conwell Theological Seminary (1982); Ph.D. in Biblical Theology, Marquette University (1995). A former Presbyterian minister (ordained 1982, Trinity Presbyterian Church, Fairfax, Virginia) with ten years of pastoral ministry in several Protestant congregations, Hahn entered the Catholic Church at the Easter Vigil in 1986. Since 1990 he has held the Father Michael Scanlan, T.O.R., Chair of Biblical Theology and the New Evangelization at Franciscan University of Steubenville, and is founder and president of the St. Paul Center for Biblical Theology. Best known outside academic circles for his conversion memoir *Rome Sweet Home* (co-authored with his wife, Kimberly Hahn) and *The Lamb's Supper*.

**Co-editor: Curtis J. Mitch**, M.A. — a former student of Hahn's, principal annotator and co-editor of the series; research fellow at the St. Paul Center for Biblical Theology.

### Denominational leaning
Explicitly and exclusively Catholic — the volume states its notes follow "the Church's guidelines for biblical interpretation" and draw on the Church Fathers, Doctors, and Magisterial teaching throughout. See the Catholic Church entry in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations" for the fuller doctrinal and ethical framework this volume operates within; this study Bible does not depart from official Magisterial teaching on any of the categories below.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inspired and inerrant: "Inerrancy is our guarantee that the words and deeds of God found in the Bible are unified and true," though "the Bible is not, for example, a textbook in the empirical sciences" (Introduction); at 2 Timothy 3:16 the notes defend "All Scripture is inspired" against the rendering "all inspired Scripture," since "neither Paul nor any other theologian in the early Church" allowed that some Scripture might not be inspired, while 3:17 denies that Scripture "supplies us with comprehensive instruction in all matters of Christian doctrine, worship, and ecclesial government." Read within Tradition and the Magisterium, following the essay *The Catholic Church and Sacred Scripture* (Leo XIII to *Dei Verbum* 11). |
| Salvation | Grace and faith working through love, with Baptism and the sacraments as means: faith "leads to justification because it leads to Baptism" (Romans 3:28 note), and "Catholic theology holds that faith does not act alone in this process but reaches out with hope for divine mercy and love for the Lord"; the Council of Trent (1547) is cited that man "can never merit the initial grace of justification," which is "conferred in Baptism"; the note sends the reader to the essay *Faith and Works* at James 2, where "faith not accompanied by works is a dead faith." Neither Calvinist nor Arminian categories apply. |
| Baptism | Regenerative, salvific and for infants: Baptism is "the sacrament that takes away sin and confers the Spirit," "the instrumental cause of our justification" (Acts 2:38 note, citing Trent), "not only a sign of forgiveness and renewal, but an instrument of grace that actually regenerates" (1 Peter 3:21), and "the benefits of Baptism are available to adults and children alike," which "explains why the apostles baptized entire households" (Acts 2:39). Mode is not discussed in the notes read. |
| The Lord's Supper | Real presence and sacrifice: at John 6:53 "Jesus is speaking literally and sacramentally," demanding that "we express our faith by eating, in a real and physical way, his life-giving flesh in the sacrament" (6:54); the Eucharist is "a sacrament, not of Christ's absence, but of his real and holy presence" and "the separate Consecration of bread and wine is a visible representation of Christ's death" (1 Corinthians 11:26); "Christ is entirely present under each form of the Sacrament" (11:27), and unworthy reception "in a state of serious (mortal) sin" is sacrilege. The word "transubstantiation" appears in the notes at John 2:10 (Cana "anticipates the transubstantiation of wine into blood"). |
| Church government | Episcopal and hierarchical: the bishop is "an overseer or spiritual shepherd who carries on the mission of the original apostles," the titles bishop and elder being "somewhat fluid in the earliest years" before "the distinction between bishops, elders, and deacons was clearly defined," and "the Church recognizes these offices as three degrees of the Sacrament of Holy Orders" (1 Timothy 3:1); the Church is "the pillar and bulwark" because the Spirit enables "the successors of the apostles to preserve the apostolic faith from corruption" (3:15); Titus "is already a bishop and thus qualified to ordain others" (Titus 1:5). At Matthew 16:19 the keys are "a symbol of teaching authority," and "Jesus consecrates Peter as the Church's chief teacher, whose office will continue on through successors," on the model of the Davidic prime minister of Isaiah 22. |
| Eschatology | Amillennial: the Revelation 20:1–6 note sets out premillennialism ("chiliasm"), postmillennialism and amillennialism, calls the last "probably the most widely held view among Catholic theologians throughout history (beginning with St. Augustine)," in which the millennium is "the entire stretch of history between the First and Second Coming, that time when Christ reigns in a spiritual and sacramental way through the Church," and adds that "the Catholic Church rejects all forms of millenarianism" (Decree of the Holy Office, 1944; CCC 676); the first resurrection may be spiritual, through Baptism (20:5). |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuationist: charisms are "graces given to build up the Church" (1 Corinthians 12:4) that "will expire when 'the perfect comes' (13:10), that is, when the Lord comes again in glory"; "interpretations that link the cessation of charismatic grace with the compilation of the NT books have absolutely no footing in the text" (13:8). Prophecy is "the preferred charismatic gift" (14:1), and tongues "may be the earthly languages of men (Acts 2:1-6) or the heavenly speech of angels" (12:10). |
| Women's ordination | Reserved to men: 1 Timothy 2:12 is "not an absolute prohibition" but "excludes women from the teaching ministry exercised by ordained clergymen," and "according to Church teaching, Paul forbids women to exercise the official function of teaching in the Christian assembly" (*Inter insigniores*, 1976); 1 Corinthians 14:34–35 prohibits "instructing the congregation in the official capacity of a pastor or homilist" while women "can lawfully pray and prophesy in the liturgical assembly"; Phoebe may be a deaconess, but "according to the Council of Nicaea … deaconesses are counted among the laity, not among the ordained clergy" (Romans 16:1). |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Opposed from conception: "Catholic tradition infers from this passage that unborn human life is willed by God and thus endowed with the right to life from the moment of conception" (Jeremiah 1:5, citing CCC 2270); the Psalm 139:13–16 notes read the womb imagery of God's knowledge of each person, and Exodus 21:22 carries only a note on "the judges." | Volume's notes at Jeremiah 1:5 and Psalm 139:13–16 |
| Homosexuality | Acts condemned, inclination distinguished: "Homosexual activity is expressly condemned in the OT … as well as the NT. It is a grave disorder that victimizes both men and women" (Romans 1:27); sex between males "is objectively disordered; hence, sins of this sort are particularly grave" (Leviticus 18:22); at 1 Corinthians 6:9 the two Greek terms "refer to the passive … and active partners … in acts of sodomy," and the RSV2CE footnote adds that "the apostle condemns, not the inherent tendencies of such, but the indulgence of them" (CCC 2357–59 cited). | Volume's notes at Romans 1:27, Leviticus 18:22, 1 Corinthians 6:9 |
| Alcohol | Moderate use accepted, drunkenness sinful: "Paul advocates temperance and not strict abstinence from alcohol" (Ephesians 5:18, citing 1 Timothy 5:23 and CCC 1809); Timothy "is advised to consume wine in moderation" (1 Timothy 5:23); "excessive consumption of alcohol leads to all manner of foolish behavior" (Proverbs 20:1); the wine of Cana anticipates the Eucharist (John 2:10). | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:18, 1 Timothy 5:23, Proverbs 20:1, John 2:10 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Indissoluble: "since God forges the indissoluble bond that unites a married couple, it follows that no civil or religious authority has the power to break it" (Matthew 19:6); "Jesus invokes his own authority to forbid divorce and remarriage" (19:9, with an essay *Jesus on Marriage and Divorce*); 1 Corinthians 7:12–16 is read as the Pauline privilege, by which a marriage between two unbaptized persons "can be dissolved when one of the spouses is subsequently baptized" (CIC 1143). | Volume's notes at Matthew 19:6, 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:12–16 |
| Contraception | Condemned: at Genesis 38:9 "Onan commits grave sin when he withdraws from sexual intercourse just before insemination," a sin "on a par with sexual sins such as adultery, homosexual activity, and bestiality," and "in Catholic moral theology, the sin of 'Onanism' covers contraceptive intercourse (including but not restricted to interrupted intercourse) as well as masturbation (CCC 2352, 2370)." | Volume's note at Genesis 38:8–10 |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the notes at Proverbs 16:33 and Acts 1:26 treat the casting of lots as "not a game of chance but a venerated means of discerning God's will," and 1 Timothy 6:10 warns against "lovers of wealth"; the Catechism's position (CCC 2413) is not stated in the notes read. | Volume's notes at Proverbs 16:33, Acts 1:26, 1 Timothy 6:10 (none on gambling) |
| Dancing | Not addressed: the only Matthew 14 note on the episode (14:9) concerns Herod's "illicit oath," not the dance; no position taken. | Volume's notes at Matthew 14 (none on dancing) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Gravely sinful: Hebrews 13:4 "prohibits every form of sexual immorality inside (e.g., adultery) and outside (e.g., fornication) the boundaries of lawful wedlock," a standard that "applies to the married and unmarried alike"; the believer's body is "consecrated to Christ through Baptism," so that promiscuity is "equivalent to adultery" (1 Corinthians 6:13) and "unchastity is an outrage against God" (1 Thessalonians 4:8). | Volume's notes at Hebrews 13:4, 1 Corinthians 6:12–20, 1 Thessalonians 4:3–8 |
| War/pacifism | Not pacifist; the state's force accepted within limits: at Romans 13:4 "Paul accepts that such extreme measures are acceptable if done at the service of justice," and the State "obliges the obedience of the Christian only when it legislates in accord with divine law" (13:1); at Matthew 5:38 Jesus "eliminates such a policy of retaliation from personal life," the law of retaliation having been "meant to limit retribution." Just-war doctrine as such is not set out in the notes read; Luke 3:14 carries no note. | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–4 and Matthew 5:38–41 |
| Death penalty | Permitted in principle, now opposed in practice: the Romans 13:4 note says "the Church, based on Scripture and Tradition, has historically recognized the right of civil governments to resort to capital punishment under certain conditions," but "more recently, the Church, witnessing to the gospel of mercy, has called for alternatives to capital punishment and has worked for its abolition (CCC 2266–67)"; Genesis 9:6 holds that "homicide thus merits the stiffest penalty: capital punishment," the Lord delegating "to men … the judicial authority to avenge wrongful deaths"; at John 8:7 Jesus "is not addressing the status or legality of the death penalty at all." | Volume's notes at Romans 13:4, Genesis 9:6, John 8:7 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Both: at 2 Thessalonians 2:15 "the traditions" are Paul's teaching "whether in writing or by oral instruction," and Chrysostom is quoted that "both written and unwritten are worthy of belief"; at 2 Timothy 3:17 Paul "also acknowledges the authority of apostolic tradition … and sees the Church … as the bearer of God's truth to the world," and at John 14:26 the Spirit is "sent from heaven to complete the teaching ministry of Jesus." The Introduction says the Church's dogmas "are not something added to Scripture; rather, they are the Church's infallible interpretation of Scripture." | Volume's notes at 2 Thessalonians 2:15 and 2 Timothy 3:15–17; Introduction |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Nicene and Chalcedonian: at John 1:1 "God the Son was present with God the Father before time itself began"; at 1:14 Christ is "fully divine, eternal, and equal in being with the Father" who "entered history as a man"; at 1:18 the variant "God, the only begotten" "directly asserts the deity of Jesus"; the Genesis 1:26 note sees in "Let us" "a hint that God himself is a communion of Divine Persons." The Comma Johanneum is given in a footnote as the Vulgate reading (1 John 5:7–8). | Volume's notes at John 1:1, 1:14, 1:18; Genesis 1:26; 1 John 5:8 |
| Predestination and free will | No predestination to damnation: "Esau I hated" "neither asserts nor implies that God predestined him for punishment or damnation" (Romans 9:13); Paul "is defending God's freedom to be patient and merciful toward sinners, not his freedom to punish" (9:14–18); the vessels of wrath "have prepared themselves for doom by rejecting the gospel," and "Paul is not saying that God has predestined the unbelievers of Israel for damnation" (9:22, citing CCC 1037). | Volume's notes at Romans 9:11–22 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive and beginning in Baptism: "growth in holiness, or progressive sanctification, is a process that begins with God's work in Baptism … and continues when believers … exert the moral effort needed to overcome sinful and selfish habits" (1 Thessalonians 4:3); "believers will continue to struggle with sin throughout their lives," so there is "an ongoing need for confession and forgiveness" (Romans 7:25); the prize of salvation "has yet to be won since the competition is still in progress" (Philippians 3:14). Purgatory is treated at 2 Maccabees 12. | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3, Romans 7:25, Philippians 3:14 |
