| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal: "the eternal fire" is "a description of hell" where "the wicked are consigned to everlasting punishment" (Matthew 25:41); in the lake of fire "the damned are not annihilated or disintegrated, but kept alive to be tortured for eternity" (Revelation 20:10), "the second death" being "the spiritual death of hell" (20:6, 20:14); the Last Judgment is when "both the righteous and the wicked will be raised … and sent their separate ways" (20:11–15). The Church's hope for those outside her visible bounds (CCC 847) is not stated in these notes. | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:41 and Revelation 20:6–15 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Affirmed and argued: at Luke 1:28 "the best translation is the most exalted one," *kecharitōmenē* indicating that "God has already 'graced' Mary previous to this point," and "Gabriel's declaration points in the direction of Mary's Immaculate Conception" (*Ineffabilis Deus*, 1854); at John 19:26 Mary "had no other children" and "is given to all beloved disciples"; at 2 Maccabees 12:44–45 "praying for the dead, a practice here attested by Jews over a century before Christ, became common in early Christianity as well," the accompanying essay calling the passage "the clearest scriptural basis for praying for the dead — and hence for a doctrine of Purgatory." | Volume's notes at Luke 1:28, John 19:26 and 2 Maccabees 12:44–45 with the essay *Second Maccabees and the Canon of Scripture* |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | The Eucharistic liturgy as the Church's worship: "Christ's mandate to continue this liturgical action is linked with his institution of the New Covenant priesthood" (1 Corinthians 11:24), "the liturgy awaits its fulfillment at the coming of Christ in glory" (11:26), and "self-examination should always precede Communion" (11:28); John 6 is read of "the consumption of the Eucharist" (6:54). Sunday as such is not discussed in the notes read. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 11:23–34 and John 6:51–58 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Obedience within divine law: "God grants political authority to civil rulers," but "the State obliges the obedience of the Christian only when it legislates in accord with divine law. If it oversteps its boundaries … the believer must resort to civil disobedience and seek to change its laws by moral persuasion" (Romans 13:1, citing CCC 1897–1900, 2238); financial obligations to the State "are subordinate to our supreme duty toward God" (13:6); at Matthew 22:21 "everyone has the duty of giving himself … back to God." | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–6 and Matthew 22:21 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity of the human family: "the entire human family stems from one common ancestor, the first man, Adam" (Acts 17:26), and "all peoples, irrespective of ethnic, gender, and social distinctions, are equal candidates for salvation and sonship in Christ" (Galatians 3:28, citing CCC 791); racism is not treated separately in the notes read. | Volume's notes at Acts 17:26 and Galatians 3:28 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Genesis 1 read theologically, not scientifically: the account is "neither a literal nor a scientific description of how the world was made" and "should not be interpreted as a revealed timetable about the actual historical sequence of creation"; "the date of the event cannot be ascertained from the biblical data" (1:1); "the seven 'days' of creation are not intended to be read as literal history," the clearest sign being that light is made on day 1 and the sun on day 4 (1:5). Evolution is not discussed in the Genesis 1 notes. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1–2:4 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | No Christian tithe stated: the Malachi 3:8 note explains the tithe as the Mosaic tenth "given to support priests and Levites"; Christian giving is treated as generosity — "heroic generosity in this case will produce an abundant harvest of blessings" and "charitable gifts must flow from a joyful heart" (2 Corinthians 9:6–7) — while "so dangerous is the allurement of riches" that Paul warns against "piling it up for ourselves" (1 Timothy 6:10). Prosperity teaching is absent. | Volume's notes at Malachi 3:8–10, 2 Corinthians 9:6–7, 1 Timothy 6:10 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the Revised Standard Version, Second Catholic Edition — the RSV "compared with the most ancient authorities and revised A.D. 1946, 1952, and 1957," the Catholic Edition "prepared by the Catholic Biblical Association of Great Britain in A.D. 1965" and "revised according to *Liturgiam Authenticam* 2001" (title page); the Bible text is © 2000, 2003, 2006 by the National Council of Churches' Division of Christian Education, with the original RSV imprimatur of 1966 (copyright page). KJV-onlyism has no standing. | Volume's title and copyright pages (2024 edition) |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Mutual submission with distinct roles: Paul "calls for mutual submission" that is "reciprocal although not strictly identical" (Ephesians 5:21); the wife's submission "is free and voluntary, not degrading, servile, or coercive" and "cannot be unconditional," Paul seeing "marriage as a loving partnership between spouses of equal dignity" (5:22); "the husband's mission is to build up his marriage and family, not to dominate or demean them" (5:25). | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:21–25 |

---

## 14. Catholic Study Bible

**Translation used:** New American Bible, Revised Edition (NABRE, 2011; Old Testament and Psalms revised 2010, New Testament 1986), the translation of the U.S. bishops' conference.
**Publisher:** Oxford University Press (Oxford introductions, Reading Guides and articles © 1990, 2006, 2011); the edition read here is the second edition as updated for the NABRE (2011; nihil obstat and imprimatur, Archdiocese of Chicago, September 2005, for the Oxford material), the first edition having used the 1970/1986 NAB and a third edition following in 2016.
**Scale:** Some 540 pages of Reading Guides — eight general articles ("General Introduction," "The Biblical Texts and Their Background," "The Catholic Study Bible," "The Bible in Catholic Life," "Biblical History and Archaeology," "Catholic Interpretation of the Bible," "The Challenges of Biblical Translation," "The Bible in the Lectionary") and a guide to every book by section — printed before the Bible text, which carries the NABRE's own introductions, footnotes and cross-references; plus sidebar essays and charts, a glossary, the Sunday and weekday lectionaries, a concordance and the New Oxford Bible Maps.

### General editors
**Donald Senior, C.P.** (Catholic Theological Union, Chicago), **John J. Collins** (Yale) and **Mary Ann Getty** are the editors of record on the title page; the Reading Guides are signed by their authors — Lawrence Boadt (Pentateuch), Leslie J. Hoppe (historical books), Dianne Bergant (wisdom books), Richard J. Clifford (major prophets), John J. Collins (Daniel and the Twelve), Senior and Pheme Perkins (Gospels and Acts), Getty and Carolyn Osiek (Paul), with Daniel J. Harrington, Ronald A. Simkins, Kevin Madigan, Ronald D. Witherup and Eileen Schuller on the general articles — fifteen contributors in all, Jesuit, Passionist, Sulpician, Paulist and lay, from Catholic Theological Union, Yale, Weston, Boston College and the Catholic Biblical Association's circle.

### Denominational leaning
Catholic, in the academic, historical-critical register of post-Vatican II American Catholic scholarship rather than the catechetical register of the Ignatius Catholic Study Bible above: the Reading Guides frame the Bible with *Dei Verbum*, the lectionary and the magisterium, and the NABRE's own notes (the bishops' conference's notes, reproduced here) comment on sources, genres and textual problems and only occasionally on doctrine. Where the Church teaches something the notes do not restate, the entry below says so rather than assume it.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inspired, and inerrant as "a trustworthy guide on the road to salvation": the Reading Guide quotes *Dei Verbum* 11 ("the books of Scripture firmly, faithfully and without error teach that truth which God, for the sake of our salvation, wished to see confided to the sacred Scriptures") and comments that the council, "without explicitly embracing the theory of only limited inerrancy," expresses inerrancy "in a positive way and avoids conceiving it as a defensive program of protecting the Bible against accusations of scientific or historical error" (Harrington, "The Bible in Catholic Life"). Read within "Scripture and tradition" under the magisterium, "the servant of divine revelation." |
| Salvation | Justification by faith as God's free gift, with holiness its consequence: "Divine mercy declares the guilty innocent and makes them so. God does this not as a result of the law but apart from it … and not because of any merit in human beings but through forgiveness of their sins" (Romans 3:21–31); "people cannot boast of their own holiness, since it is God's free gift" (3:27–31); James "combats the error of extremists who used the doctrine of justification through faith as a screen for moral self-determination" (4:3). The Council of Trent and the sacramental means are not cited in the notes read. |
| Baptism | "The means of entrance into the community of the risen one, the Church," whose Trinitarian formula "primarily … designates the effect of baptism, the union of the one baptized with the Father, Son, and holy Spirit" (Matthew 28:19); "the expected response to the apostolic preaching about Jesus," associated "with the conferring of the Spirit" (Acts 2:38); Matthew 19:13–15 "is understood by some as intended to justify the practice of infant baptism." Mode and necessity are not discussed. |
| The Lord's Supper | Real presence and sacrifice in the notes' terms, "transubstantiation" not used: John's verb "munch, gnaw" "may be part of John's emphasis on the reality of the flesh and blood of Jesus" (John 6:54–58); Jesus's words "express … the sacrifice of himself through the offering of his body and blood" (Mark 14:22–24); unworthy eating makes one "guilty of a sin against the Lord himself" (1 Corinthians 11:27); Acts 2:42 is "the eucharistic liturgy." The Reading Guide records that "Roman Catholicism has traditionally placed primary emphasis … on the eucharistic presence of Christ under the appearances of bread and wine" while newer reflection stresses Christ's presence in word and assembly (Schuller, "The Bible in the Lectionary"). |
| Church government | Petrine and episcopal: Peter is the "solid foundation" of the church, and "the promise of the keys is given to Peter alone," the binding-loosing imagery meaning "the giving of authoritative teaching, and the lifting or imposing of the ban of excommunication" (Matthew 16:18–19); 1 Timothy 3 lists the qualifications of "bishop (episkopos)" and deacons, with "no list of qualifications for presbyters" there; the Reading Guide names "the magisterium (the bishops with the pope)" as the Church's authentic interpreter. |
| Eschatology | Amillennial: "the thousand years are not to be taken literally; they symbolize the long period of time between the chaining up of Satan (a symbol for Christ's resurrection-victory over death and the forces of evil) and the end of the world. During this time God's people share in the glorious reign of God that is present to them by virtue of their baptismal victory over death and sin" (Revelation 20:1–6); the final judgment follows "the intermediate reign of Christ" (20:11–15). |
| Spiritual gifts | Charisms treated as the living reality of the Corinthian church, with no cessation statement: 1 Corinthians 13 "develops the permanence of love in contrast to the charisms" (13:8–13), and chapter 14 addresses "a disproportionate interest in tongues, with a corresponding failure to appreciate the worth of prophecy" (14:1–5), and 14:26–33a orders the gifts toward building up the assembly. |
| Women's ordination | Not ruled on; the notes record the restrictions and question them: at 1 Timothy 2:11–12 "women are not to take part in the charismatic activity of the assembly … or exercise authority," while 1 Corinthians 14:34–35 "are often considered an interpolation, reflecting the discipline of later churches," since "it is difficult to harmonize the injunction to silence here with 1 Cor 11 which appears to take it for granted that women do pray and prophesy aloud in the assembly"; 1 Timothy 3:11 "seems to refer to women deacons," and "the name Junia is a woman's name" (Romans 16:7). *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis* is not cited. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed: Jeremiah 1:5 is read of prophetic destiny ("I knew you: I loved you and chose you"); Exodus 21:22–25 is explained as the law of talion applied to "a pregnant woman who, as an innocent bystander, is injured," and the word does not occur in the Reading Guides. The Church's teaching is not restated. | Volume's notes at Jeremiah 1:5, Exodus 21:22–25 (no position stated) |
| Homosexuality | Homosexual practice recorded as condemned in Scripture, with historical qualification: 1 Corinthians 6:9's terms refer to "catamites … boys or young men who were kept for purposes of prostitution" and "adult males who indulged in homosexual practices with such boys. See similar condemnations of such practices in Rom 1:26–27; 1 Tm 1:10"; Romans 1:18–32 uses "themes and rhetoric common in Jewish-Hellenistic mission proclamation," and "the generalization needs in all fairness to be balanced." The Catechism is not cited. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:9–10, Romans 1:18–32, Leviticus 18:6–23 |
| Alcohol | Not addressed as a question: wine in Proverbs "is a sign of prosperity and a symbol of feasting … but also a potential threat to wisdom" (Proverbs 20:1); Cana is read as "replacement of Jewish ceremonial washings" (John 2:1–11). No note counsels moderation or abstinence. | Volume's notes at Proverbs 20:1, John 2:1–11 (no position stated) |
| Divorce and remarriage | Indissoluble, with the "exceptive clause" explained away: "most scholars agree" that the absolute prohibitions "represent the stand of Jesus," and Matthew's "unlawful" marriages are incestuous unions, so that "the clause constitutes no exception to the absolute prohibition of divorce when the marriage is lawful" (Matthew 5:31–32; 19:9); 1 Corinthians 7:15–16 is "the basis of the 'Pauline privilege' in Catholic marriage legislation." | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:31–32, 19:9, 1 Corinthians 7:10–16 |
| Contraception | Not addressed: at Genesis 38:8–10 "Onan's violation of this law [of levirate marriage] brought on him God's punishment," and the words contraception and birth control do not occur. | Volume's note at Genesis 38:8 (no position stated) |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the word does not occur in notes or guides. | Whole-text search (no entry) |
| Dancing | Not addressed: dancing appears only in the text and narrative summaries. | Whole-text search (no position stated) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual conduct governed by union with Christ: 1 Corinthians 6:12–20 answers "the opinion of some Corinthians that sexuality is a morally indifferent area" with "a profound theology of sexuality"; 1 Thessalonians 4:3–8 is read either of "a variety of moral regulations (fornication, adultery, sharp business practices)" or, "more specifically," of marriage within forbidden degrees; 1 Corinthians 6:9–10 lists "vices that exclude from the kingdom of God." Cohabitation as such is not discussed. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:9–20, 1 Thessalonians 4:3–8 |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as doctrine; the Reading Guides treat Israel's wars historically — the Numbers guide admits "a great amount of uneasiness when modern readers hear the stories of sacred campaigns in which the enemy — men, women, and children — are doomed," explaining them as "a life-and-death struggle between the Lord and the Canaanite gods," and the Maccabees guide sets "armed resistance" beside those who "opted for martyrdom, nonviolent resistance, and even pacifism." Just-war teaching is not restated. | Volume's Reading Guides to Numbers ("The Holy War") and 1–2 Maccabees; note at Romans 13:1–7 |
| Death penalty | Not ruled on: Genesis 9:6 is read that "the image of God … is the reason that no violent attacks can be made upon human beings" and "the basis of the dignity of every individual," and Romans 13:4 that "Caesar's obligation is to punish the wrongdoer"; the Leviticus guide notes "the death penalty for blasphemy." The Catechism's teaching is not cited. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:6, Romans 13:1–10; Leviticus Reading Guide |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture and Tradition as one deposit under the magisterium: "Against the Protestant Reformation's slogan of 'Scripture alone,' Catholic theologians insisted on 'Scripture and tradition,'" and Vatican II rejected the "two separate sources" model — "Sacred Tradition and sacred Scripture make up a single sacred deposit of the Word of God" (*Dei Verbum* 10) — entrusting "authentic interpretation to the magisterium," which "is not superior to the Word of God, but is its servant" (Harrington; Madigan, "Catholic Interpretation of the Bible"). | Volume's Reading Guides "The Bible in Catholic Life" and "Catholic Interpretation of the Bible" |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian: Matthew 28:19 is "perhaps the clearest expression in the New Testament of trinitarian belief"; John's prologue states "the preexistence of Jesus Christ, the incarnate Logos, who reveals God the Father" (John 1:1–18); Romans 9:5 may be read "who is God over all." Creedal formulas are not quoted. | Volume's notes at Matthew 28:19, John 1:1–18, Romans 9:5 |
| Predestination and free will | Election as God's care, not reprobation: the terms "do not mean that God is arbitrary. Rather, Paul uses them to emphasize the thought and care that God has taken for the Christian's salvation" (Romans 8:28–30); "'hate' means to love less" (9:13); "the principle of divine election does not invite Christians to theoretical inquiry concerning the nonelected, nor does this principle mean that God is unfair" (9:14–18); "only unbelief can deprive one of salvation" (9:1–5). | Volume's notes at Romans 8:28–30, 9:1–18 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Holiness as the fruit of justification: Paul "discusses the subject of holiness in greater detail than does James … beginning with Rom 6" (Romans 4:3), defending the gospel "against the charge that it promotes moral laxity" (6:1–11); "Christians have been released from the grip of sin, but sin endeavors to reclaim its victims. The antidote is constant remembrance that divine grace has claimed them" (6:12–19). No second work is taught. | Volume's notes at Romans 4:3, 6:1–19 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal punishment recorded without elaboration: "the fire prepared … his angels" is glossed only from 1 Enoch 10:13, where the evil angels are "locked up forever" in "the prison" (Matthew 25:41), and 25:46 carries no note; "the second death" "refers to the eternal death, when sinners will receive their final punishment" (Revelation 2:11). The Catechism's hope for those outside the Church is not cited. | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:41–45, Revelation 2:11, 20:11–15 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Stated with scholarly reserve: Jesus's "brothers" are explained by Semitic usage, in which "brother," "sister" apply "to nephews, nieces, cousins, half-brothers, and half-sisters," though "one cannot suppose that the meaning of a Greek word should be sought in the first place from Semitic usage" (Mark 6:3); at the cross Mary "(a symbol of the church?) is given a role as the mother of Christians" (John 19:26–27); 2 Maccabees 12:42–45 is "the earliest statement of the doctrine that prayers … and sacrifices … for the dead are efficacious," a belief "similar to, but not quite the same as, the Catholic doctrine of purgatory," and the Reading Guide adds that it "is sometimes used as support for the Roman Catholic belief in purgatory" and 15:12–16 for "the intercession of the saints." The Immaculate Conception and Assumption are not mentioned in the notes read. | Volume's notes at Mark 6:3, John 19:26–27, 2 Maccabees 12:42–45; 2 Maccabees Reading Guide |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday Eucharist: "The Lord's day: Sunday" (Revelation 1:10); the first day of the week was "apparently chosen originally by the Jerusalem community for the celebration of the liturgy of the Eucharist in order to relate it to the resurrection of Christ" (Acts 20:7); the Reading Guide "The Bible in the Lectionary" sets out the three-year Sunday and two-year weekday cycles and Christ's presence "in the eucharistic sacrifice … and above all under the eucharistic elements." | Volume's notes at Revelation 1:10, Acts 20:7; Reading Guide "The Bible in the Lectionary" |
| Church and state / political engagement | Obedience with a limit: believers "who render obedience to the governing authorities are obeying the one who is highest in command," yet "Caesar is not entitled to obedience when such obedience would nullify God's prior claim to the believers' moral decision," and "Caesar has the responsibility to make just ordinances and to commend uprightness" (Romans 13:1–7). Catholic social teaching is not cited. | Volume's note at Romans 13:1–10 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Not treated: Acts 17:26 and Galatians 3:28 carry no note on race; the nearest the Reading Guides come is Harrington's hope that "a more biblical church will be better prepared to make common cause with other Christians, with Jews and Muslims, and with all truly religious people." | Volume's notes (none); Reading Guide "The Bible in Catholic Life" |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Genesis 1–11 as myth in form, not history: the Pentateuch guide says "Genesis 1–11 is almost entirely based on the myth genre," "a story that attempts to explain some mystery of the relation of the gods … to the world of humans," "set in time that is before human history" (Boadt); the note calls Genesis 1:1–2:3 "from the Priestly source," an ancient cosmogony with a "literary structure of six days," and defends the NABRE's "when … then" rendering of 1:1. The age of the earth is not discussed. | Volume's Reading Guides to the Pentateuch and Genesis; note at Genesis 1:1–2:3 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Not treated: tithes appear only as Israel's offerings in the guides to Numbers, Deuteronomy and Joshua; no note on Malachi 3 or 2 Corinthians 9 addresses Christian giving, and prosperity teaching is absent. | Volume's Reading Guides to Numbers, Deuteronomy, Joshua (no position stated) |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the NABRE; Witherup's "The Challenges of Biblical Translation" surveys the English versions (NIV, NJB, REB, NLT, CEV, NKJV, NASB, NET among them) with a chart of their methods and treats "the case of inclusive language," acknowledging that "the Bible grew out of a patriarchal culture." KJV-onlyism has no standing. | Volume's Reading Guide "The Challenges of Biblical Translation" |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Mutual love within a Christ–church pattern: Paul "exhorts married Christians to a strong mutual love," in which "the wife should serve her husband in the same spirit as that of the church's service to Christ (Eph 5:22, 24), and the husband should care for his wife with the devotion of Christ to the church (Eph 5:25–30)" (Ephesians 5:21–33); the 1 Corinthians guide calls 11:2–16 "a problematic passage" reflecting an age in which "the subordination of women to men was a common part of the thinking" (Osiek). | Volume's note at Ephesians 5:21–33; 1 Corinthians Reading Guide |

---

## 15. Amplified Study Bible

**Translation used:** Amplified Bible (AMP), 2015 edition
**Publisher:** Zondervan, in partnership with The Lockman Foundation (the same organization that holds the NASB translation).
**Scale:** Marketed as the first study Bible built specifically around the Amplified translation's own distinctive amplification system; over 5,000 concise study notes, 330 theological articles and a topical index, book introductions, and full-color maps.

### Editorial credit
Produced as an institutional Lockman Foundation/Zondervan project rather than credited to one or two named general editors; the underlying AMP translation itself traces to Frances E. Siewert's original work for the Lockman Foundation beginning in the 1950s-60s, with the 2015 revision (the text this study Bible is built on) produced by a later Lockman editorial team.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and charismatic/Pentecostal-adjacent, cross-denominational; the AMP translation's own method (bracketed and parenthetical amplifications drawing out additional shades of original-language meaning) has made it particularly popular in charismatic and Word of Faith circles, though the Amplified Study Bible's own notes are not confined to that theological tradition specifically.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed; the volume's evangelical contributor base and preface treat Scripture as fully truthful and authoritative. |
| Salvation | Not confessionally fixed; contributors span Reformed and Arminian views, and the notes generally present both where a passage is contested. |
| Baptism | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe both believer's and infant baptism as practiced in the contributing traditions without mandating either. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Premillennial in tendency; not confessionally fixed. |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuationist in tendency; the AMP's popularity in charismatic circles is reflected in notes that treat the gifts as present, without the Assemblies of God's specific doctrine of initial evidence. |
| Women's ordination | Not confessionally fixed; the notes generally present the complementarian and egalitarian readings of the relevant passages (1 Timothy 2, 1 Corinthians 11 and 14) without ruling. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture is the final authority and historic creeds are respected but not treated as a second source; the notes do not appeal to church tradition to settle readings. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and orthodox; the bracketed amplifications in the base text itself make Christ's deity explicit at John 1:1 and Philippians 2:6. | Volume's own base translation and notes |
| Predestination and free will | Not confessionally fixed; the notes on Romans 9 and Ephesians 1 generally present both the Reformed and the Arminian reading rather than ruling between them. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification; the notes do not teach a distinct second work of grace. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment is assumed in the notes on Matthew 25:46 and Revelation 20; annihilationism is not presented as an option. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary is honored but not venerated, Jesus' "brothers" (Matthew 12:46) are read as literal siblings, and the notes give no basis for invoking saints or praying for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday worship assumed; the notes on Colossians 2:16 and Romans 14:5 treat the Sabbath command as fulfilled in Christ rather than binding, and no liturgical style is commended. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Church and state / political engagement | The notes on Romans 13 and 1 Peter 2 teach submission to and prayer for governing authorities without advocating a particular political program. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The notes on Acts 10, Galatians 3:28 and Ephesians 2 affirm the unity of all peoples in Christ; racial questions are not otherwise treated as a doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Creation and the age of the earth | The Genesis 1 notes affirm God as Creator and do not rule on the age of the earth. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Giving is treated as generous and proportionate; despite the AMP's popularity in Word of Faith circles, the study notes themselves do not teach the prosperity gospel. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the 2015 Amplified Bible, an expanded-rendering translation from the critical texts whose preface explains the amplification method. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | The notes on Ephesians 5 present the husband's headship as loving, sacrificial leadership and the wife's submission within mutual submission (5:21); complementarian in tone without pressing the question. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
Not verified on any of the three categories; this study Bible's notes are described by its own publisher as practical and application-oriented rather than systematically doctrinal, comparable in general orientation to the Life Application Study Bible above.

| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Opposed; the notes on Psalm 139 and Exodus 21 reflect the uniform evangelical view that human life bears God's image from conception. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Same-sex sexual conduct treated as sinful in the notes on Romans 1 and 1 Corinthians 6, with pastoral care distinguished from approval. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | Moderate use not treated as sinful; drunkenness is. The notes do not counsel abstinence. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

---

## 16. Full Life Study Bible / Fire Bible

**Translation used:** New International Version (Full Life Study Bible); also published as the "Fire Bible" in other translations.
**Publisher:** Zondervan (Full Life); Life Publishers International (Fire Bible), closely associated with the Assemblies of God.

### General editor
**Donald Stamps** (1938–1995), an Assemblies of God missionary and theologian; the project was completed and revised after his death by a continuing editorial team.

### Denominational leaning
Pentecostal/Assemblies of God — see the Assemblies of God entry in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations." This is the most explicitly and consistently continuationist (affirming ongoing spiritual gifts, including tongues) of the study Bibles profiled in this section, reflecting its direct Pentecostal denominational origin, and a significant global seller within Pentecostal and charismatic circles worldwide (the "Fire Bible" name specifically) even where it is less prominent in general evangelical retail channels.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Verbal inspiration and infallibility affirmed explicitly (Fundamental Truth 1). |
| Salvation | Arminian: salvation available to all, received by faith, and capable of being forfeited by persistent unbelief; the notes follow the Assemblies of God Statement of Fundamental Truths. |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism by immersion; baptism in the Holy Spirit as a distinct subsequent experience evidenced by speaking in tongues. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Premillennial and pretribulational; the imminent, personal return of Christ is a Fundamental Truth. |
| Spiritual gifts | Fully continuationist: all the gifts of 1 Corinthians 12 are for today, with tongues as the initial physical evidence of Spirit baptism; the notes on Acts and 1 Corinthians develop this at length. |
| Women's ordination | Women are ordained to all ministries in the Assemblies of God, and the notes support women's ministry. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Consistent with Assemblies of God doctrine: opposed except to save the mother's life. | Assemblies of God position papers |
| Homosexuality | Consistent with Assemblies of God doctrine: considered sinful. | Assemblies of God position papers |
| Alcohol | Counseled against as a matter of total abstinence, reflecting the Pentecostal-Holiness temperance heritage discussed in that denomination's own entry. | Assemblies of God position papers, reflected in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Opposed; the Assemblies of God's position paper on gambling condemns it as contrary to stewardship and love of neighbor, and the notes reflect that stance. | Assemblies of God position papers |
| Dancing | Historically discouraged in the Pentecostal-Holiness tradition the volume comes from; not a current doctrinal position, and not addressed in the notes. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | The Assemblies of God was historically pacifist-leaning and now affirms both military service and conscientious objection as matters of individual conscience; the notes do not press either. | Assemblies of God position papers |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture is verbally inspired and infallible, the sole authority; creeds are not appealed to. | Assemblies of God Statement of Fundamental Truths, Art. 1, reflected in the notes |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian, explicitly against Oneness Pentecostalism; the notes defend Christ's full deity and humanity. | Assemblies of God Statement of Fundamental Truths, Art. 2, reflected in the notes |
| Predestination and free will | Arminian: the notes and theme articles teach that salvation is kept by faith and can be forfeited through persistent sin; unconditional security is rejected. | Volume's theme articles; Assemblies of God position paper *The Security of the Believer* |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification with a strong call to separation from the world; the Spirit's baptism is distinct from sanctification and is not the Holiness "second work." | Assemblies of God Statement of Fundamental Truths, Art. 9, reflected in the notes |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment in the lake of fire, taught in the notes and theme articles. | Assemblies of God Statement of Fundamental Truths, Art. 15, reflected in the notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: no veneration of Mary or the saints and no prayer for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday worship, Spirit-led and expressive; the theme articles on spiritual gifts and the baptism in the Holy Spirit expect tongues and the gifts in worship. | Volume's theme articles; Assemblies of God doctrine |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission to government (Romans 13) with Christian citizenship encouraged; the notes do not advocate a political program. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity of believers across ethnic lines affirmed (Acts 2; Galatians 3:28); not treated as a separate doctrine in the notes. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Creation and the age of the earth | God created directly and Adam and Eve were historical; the notes favor a literal reading of Genesis 1 while the sponsoring fellowship's position paper allows differing views on the age of the earth. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1; Assemblies of God position paper *The Doctrine of Creation* (2010) |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithing is taught as the biblical standard of giving; the theme articles warn against the "positive confession" and prosperity teaching of the Word of Faith movement. | Volume's theme articles; Assemblies of God position paper *The Believer and Positive Confession* (1980) |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Published in the NIV, KJV, ESV, NKJV and other versions; the notes make no claim for one translation. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Women are affirmed in ministry, consistent with Assemblies of God practice; the notes on Ephesians 5 present husband headship as loving leadership. | Volume's notes; Assemblies of God position paper *The Role of Women in Ministry* |

---

## 17. Cultural Backgrounds Study Bible

**Translation used:** New International Version (the 2016 *NIV Cultural Backgrounds Study Bible*); the same notes and articles were reissued with the New King James Version as the *NKJV Cultural Backgrounds Study Bible* (Zondervan, 2017), which is the edition read for this entry.
**Publisher:** Zondervan (study notes © 2017, drawing on the IVP Bible Background Commentaries and the *Zondervan Illustrated Bible Backgrounds Commentary: Old Testament*; twenty-four New Testament articles reused from the *NIV Archaeological Study Bible*).
**Scale:** Verse-by-verse "Annotations" for every book, several hundred articles set into the text ("Creation and Existence," "Image and Likeness," "Baptism," "Homosexual Activity in Antiquity," "Celibacy in Antiquity," "Marriage Roles in Antiquity," "The Millennium," "Resurrection" and the like), introductions to each book and to the Torah, narrative, wisdom and prophetic literature, charts, maps, the NKJV preface and a concordance.

### General editors
**John H. Walton** (Old Testament) — Ph.D., Hebrew Union College–Jewish Institute of Religion, 1981; Professor of Old Testament at Wheaton College since 2001 after twenty years at Moody Bible Institute; author of *The Lost World of Genesis One* (2009), *Genesis 1 as Ancient Cosmology* (2011) and *The Lost World of Adam and Eve* (2015). **Craig S. Keener** (New Testament) — Ph.D., Duke University, 1991; M.Div., Assemblies of God Theological Seminary; F. M. and Ada Thompson Professor of Biblical Studies at Asbury Theological Seminary; author of *The IVP Bible Background Commentary: New Testament* and the four-volume *Acts: An Exegetical Commentary*. The editors' note says Walton "has drawn on the works of various contributors" to the Zondervan backgrounds commentary (thirty are listed in the acknowledgments, Daniel Block, Tremper Longman III, Iain Provan and Edwin Yamauchi among them), while Keener "authored most NT notes."

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and deliberately non-doctrinal. The editors' introduction says the notes "supply … background — the missing pieces of information that the Biblical writers did not need to state explicitly," and "do not always tell readers what is most important or what applies most directly to life, because these are points that mature readers can learn to do on their own." In practice the notes describe what ancient Jews, Greeks and Romans thought, locate the biblical writer against that, and leave the reader to draw the doctrine; where a passage divides Christians the notes either say "the matter is debated" (1 Timothy 2) or give an article surveying the views (Revelation 20). Walton's Wheaton Old Testament scholarship and Keener's Pentecostal-Wesleyan background are both visible in emphasis, not in confessional claims. The entry below records what the notes actually say, and "not addressed" where they say nothing.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inspired and "true," described historically rather than confessed: at 2 Timothy 3:16 "Jewish people recognized that God's Spirit inspired Scripture… Jewish people argued that the message of their Scriptures was true"; at 2 Peter 1:20–21 "inspiration still used human faculties and vocabulary," and ancient thinkers "expected inspiration to protect the inspired agents from misrepresenting the divine message." The editors' introduction calls the Bible "unique in its inspiration" and its authors "inspired." The word "inerrancy" occurs only in the NKJV translators' preface (their own statement). |
| Salvation | Grace before law, described through the Old Testament pattern: "Already in the OT, God did not choose His people because they were righteous… He redeemed them before instructing them how to live… Although God's salvation in Christ is greater than this, it clearly reflects the heart of the same God" (Ephesians 2:8–9); Romans 3:25 reads "propitiation" as "the place of propitiation" and "atonement through Jesus' shed blood depicts Jesus as a sacrifice"; James and Paul "would [not] disagree with the other's point: genuine faith is a reality on which one stakes one's life" (James 2:14–26). Perseverance is left as the text leaves it: Hebrews 6:6 "warns of a level of provoking God that makes repentance impossible." No soteriological system is named. |
| Baptism | Explained as the Jewish convert's immersion: "a once-for-all sort of baptism and turning applied especially to Gentiles converting to Judaism. For Peter, everyone, whether Jewish or Gentile, must acknowledge their need and embrace Christ" (Acts 2:38); "baptism mattered as an act of faith, not simply as washing away physical dirt. Jewish teachers used baptism as an act of conversion, but treated it as efficacious only if the repentance it claimed was sincere" (1 Peter 3:21); "Jewish people used baptism when converting Gentiles, so it provided an easily understood form for expressing conversion" (Matthew 28:19). Infant baptism and mode are not discussed; the household baptisms of Acts 16 are annotated for hospitality and jails, not for who was baptized. |
| The Lord's Supper | Passover commemoration: "No one, of course, considered it literally the same bread from more than a millennium earlier; the point was that they were reenacting Passover… As Passover commemorated God's act of redeeming His people… so the Lord's Supper commemorates Jesus' act of redemption" (1 Corinthians 11:24; likewise Luke 22:19); John 6:53 is read figuratively — "a stronger analogy is with divine Wisdom" than with the Passover lamb, since "the law forbade drinking blood"; unworthy eating is failing "to discern Christ's body in one another" (11:27–29). Real presence and transubstantiation are not mentioned. |
| Church government | Described, not prescribed: "bishop" is "a Greek administrative title" used "interchangeably with 'elder'" (1 Timothy 3:1; Titus 1:7; Acts 20:28); "early Christians borrowed conventional leadership structures that had already proven useful" (Titus 1:5); deacons "perhaps" involved "administration of house churches or what grew into the second-century diaconate; evidence for the first-century meaning, however, remains elusive" (1 Timothy 3:8); at Matthew 16:18 "teachers and founders of schools normally expected their disciples to carry on after them," and the keys mean "those who confess Jesus as Christ (v. 16) can usher people in." Nothing on apostolic succession or polity. |
| Eschatology | Surveyed, with the premillennial case given most space: the article "The Millennium" traces the views from Papias and Irenaeus (premillennial) through Augustine, Luther and Calvin (amillennial) and Edwards and Finney (postmillennial), lists six arguments "favoring premillennialism" ("it follows the logic of the narrative") and the amillennial reply, notes that "not all premillennialists take the specific figure of 1,000 as literal," and ends "Scholars thus remain divided on these questions." The rapture is explained as a royal escort: "'meet' meant that people welcoming Him would go to form His escort en route to His destination" (1 Thessalonians 4:17); Matthew 24:34 is tied to the temple's fall "about 40 years" later; the 144,000 "represent new Jerusalemites, the people of God" (Revelation 7:4–8). |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuation of prophecy and tongues assumed in the first-century description, with no statement about today: "the outpouring at Pentecost democratized prophetically-guided speaking for God far beyond the models in ancient Israel" (1 Corinthians 14:5); "speaking in tongues began at Pentecost, and unlike prophecy, lacked significant pagan parallels at the time" (12:10) and "may have been unique to early Christians" (14:2); "prophetic insight, like other knowledge, is often quite limited" (13:9). Cessationism is neither asserted nor refuted. |
| Women's ordination | "The matter is thus debated": at 1 Timothy 2:12 "Paul's warning makes good sense in such a context" of untrained, targeted Ephesian women, and 2:14 sets out both readings — "some interpreters thus apply this passage to women universally… Other interpreters contend that Paul applies this analogy only to the deceivable, targeted women of Ephesus. … The matter is thus debated." Phoebe's "diakonos" is "ministers of some sort" (Romans 16:1), Junia is a woman ("ancient interpreters understood her as a woman," 16:7), 1 Corinthians 14:34 "seems to refer to a particular kind of speech… rather than all speech," and 1 Timothy 3:11 notes that "a Roman governor in Asia Minor is apparently familiar with female deacons." The notes lean toward the situational reading without ruling. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Background only: the article "Celibacy in Antiquity" records that "Greeks and Romans debated abortion; Jews, the Hippocratic medical tradition and a number of Greco-Roman moral writers condemned it as taking life… Very early Christian writers, following Jewish values, condemned child abandonment, infanticide and abortion (e.g., Didache 2.2)"; Exodus 21:22 is read with the ancient Near Eastern codes as "a fine… probably as compensation for the fetus," the mother's injury being what carries "life for life." No contemporary position is stated. | Volume's article "Celibacy in Antiquity" (1 Corinthians 7); notes at Exodus 21:22, Psalm 139:13, Jeremiah 1:5 |
| Homosexuality | Scripture's prohibition recorded, with its ancient setting: "Scripture already prohibited such behavior (Lev. 18:22; 20:13)… Greek-speaking Jews adopted the 'against nature' charge against homosexual intercourse, blending it with a Biblical argument from God's design in creation" ("Homosexual Activity in Antiquity"); Paul "probably connects it to God's design in creation… humanity distorted God's male and female image in themselves through sexual misconduct" (Romans 1:26); "the ancient world… thought in terms of same-sex actions rather than orientation" (1 Timothy 1:10); the Sodom "interrogation" reading is rejected (Genesis 19:5). Pastoral or civil application is not given. | Volume's article "Homosexual Activity in Antiquity" (Romans 1); notes at Romans 1:26–27, 1 Corinthians 6:9, Leviticus 18:22, Genesis 19:5, 1 Timothy 1:10 |
| Alcohol | Moderation described as the ancient norm: wine was "watered down (often about two parts water to one part wine)… served undiluted only when people deliberately wanted to get drunk, behavior that most Jewish people considered unacceptable" (1 Timothy 5:23; John 2:10); "ancient wisdom literature urges against over-consumption" (Proverbs 20:1); "although Jewish wisdom despised drunkenness, it was a common feature of ancient Mediterranean life" (Ephesians 5:18). Abstinence is not commended. | Volume's notes at Proverbs 20:1, 23:30, John 2:10, Ephesians 5:18, 1 Timothy 5:23 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Jesus "sides with" Shammai: "Shammaites allowed divorce only for grounds of unfaithfulness; Jesus sides with them as against the many others who allowed it 'for just any reason'"; "viewing remarriage as adultery treats a first marriage as indissoluble in God's sight. This was shocking hyperbole, however, since Jesus' point is that marriage should not be broken, not that it never is broken"; 1 Corinthians 7:15's "not under bondage" "was the precise language in Jewish divorce contracts for freedom to remarry," and "the principle common to both passages seems to be that believers should never break their marriage covenant, but that neither are they ultimately responsible for the other partner doing so" (Matthew 19:9). | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:31–32, 19:3–9, Mark 10:11–12, 1 Corinthians 7:10–15, Deuteronomy 24:1–4 |
| Contraception | Not addressed as a question: Onan's act "does not refer to masturbation… It rather refers to the practice of ejaculating outside the woman's body ('withdrawal') as a means to avoid impregnation," his offence being the levirate duty (Genesis 38:9); Genesis 1:28 notes that "God desires that people multiply without restriction" against Mesopotamian overpopulation anxieties; extended nursing "may have served as a kind of natural contraceptive" (1 Samuel 1). | Volume's notes at Genesis 38:8–9, 1:28, 1 Samuel 1:22–24 (no position stated) |
| Gambling | Not addressed: gambling appears only as what soldiers did with dice at the cross (Matthew 27:35; Psalm 22:18). | Volume's notes at Matthew 27:35, Psalm 22:18 (no position stated) |
| Dancing | Not addressed as a question; dance is described as worship and celebration in Israel and its neighbours ("the OT attests to the importance of dance in ancient Israel," Psalm 149:3; 1 Kings 18:26). | Volume's notes at Psalm 149:3, 1 Kings 18:26, 2 Samuel 6:14 (no position stated) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Paul's standard stated against the Greek one: "Outside of marriage, respected Gentile men believed that they maintained honor by sleeping with sexual partners of lower status. For Paul, however, honorable sexual behavior was restricted to marriage" (1 Thessalonians 4:4); Greek men "often married around age 30, before that time fulfilling sexual urges with prostitutes, slaves, or other males… Jewish circles regarded such behaviors as immoral" (Hebrews 13:4); sleeping with prostitutes "was like desecrating Christ's body as well as one's own" (1 Corinthians 6:15–17). Cohabitation as a modern arrangement is not discussed. | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3–4, Hebrews 13:4, 1 Corinthians 6:12–18, 7:1–2; article "Prostitution and Sexual Immorality" |
| War/pacifism | Not adjudicated: the sixth commandment "specifically refers to homicide, meaning this verse cannot easily be brought into discussions of pacifism, capital punishment or vegetarianism" (Exodus 20:13); Jesus "assigns the kingdom instead to the meek… and those who make peace" against those who "believed that God would help them wage war against the Romans" (Matthew 5:9); "some ancient thinkers advocated nonresistance… Jesus' words go even deeper: to love one's enemies" (5:44); the two swords of Luke 22:36 fulfil Isaiah 53:12. Just-war theory is not mentioned. | Volume's notes at Exodus 20:13, Matthew 5:9, 5:39, 5:44, Luke 22:36, Romans 13:1–7 |
| Death penalty | Described as the ancient judicial norm: at Genesis 9:6 "exacting punishment for murder is not reserved for deity but is placed under the purview of human judicial systems… capital punishment was common in cases of homicide"; Romans 13:4's sword "probably refers to the government's *ius gladii*, 'right of the sword,' meaning authority to execute"; Exodus 20:13 "cannot easily be brought into discussions of… capital punishment." No modern position is taken. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:6, Exodus 20:13, 21:12, Romans 13:4 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Not adjudicated; canon described historically: "Most Judeans seem to have accepted the same books of Scripture that we today include as the OT… Some Diaspora Jews also apparently included various additional books in Greek that are now part of the Apocrypha, books widely read in the Diaspora but not accepted as canonical by Judeans" (2 Timothy 3:15); the Pharisees' corban rule "exploited the loophole" against honouring parents (Mark 7:11); Paul "has passed on Jesus' teachings" as "ancient intellectual schools emphasized carefully passing on traditions" (2 Thessalonians 2:15). | Volume's notes at 2 Timothy 3:15, Mark 7:10–11, 2 Thessalonians 2:15 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Christ's deity affirmed through its first-century resonance: Jesus "transcends wisdom, for in the beginning, He already was" (John 1:1); John "probably means the creation of the universe from nothing" (1:3); "firstborn" "does not imply that the Son was created" (Colossians 1:15); "for Jesus to be listed here between the Father and the divine Spirit implies Jesus' deity" (Matthew 28:19); Revelation 1:4–5 allows "a Trinitarian reading." The Old Testament notes are careful that Israelite readers would not have understood "the third person of the Trinity" (Numbers 27:18; Psalm 51:11; Joel 2:28). | Volume's notes at John 1:1–3, Colossians 1:15–19, Philippians 2:6–7, Matthew 28:19, Revelation 1:4–5, Numbers 27:18, Psalm 51:11 |
| Predestination and free will | Both held together as "most Jewish people" held them: "the majority of Jewish thinkers affirmed both that God was sovereign and that humans had responsibility to choose rightly. Paul here encourages his audience… that God is in control" (Romans 8:29); "most Jewish people recognized both God's sovereignty and human responsibility without viewing them as contradictory" (9:11–12); "though God hardened Pharaoh's heart, Pharaoh also initially hardened his own heart" (9:18). Calvinism and Arminianism are not named. | Volume's notes at Romans 8:29, 9:11–22, Ephesians 1:4–14 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Not treated as doctrine: "sanctification. Being holy, i.e., consecrated or set apart for God" (1 Thessalonians 4:3); "spirit, soul and body" is "a way of emphasizing the whole" rather than a trichotomy (5:23). No second work, no progressive-sanctification scheme. | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3, 5:23, Hebrews 12:14 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Jewish Gehinnom described, Revelation's imagery explained: the lake of fire is "the Jewish image of fiery Gehenna (Gehinnom), where many Jewish teachers believed that the wicked would be either tortured or consumed" (Revelation 20:14); Luke 16:23's torment "indicate[s] that he is in what Jewish tradition specified as Gehinnom"; "the smoke… ascends forever and ever" is "taken from Is. 34:10" (Revelation 14:11), and the vindicated do not watch the damned, unlike "some Jewish visionaries" (14:10); Daniel "had predicted a resurrection of the damned," a view "followed… by many early Christian writers" (20:5). Annihilationism and eternal conscious torment are both left as the sources leave them. | Volume's notes at Revelation 14:10–11, 20:5, 20:13–15, Luke 16:23–24, Mark 9:43–48, Matthew 3:12 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant in reading, without polemic: Jesus "had younger brothers (7:3–5), who would normally take the responsibility" for Mary, "a widow in her mid-40s," and John 19:26–27 is a dying man's verbal testament (John 19:26); Matthew 1:25 — "Joseph and Mary abstained from intercourse before Jesus' birth… Yet God's plan was not merely a virgin conception, but a virgin birth" — says nothing of afterwards; Luke 1:28's "highly favored" is "unusual, especially to a person of low status"; Hebrews 9:27 records that "some Jewish people did allow for a posthumous purgatory for some Israelites," against Scripture's "death being one's final opportunity for repentance." Catholic Marian doctrine is not mentioned. | Volume's notes at John 19:26, Matthew 1:25, 13:55, Luke 1:28, 1:48, Hebrews 9:27 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Historically cautious: "Christians also may have often met on the first day of the week, in honor of Jesus' resurrection, although our first explicit evidence for this role for Sunday comes from the second century" (1 Corinthians 16:2); Acts 20:7 "probably runs from Sunday evening through Monday morning"; "the Lord's Day" may be set against the imperial "Lord's day" (Revelation 1:10); Romans 14:5 explains Roman contempt for the Sabbath and that "many Gentile believers did not have the same options" to keep it. No position on Sabbath, Sunday or liturgy. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 16:2, Acts 20:7, Revelation 1:10, Romans 14:5, Colossians 2:16, Exodus 20:8 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Loyalty "a general principle… not of absolute submission in the case of evil": "such exhortations did not mean that one should do evil even at the bidding of the state; philosophers were supposed to maintain their beliefs, and Jews and Christians would not worship the emperor" (Romans 13:1–7); Nero "had not yet persecuted Christians" (13:2); Matthew 22:21 is read as surrender to God of what bears God's image, oneself; Peter's "obey God rather than men" echoes Socrates (Acts 5:29). | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–7, Matthew 22:21, 1 Peter 2:13–17, Acts 5:29 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Unity across ethnic lines as the early church's distinctive: "only a minority of groups even claimed to surmount ethnic and class divisions… Early Christians formed the fullest bridge between Jews and Gentiles" (Galatians 3:28); "ethnic division between Jews and Syrians in Caesarea often led to violence; God, however, was working to transcend this barrier" (Acts 10:28); "from one blood" "evokes the Biblical account of Adam" (17:26); Moses' Cushite wife is given three identifications, including Nubian (Numbers 12:1). Modern race is not discussed. | Volume's notes at Galatians 3:28, Acts 10:28, 17:26, Ephesians 2:14–15, Numbers 12:1 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Walton's functional reading, stated in the notes themselves: "'beginning' is a way of talking about the seven-day period rather than a point in time prior to the seven days" (Genesis 1:1); "it is more appropriate to translate 'without function' rather than 'without form'" (1:2); "it is a functional existence, not necessarily a physical existence… Light was not considered something physical in the ancient world" (1:3); "in the ancient world something was created when it was given a function. In the ancient world, the cosmos is less like a machine, more like a kingdom" ("Creation and Existence"). The age of the earth, the length of the days and evolution are not discussed; Genesis 2:7's dust "is similar to what is found in ancient Near Eastern mythology." | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1–3, 1:14, 2:7; articles "Creation and Existence," "Image and Likeness," "Rest," "The 'Firmament' and 'Waters Above'" |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithe as Israel's temple tax, giving as voluntary: "distinctions between tithes and taxes were blurred in the Biblical world," and Malachi's promise "is contingent not upon the ritual of tithing but upon the posture of repentance" (Malachi 3:8); the Pharisees' three tithes came to "roughly 23 percent" (Matthew 23:23); 2 Corinthians 9:7 recalls "voluntary giving." On prosperity: "many teachers taught that those who served God would become more prosperous. This teaching was, however, meant as a general principle… Philosophers did not, however, normally see wealth as a reward for doing good" (1 Timothy 6:5). | Volume's notes at Malachi 3:8–10, Matthew 23:23, 2 Corinthians 9:6–10, Genesis 14:20, 1 Timothy 6:5–17 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | The notes are translation-neutral and were issued on both the NIV (2016) and the NKJV (2017); the NKJV edition carries the NKJV translators' preface (with its inerrancy statement and defence of the Textus Receptus) as front matter, while the Old Testament notes repeatedly correct the NKJV's own renderings and capitalizations ("despite the NKJV's capitalization of 'spirit,'" Numbers 27:18). KJV-onlyism has no standing. | Volume's front matter (NKJV preface); notes at Numbers 27:18, Genesis 1:2 |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Paul "among the small proportion of ancient thinkers who valued mutual concern": "while continuing to uphold the call for subordinates to submit, Paul here goes beyond traditional expectations in calling for mutual submission… although specifying the wife's submission in v. 22, he grounds it grammatically in the mutual submission of v. 21" (Ephesians 5:21); wifely submission "is a general principle not applicable to situations of abuse or participation in sin" (5:22); "Paul instructs husbands here only how to love self-sacrificially" (5:25); "by ancient standards, Paul's treatment of marriage roles in Eph. 5:21–33 is clearly among these more moderate approaches" ("Marriage Roles in Antiquity"); "head" "often signified a position of authority… others cite instances also for 'source'" (1 Corinthians 11:3). Keener's egalitarian conclusions are implied, not stated. | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:21–33, 1 Peter 3:1–7, Colossians 3:18, 1 Corinthians 11:3; article "Marriage Roles in Antiquity" |

---

## 18. Thomas Nelson NKJV Open Bible, Expanded Edition

**Translation used:** New King James Version (© 1982), "the latest revision of the trusted King James Version" (publisher's welcome).
**Publisher:** Thomas Nelson, Nashville; The Open Bible study material © 1983, 1985, 1990, 1997 by Thomas Nelson, Inc. (the Expanded Edition read here is the 1997 revision, in its 2006–2008 printing), in a line that goes back to the original Open Bible of the 1970s and has since been reissued in KJV, NASB and NKJV editions.
**Scale:** "Over 4,500 study notes"; the Biblical Cyclopedic Index, a topical index that "combines the most useful features of a concordance, reference system, and index"; "The Christian's Guide to the New Life," "thirty-two outlines" of doctrine whose notes are "chained by book chapter-verse" through the text so that a reader "can actually cover the material of an advanced course in systematic theology"; book introductions and outlines (Bruce H. Wilkinson and Kenneth D. Boa); the Visual Survey of the Bible; Read-Along References and Read-Along Translations; a concordance; and study helps including "How to Study the Bible," "A Guide to Christian Workers," "The Laws of the Bible," "Why Protestants Reject the Apocrypha" and Merrill F. Unger's "The Greatest Archaeological Discoveries."

### Editorial credit
A Thomas Nelson editorial project "prepared by eminent scholars under the supervision of Thomas Nelson Publishers" rather than one general editor; the volume credits each help to its author: the Biblical Cyclopedic Index to Wick Broomall (Presbyterian minister), Jean M. Alley and Jeffrey T. Tucker; "The Christian's Guide to the New Life" to Paul R. Fink (Liberty Baptist College), Joseph B. Snider and Neal D. Williams; the introductions and outlines to Bruce H. Wilkinson (Walk Thru the Bible) and Kenneth D. Boa; "The Scarlet Thread of Redemption" to W. A. Criswell (First Baptist, Dallas); "A Guide to Christian Workers" to R. G. Lee and C. M. Ward (Assemblies of God); the Harmony of the Gospels to Jim Bill McInteer (Church of Christ); Arthur L. Farstad (executive editor of the NKJV) as consulting editor. In the same institutional category as the Life Application Study Bible, NIV Study Bible and Baker CSB Illustrated Study Bible elsewhere in this section.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly conservative evangelical, cross-denominational by design — Baptist, Presbyterian, Church of Christ and Assemblies of God contributors side by side — with the doctrinal Guide written in the plain dispensational-Baptist idiom of its Liberty-trained editors (plenary-verbal inspiration, eternal life as a present and unlosable possession, elders and deacons, a premillennial survey of Revelation) while the topical index catalogs what Scripture says under each head without arguing a system.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Plenary-verbal inspiration of the original manuscripts: the Guide rejects the "natural theory" and the "content theory" for "the plenary-verbal view, which says that all (plenary) the very words (verbal) of the Bible are inspired by God," adding that "plenary-verbal inspiration does not guarantee the inspiration of any translation, but only of the original Hebrew and Greek manuscripts" (Isaiah 59:21, "Inspiration of God's Word"); the publisher's welcome affirms "the conviction that the words of Scripture as originally penned in the Hebrew and Greek were inspired by God." The word "inerrant" is not used. |
| Salvation | By grace through faith, a free gift, with eternal security: "There is nothing that one can do to earn this gift. If one could earn it, it would not be a gift; it would be wages" (Romans 6:23, "New Life: A Free Gift"); faith is the acrostic "Forsaking All I Take Him," to "forsake all (repent of our sins) and to take Him" (Acts 16:31); everlasting life "is still a present possession that can never perish (John 10:28)" (John 5:24); "we are not to confuse people by allowing them to believe that church membership, tithing, or any good works are somehow connected with becoming a Christian" (1 Corinthians 15:3–4). The index entry "Assurance" lists "absolute assurance" as "compatible with … a nature still subject to sin." |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism as public profession: "It is faith you show publicly when you are baptized … Baptism is our open profession before the world … Baptism is one of the first tests of obedience" ("A Guide to Christian Workers"); the index lists its subjects as "believing Jews," "believing Gentiles" and "households," and its characteristics as "by water," "only one," "necessary (Acts 2:38, 41)" and "follows faith," with Romans 6:3–4 "figurative of regeneration" (index, "Baptism, Christian"). Mode and infants are not discussed. |
| The Lord's Supper | Memorial: "commemorative of Christ's death," "introductory to the new covenant," "means of Christian fellowship," "memorial feast," with John 6:26–58 "spiritually explained" (index, "Lord's Supper"); "Baptism like Communion 'shows the Lord's death'" (Guide to Christian Workers). |
| Church government | Elders and deacons of the local church, without a prescribed polity: "The NT uses four terms to describe the leadership of the church: (1) 'elder' … (2) 'bishop' … (3) 'pastor' … (4) 'deacon,'" the first three being one leadership whose "function … is twofold: (1) teaching and (2) ruling," and deacons being those who "assist them" (1 Timothy 3:1–13, "The Offices of the Church"); the local church is "a geographically located, temporally limited, and visibly evident manifestation of the universal church" (Romans 16:5); the index lists "under bishops," "function of deacons," "official assemblies … Acts 15" and "function of the presbytery … 1 Tim. 4:14" (index, "Church"). |
| Eschatology | Premillennial in its survey, with the interpretive options named: the Visual Survey reads Revelation 20 as "this one thousand-year period [in which] Christ reigns over the earth with His resurrected saints," while the Revelation introduction sets out "four major alternatives" — idealist, preterist, historicist and futurist (the last taking Revelation 4–22 as "the time immediately preceding the Second Advent (the 'Tribulation,' usually seen as seven years …)") — and says "advocates of all four interpretive approaches … agree" on the book's pastoral purpose. The index defines "Rapture, the" as the "translation of saved at Christ's return" without a timing, and lists the Second Coming's time as "after the rise of the Antichrist." |
| Spiritual gifts | Gifts affirmed for every believer, the continuation question not raised: "every believer has been given spiritual gifts … and the gifts belong to God and are given for the believer to use for the glory of God" (1 Corinthians 12:1–10, "Using Spiritual Gifts"); the index treats tongues at Pentecost and Corinth descriptively — "spiritual gift," "interpreter of, required," "love superior to," "subject to abuse" ("Tongues, speaking in"). Neither cessationism nor an initial-evidence doctrine appears. |
| Women's ordination | Not addressed as a question; the Guide speaks of the elder and "his people," and the index records woman as "made to help man," "becomes subject to man (Gen. 3:16)" and "made equal in Christ (Gal. 3:28)," with women "judging Israel," "leading an army," "prophesying" and their "labor … commended (Phil. 4:2, 3)" (index, "Woman"); the index on the sexes lists "subordination of … 1 Cor. 11:3–16," "equality in Christ … Gal. 3:28" and "different functions of … 1 Tim. 2:8–15" side by side. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed as a moral question: the index entry "Abortion—accidental or planned miscarriage" lists only "laws concerning … Ex. 21:22–25," "pronounced as a judgment … Hos. 9:14," "sought to relieve misery … Job 3:16" and a figurative use; no study note or Guide outline treats it. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Abortion" (no position stated) |
| Homosexuality | Sinful: "Homosexuality — Forbidden … Lev. 18:22; Considered an abomination … 1 Kin. 14:24; Punishment … Lev. 20:13; Unclean … Rom. 1:24, 26, 27," and listed under "Sexual perversion" with adultery, prostitution, incest and bestiality, their judgment "defilement … destruction … death" (index). | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Homosexuality," "Sexual perversion" |
| Alcohol | Total abstinence commended through the index, without a doctrinal ruling: under "Temperance … C. In the use of wine, total recommended by: Solomon … Prov. 23:31–35; Angel … Judg. 13:3–5; Nazirite vow … Num. 6:2, 3; First, among Rechabites … Jer. 35:1–10," while the "Wine" entry records its use by Jesus, Melchizedek and Timothy and its "evil effects," and "Drunkenness" its "penalties"; the Guide to Christian Workers answers "I see no harm in worldly amusements" with "Instead of asking, 'What harm is there in it?' I should ask, 'What good is there in it?'" | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Temperance," "Wine," "Drunkenness"; "A Guide to Christian Workers" |
| Divorce and remarriage | Adultery the one ground: "Marriage binding as long as life … Mark 10:2–9; Rom. 7:2, 3; Divorce allowed because of adultery … Matt. 5:27–32; Marriage of the divorced constitutes adultery … Luke 16:18; Reconciliation encouraged … 1 Cor. 7:10–17" (index, "Divorce"); "Fornication excludes remarriage … Matt. 5:32" and "Permanent bond … Matt. 19:6" (index, "Marriage"). | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Divorce," "Marriage" |
| Contraception | Not addressed: no index entry, note or outline. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index (no entry); whole-text search |
| Gambling | Not addressed: no index entry, note or outline; "worldly amusements" are answered only in general terms (1 Corinthians 10:31; 6:12). | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index (no entry); "A Guide to Christian Workers" |
| Dancing | Catalogued without a ruling: "Israelites before the gold calf … David only … Greeting a prodigal … Lustful exhibition … Mark 6:22; Religious exercise … 1 Chr. 15:25–29; Time of rejoicing … Time to dance … Eccl. 3:4; Young women alone … Judg. 21:20, 21" (index, "Dancing"). | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Dancing" |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Condemned: "Fornication—sexual relations among the unmarried … Sins against the body … 1 Cor. 6:18; Excludes from God's kingdom … 1 Cor. 6:9; Disrupts Christian fellowship" (index); sexual love is "good and holy," "for procreation" and "in marriage only … Prov. 5:15–20" (index, "Sexual love"), and marriage is "prevention of fornication … 1 Cor. 7:2, 9" (index, "Marriage"). | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Fornication," "Sexual love," "Marriage" |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine: the index "War" entry is historical ("caused by … regulations concerning … methods of attack"), and the Guide's "Function of Human Government" gives the state the sword "to impose punishment upon criminals (vv. 3, 4)" (Romans 13:1–4). | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "War"; Guide at Romans 13:1–4 (no position on Christian participation) |
| Death penalty | Recorded as divinely instituted: "Capital punishment—the death penalty. A. Institution of: By God … Gen. 9:5, 6; Ex. 21:12–17. B. Crimes punished by: Murder … Adultery … Incest … Sodomy … Rape … Witchcraft … Disobedience to parents … Blasphemy … False doctrines" (index); the Guide says officials "bear the sword" (Romans 13:1–4). Its application today is not discussed. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Capital punishment"; Guide at Romans 13:1–4 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone: the Guide's "We Know God Through His Word" and "Revelation of God's Word" outlines treat the Bible as God's sufficient self-revelation, the study help "Why Protestants Reject the Apocrypha" excludes the deuterocanon, and the Guide to Christian Workers answers "Why should I accept the Bible as the final authority?" from Scripture itself. Tradition is not treated as a source. | Volume's Guide outlines on the Word; "Why Protestants Reject the Apocrypha"; "A Guide to Christian Workers" |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and Chalcedonian: "The one, indivisible divine essence has eternally existed in three Persons … They comprise one divine Being—not as rooms in a house, but as notes in a chord … each Person of the Trinity has the full divine nature" (2 Corinthians 13:14, "Persons of the Trinity"); Christ "took on a body permanently when He was conceived in Mary's womb," was "fully man apart from sin" and "also fully God," and appeared in the Old Testament as "the 'Angel of the LORD'" (Isaiah 9:6, "The Person of the Son of God"). | Volume's Guide at 2 Corinthians 13:14 and Isaiah 9:6 |
| Predestination and free will | Predestination affirmed as God's eternal plan, election left undefined: the index "Predestination—God's eternal plan" lists it as "determined by God's counsel, foreknowledge, good pleasure, will, purpose, power" and "expressed toward the believer in election … salvation … justification … sanctification … glorification … eternal destiny," with "Foreknowledge of God" cross-referenced; the Guide speaks of salvation as received by faith and does not take up the Calvinist–Arminian question. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Predestination," "Foreknowledge of God," "Assurance"; Guide on new life |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive, by the Spirit's repeated filling: "Sanctification—growing in holiness" (index); "Unlike the indwelling of the Spirit, filling is a repeated experience … The prerequisites are simply confession of sin and yielding to God" (Ephesians 5:18, "Walking in the Spirit: Filling"). No crisis second work is taught. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Sanctification"; Guide at Ephesians 5:18 |
