| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment: "Hell—the place of eternal torment … Everlasting fire … Everlasting punishment … Outer darkness … Everlasting destruction … Lake of fire," its punishment "bodily," "in the soul" and "with degrees" (index); "If God is eternal and heaven is eternal—hell will be eternal also" (Guide to Christian Workers, to "I may be punished but not eternally"). | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Hell"; "A Guide to Christian Workers" |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary "mother of other children … Mark 6:3," "told of virginal conception," "in Upper Room" (index, "Mary"); "the Holy Spirit's power conceived a child" in her womb (Guide, Isaiah 9:6). Veneration of saints and prayer for the dead are not discussed, and the Apocrypha (with 2 Maccabees 12) is rejected. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Mary"; "Why Protestants Reject the Apocrypha" |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday: "First day of the week—Sunday. Day of Christ's resurrection … Disciples break bread together … Acts 20:7; Called 'the Lord's Day' … Rev. 1:10" (index), while the Sabbath is "seventh day of the week" and "made for human good" (index, "Sabbath"); giving is "on the first day of the week" (Guide, 2 Corinthians 9:6–8). Style is not addressed beyond the Guide's outlines on "The Meaning," "Reasons" and "Expressions of Worship." | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "First day of the week," "Sabbath"; Guide on worship |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission, taxes and prayer, with the Acts 4 limit: "It is impossible for a believer to be a good Christian and a bad citizen at the same time … the powers that be are ordained by God … This truth applies even to atheistic human governments unless, of course, the law is anti-scriptural. In that situation the believer must obey God rather than humans" (1 Peter 2:13, "Our Responsibility to Human Government"); government exists "to protect, punish, and promote" (Romans 13:1–4); Christians may "occupy positions in" government (index, "Government"). | Volume's Guide at 1 Peter 2:13 and Romans 13:1–4; index, "Government" |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | One blood, equal in Christ: the nations are "descendants of Noah's sons … made from one blood … Acts 17:26" (index, "Nations"), believers are "unified in Christ … Gal. 3:28" (index, "Church"). Race is not otherwise treated. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Nations," "Church" |
| Creation and the age of the earth | God as Creator affirmed, the age not addressed: "Creation—causing what did not exist to exist," its author "God … Jesus Christ … Holy Spirit," the first creation "subject to vanity" (index); the Genesis introduction and Visual Survey narrate the creation and fall without ruling on the length of the days. | Volume's Biblical Cyclopedic Index, "Creation"; Genesis introduction |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Proportionate giving with the tithe's principle retained: giving "is to be regular, 'on the first day of the week'; … systematic …; and … proportionate, 'as he may prosper'" (1 Corinthians 16:2), and "people who fail to honor God with their money actually rob God (Mal. 3:8) … For the children of God who honor God with their money God promises abundant blessing (Mal. 3:10; Luke 6:38)" (2 Corinthians 9:6–8, "Giving"); the index lists "Tithes—the tenth of one's income" historically. Prosperity teaching as a system is absent; "the important rule of thumb is not how much is given, but how much is left after the giving." | Volume's Guide at 2 Corinthians 9:6–8; index, "Tithes" |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the NKJV, whose preface (included) defends the Received Text and the Majority Text against the Westcott–Hort reliance on "relatively few manuscripts" while holding that "scholars agree that the science of New Testament textual criticism is in a state of flux"; the Guide states that inspiration belongs "only [to] the original Hebrew and Greek manuscripts," not to any translation. Not KJV-only. | Volume's NKJV preface; Guide at Isaiah 59:21 |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Complementarian: "The wife is to submit to the authority of her husband, not to men in general. This does not mean the wife is by nature inferior to the husband … To keep their special union from fracturing and destroying itself, one member is charged to lead and one to submit" (1 Peter 3:1–6, "The Role of the Wife"); the husband is to "love his wife" and "dwell together" with her, with "no areas of his life where there are signs that say, 'Private, husband only—wife keep out,'" and so "manifest his headship properly" (Colossians 3:19, "The Role of the Husband"). | Volume's Guide at 1 Peter 3:1–6 and Colossians 3:19 |

## 19. The Orthodox Study Bible

**Translation used:** New Testament, New King James Version (1982), "a translation of the Received Text of the Greek New Testament, with notes showing major variants in the Majority Text and the critical text"; Old Testament, the St. Athanasius Academy Septuagint (SAAS, © 2008), prepared from Rahlfs's Greek text with Brenton's 1851 translation and the NKJV Old Testament "in the places where the English translation of the LXX would match that of the Masoretic (Hebrew) text," in the canonical order of the Church of Greece's 1928 *Old Testament According to the Seventy*. The volume's own introduction says the SAAS "does not claim to be a new or superior translation."
**Publisher:** Thomas Nelson, 2008 (study material © St. Athanasius Academy of Orthodox Theology); the New Testament and Psalms edition appeared in 1993, "the first English Bible with study material reflecting the ancient faith of the Eastern Orthodox Church."
**Scale:** Verse-by-verse study notes for every book of the Septuagint canon (the Maccabees, Wisdom, Sirach, Tobit, Judith, Baruch and 1 Ezra included) that "give primary attention to: 1. The Holy Trinity… 2. The Incarnation… 3. The Centrality of the Church… 4. The Virtues," with "specific attention… to the biblical interpretations of the fathers of the ancient and undivided Church, and to the consensus of the Seven Ecumenical… Councils"; some sixty study articles ("Holy Baptism," "The Eucharist," "Justification by Faith," "Deification," "Mary," "Marriage," "Creation," "The Second Coming of Christ" and others); book introductions and outlines; liturgical notes keyed ω tying passages to the Church year; essays by Bishops Kallistos Ware, Basil and Joseph; a lectionary, morning and evening prayers, a glossary and an index to annotations.

### General editor / project leadership
The volume credits no single general editor. The Academic Community of **St. Athanasius Academy**, "chaired by Fr. Jack N. Sparks, Dean," produced it; **Fr. Peter E. Gillquist** (1938–2012), the Academy's long-time project director and a leader of the 1987 reception of former Campus Crusade staff into the Antiochian Archdiocese, appears among the Old Testament contributors. The acknowledgments list **Bishop Kallistos Ware** and some twenty clergy among those who "worked on the study material for one or more books of the New Testament," with "later editing by [Dr. Petros Vasiliadis] and his colleagues at the University of Thessalonica," and more than a hundred bishops, priests, deacons and lay scholars for the Old Testament, among them Frs. Patrick Henry Reardon, Eugen Pentiuc and John Chryssavgis and Drs. David and Mary Ford.

### Denominational leaning
Eastern Orthodox, and unlike every other volume in this section the notes state the Church's teaching as the Church's: "the Orthodox Church allows divorce and a second marriage as a concession to human weakness" (Matthew 5:31–32); "The Church, however, authoritatively rejected this teaching (called chiliasm) at the Second Ecumenical Council" (Revelation 20:2); "women are not ordained to the offices of bishop and presbyter" (1 Timothy 2:12). Patristic citations (Chrysostom above all) are given by abbreviation, and liturgical texts are quoted as authorities alongside Scripture. The entry below therefore records positions as stated in the notes and articles, not as inferred from Orthodox teaching generally. See also the Eastern Orthodox Church entry in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations."

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inspired, read within Holy Tradition: Scripture "was not written by the mere volition of men but by the inspiration of the Holy Spirit," and "so Scripture is to be interpreted by holy men guided by the Holy Spirit… The Church, founded by the apostles, likewise receives the Holy Spirit" (2 Peter 1:19–21); Timothy's training "combined both oral and written instruction… A part of this tradition is Scripture" (2 Timothy 3:14–17); Bishop Kallistos: "The decisive criterion of our understanding of what Scripture means is the mind of the Church. The Bible is the book of the Church," while Orthodox "neglect at our peril the results of independent scholarly research into the origin, dates and authorship of the books." The words "inerrant" and "infallible" are not applied to Scripture; the only "infallible" in the notes denies it to "any individual apostle (or bishop or patriarch)" (Galatians 2:11). |
| Salvation | Union with Christ, not acquittal: "Rather than justification as a legal acquittal before God, Orthodox believers see justification by faith as a covenant relationship with Him, centered in union with Christ"; "I have been saved, being joined to Christ in Holy Baptism; I am being saved, growing in Christ through the sacramental life of the Church; and I will be saved, by the mercy of God at the Last Judgment"; "nowhere does the Bible say we are justified by faith 'alone'" ("Justification by Faith," Romans 5); "we are not saved by good works, but for good works" (Ephesians 2:8–10); "justification is not by faith alone (Jam 2:24)" (Acts 10:35). Apostasy is real but repentance remains: "The Church holds that those who apostatize may repent and be brought back" (Hebrews 6:4–6). The goal is deification — "through union with Christ, we become by grace what God is by nature" ("Deification," 2 Peter 1). |
| Baptism | Regenerative, by triple immersion, infants included, followed by chrismation: baptism is "the means by which we enter the Kingdom of God (Jn 3:5), are joined to Christ (Rom. 6:3), and are granted the remission of our sins and the gift of the Holy Spirit (Acts 2:38)"; "historically, the Orthodox Church has baptized by triple immersion" ("Holy Baptism"); "we are cleansed, justified, and sanctified — all in baptism… Without our repentance and faith, however, immersion in water would be of no effect" ("The New Birth," John 3); "all his family would include not only his wife and children, but any servants" (Acts 16:33); "candidates first repent… Second, they are baptized by immersion. Third, they are given the gift of the Holy Spirit through chrismation" (Acts 2:38–39). |
| The Lord's Supper | The true Body and Blood, the "how" left a mystery: "The bread is truly His Body, that which is in the cup is truly His Blood, but one cannot say how they become so"; transubstantiation is "a philosophical definition… unknown in the ancient Church," and the "symbol-only view" of "the radical reformers" equally rejected; "a mere symbol, a quarterly reminder, could hardly have the power to cause sickness and death!" ("The Eucharist," 1 Corinthians 11); "in Communion, we truly eat His flesh and drink His blood" (John 6:53–56); Cana prefigures "the transformation of bread and wine into the Body and Blood of Christ" (John 2:9). |
| Church government | Four orders, episcopal, conciliar, not papal: "all four 'orders'… laity, deacons, presbyters, and bishops — are necessary"; "the bishopric is a specific office both in the New Testament and in the early Church. The Twelve were the first to hold this office… and they in turn consecrated other bishops" ("The Four 'Orders' in Church Government," 1 Timothy 3); "Peter was not a leader over the others, but among them… where Peter advised, but James presided. Papal claims in later centuries must not be confused with the NT witness regarding Peter" (Matthew 16:19); "this rock refers not to Peter per se, but to 'the faith of his confession' (JohnChr)" (16:18); "married men may be ordained to the Orthodox priesthood… Married bishops are in accordance with the general teaching of Orthodox canon law" (1 Timothy 3:2). |
| Eschatology | Amillennial by conciliar decision: "a few early Fathers and writers believed in a literal thousand year binding of Satan and reign of Christ and the saints on earth. The Church, however, authoritatively rejected this teaching (called chiliasm) at the Second Ecumenical Council… a thousand years… is interpreted as the Church age" (Revelation 20:2); "the first resurrection (v. 6) is the heavenly life of souls who have died in Christ before His Second Coming" (20:4–6); "this generation refers to all believers at all times, the generation of the Church" (Matthew 24:34). "Much of modern Christendom has succumbed to divisive speculation… premillennial, postmillennial, and amillennial camps… pre-tribulation, mid-tribulation, and post-tribulation… The emphasis of historic Orthodoxy is that Jesus will come again, not when" ("The Second Coming of Christ," Titus 2). |
| Spiritual gifts | Continuing, ordered under the bishop, with tongues ranked last: "The Orthodox Church has never believed or behaved as though the gifts of the Spirit or the healing miracles of Christ have somehow passed away" ("Healing," James 5, citing Irenaeus and Seraphim of Sarov); "there is a hierarchy of honor in the Church of the NT as there is in the Church today. The apostolic work, that of the bishop, sets the pace for all the rest" (1 Corinthians 12:28–31); "the gift of tongues is last on the list of the gifts… temporary… primarily for the edification of the one who speaks… St. John Chrysostom concludes that tongues are 'neither altogether useless, nor very profitable'" (14:2). |
| Women's ordination | Reserved to men, deaconesses acknowledged: "The Church's greatest saint is a woman, the Virgin Mary, Mother of God. Some women, including Mary Magdalene, are called 'Equal to the Apostles,' others serve as deacons (Rom 16:1). While sharing full equality in Christ, women are not ordained to the offices of bishop and presbyter" (1 Timothy 2:12); Phoebe "was both a deaconess… and involved in missionary work" (Romans 16:1–2); 1 Timothy 3:11 "or perhaps 'women,' referring to deaconesses"; at 1 Corinthians 14:34–36 "all are equal in Christ, while the order of the original creation remains in the new creation." |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Murder: "Verses 13–16 reveal the sanctity of life of an unborn child and explain why, both in ancient Israel and in the Church, aborting a babe in the womb is seen as the sin of murder" (Psalm 138 [139]); Elizabeth's confession "deals a fatal blow to the idea that abortion is justifiable because the fetus is not yet a person" (Luke 1:43); "in our modern era, laws are being passed favoring homosexuality and abortion. But laws of this kind are an abomination to God" (Leviticus 18). | Volume's notes at Psalm 138 (139):13–16, Luke 1:41–43, Leviticus 18:1–30 |
| Homosexuality | Condemned as against nature: "Homosexual behavior, men with men, is a vile rejection of God's order for creation. These passions are against nature and therefore spiritually devastating" (Romans 1:26–27); "the perversion of homosexuality (v. 22)" among Canaan's "unjust and ungodly" ordinances, with the application to modern law quoted above (Leviticus 18); "such were some of you shows that in Christ we can be set free from the very worst of sins" (1 Corinthians 6:11); Sodom "lusted after angels because of their sin of immorality and homosexuality" (Jude 5–7). | Volume's notes at Romans 1:26–27, Leviticus 18, 1 Corinthians 6:9–11, Jude 5–7, Judges 19:24–30 |
| Alcohol | Not evil in itself, excess condemned, fasting assumed: "Wine and strong drink are not evil in themselves. Wine is intemperate in those who drink too much of it" (Proverbs 20:1); Timothy's ailments "perhaps from strict water-fasts… Paul does not suggest breaking the fasts, only decreasing their rigor" (1 Timothy 5:23); "he who is made drunk with wine totters and sways, but he who is inebriated with the Holy Spirit is rooted in Christ and gloriously sober" (Ephesians 5:18–20). | Volume's notes at Proverbs 20:1, 1 Timothy 5:23, Ephesians 5:18–20, John 2:9 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Oikonomia stated outright: "while recognizing divorce as a serious sin, the Orthodox Church allows divorce and a second marriage as a concession to human weakness and as a corrective measure of compassion when a marriage has been broken. A third marriage is permitted only under specific, limited circumstances, and a fourth marriage is never permitted" (Matthew 5:31–32); "divorce is hated by God (Mal 2:16), a last measure, a great calamity. Paul forbids remarriage while the first spouse is still living (v. 39)" (1 Corinthians 7:11). | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:31–32, 19:8–9, 1 Corinthians 7:11 |
| Contraception | Not addressed: the Genesis 38 notes treat Tamar's fornication (v. 24), not Onan, and the words do not occur. | Whole-text search of notes and articles (no entry) |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the word occurs once, figuratively, of leprosy (Leviticus 13). | Whole-text search (no position stated) |
| Dancing | Not addressed as a question: David's dance is set against Herodias's daughter's (1 Chronicles 15:27–29), and the ark greeted "with dancing is perhaps an import from Canaanite practices" (2 Kingdoms 6). | Volume's notes at 1 Chronicles 15:27–29, 2 Kingdoms 6 (no position stated) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Chastity within marriage, celibacy a gift: "the libertines in Corinth — as some do today — argued that illicit sex (adultery and fornication) is as necessary for the body as eating… Paul contends the body belongs to God… This is why in the Orthodox Church abstinence from foods is the first discipline of the Fast" (1 Corinthians 6:13); "one's vessel must be possessed with purity, self-control, and chastity" (1 Thessalonians 4:4); "Paul's personal preference is for celibacy, but he knows this is a 'gift from God' (v. 7) that is not given to all" (7:1); "both fornication and adultery, however, are one and the same sin" (Genesis 38:24). Cohabitation as such is not named. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:13, 7:1, 1 Thessalonians 4:4, Genesis 38:24; article "Marriage" |
| War/pacifism | Non-retaliation taught, no theory of war: "Jesus warns us not to resist violence with more violence. Evil can only be overcome by good," illustrated by the desert monk who pursued the bandits to give them his walking stick (Matthew 5:39–42); "the love of enemies is not merely an emotion, but includes decision and action" (5:44–47); the civil authority "is responsible to protect those who are good and to execute punishment on those who practice evil" (Romans 13:4); Deuteronomy 20 is read "in a mystery" of the undivided heart. Just war and pacifism are not named. | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:39–47, Romans 13:1–5, Deuteronomy 20:8; "The Eternal Kingdom" |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a question: Romans 13:4's minister is "to execute punishment on those who practice evil"; John 8:7–11 — Christ "upholds a great principle of the Law — that the wages of sin is death… without neglecting its greater aspect of mercy"; Exodus 21:24's retaliation is what "the saints in the Old Testament did not fulfill." No note at Genesis 9:6. | Volume's notes at Romans 13:4, John 8:7–11, Exodus 21:24 (no position stated) |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Holy Tradition as the Spirit-given context of Scripture: "two types of traditions: (1) The tradition of men (Mt 15:1–9; Col 2:8) is soundly condemned… (2) The tradition of the apostles or 'Holy Tradition,' by contrast, is to be preserved by the Church, for God is its source" (2 Thessalonians 2:15); the Russian reception rite is quoted — the convert must accept Scripture "in accordance with the belief which has been handed down by the Holy Fathers" (Kallistos, "How to Read the Bible"); the Septuagint canon is printed and annotated throughout, 2 Maccabees 12:42–45 being "a clear statement that the righteous Jews prayed for the departed." | Volume's note at 2 Thessalonians 2:15; 3:6; essay "How to Read the Bible"; notes at 2 Maccabees 12:42–45 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Nicene-Constantinopolitan without the *filioque*, Chalcedonian: "the Holy Spirit eternally proceeds from the Father alone… In conformity with Christ's words, the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed confesses belief 'in the Holy Spirit… who proceeds from the Father'" (John 15:26); "the Word became fully human without ceasing to be fully God… for anything not assumed by Christ would not have been healed" (1:14); "Christ has equality with God not by seizure but by nature" (Philippians 2:6); the Trinity found in Genesis 1:1–3 and 1:26 ("God the Father is speaking to God the Son (JohnChr)"). | Volume's notes at John 1:1–14, 15:26, Philippians 2:5–8, Colossians 1:15–16, Genesis 1:1–3, 26; article "The Holy Trinity" |
| Predestination and free will | Synergy, foreknowledge, no reprobation: "Predestined must not be understood as overpowering man's free response, for man's free will is a gift from God. Nor does the term apply to individuals" (Romans 8:28–30); "Esau I have hated… does not mean God did not love Esau. Rather, God foresaw the wickedness Esau would choose and hated it" (9:10–13); "there is no automatic, fated condemnation: God's just judgment of us is based on our exercise of free will" ("Basis of God's Judgment," Romans 2); "note the cooperation: we work out our own salvation (v. 12) while it is God who works in us" (Philippians 2:12–13). | Volume's notes at Romans 8:28–30, 9:10–24, Ephesians 1:4–6, Philippians 2:12–13; article "Basis of God's Judgment" |
| Sanctification and holiness | Deification by participation in God's energies: "we participate in God's energy… We are to become like God by His grace, and truly His adopted children, but never become God by nature" (2 Peter 1:4); "the energy of the fire interpenetrates the sword. The sword never becomes fire, but it picks up the properties of fire" ("Deification"); "God's original intent for the Incarnation was not redemption from the Fall but adoption as sons of God (v. 5), that is, deification… 'God became man that man might become god'" (Ephesians 1:4–6). "Ancestral Sin": "mankind is not totally depraved… We who are of Adam's race are not guilty because of Adam's sin, but because of our own sin." | Volume's articles "Deification" (2 Peter 1), "Ancestral Sin" (Genesis 3); notes at 2 Peter 1:3–4, Ephesians 1:4–6 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal, self-chosen, God's presence as torment: "God did not create hell for man; rather, people choose this torment by their coldness of heart" (Matthew 25:41); "even the presence of God, the Lamb, and His good servants, the holy angels, is a torment" (Revelation 14:9–11); "the lake of fire, the second death of eternal damnation" (20:7–10); "the great gulf is… the complete separation between virtue and wickedness, a separation that cannot be overcome after death" (Luke 16:24–26); the Liturgy's prayer "for a good defense before the dread Judgment Seat of Christ" is quoted. Universalism is not entertained. | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:31–46, Revelation 14:9–11, 20:7–15, Luke 16:22–26; article "Basis of God's Judgment" |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | All affirmed from the text: "The tradition of the Church holds that Mary remained a virgin all her life… We entreat her… to intercede with her Son on our behalf… We venerate her — but we do not worship her" ("Mary," Luke 1); "Christ Himself had no blood brothers, for Mary had but one Son: Jesus. The brothers mentioned here were either stepbrothers… or cousins" (Matthew 12:46–50); "highly favored (Gr. charitou) can also be translated 'full of grace'" and Mary is "the new Eve" (Luke 1:28); the elders "present to God the prayers of the saints still on earth" (Revelation 5:8); 2 Maccabees 12:42–45 grounds "prayers for the departed"; "icons will be used in worship as windows into heaven" (2 Chronicles 3). | Volume's article "Mary"; notes at Matthew 12:46–50, Luke 1:27–28, John 19:25–27, Revelation 5:8, 2 Maccabees 12:42–45, 2 Chronicles 3:7–14, 3 Kingdoms 6:22–36 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | The Divine Liturgy on Sunday: "the first day of the week was Sunday, the Lord's Day of Resurrection, the Church's customary day to celebrate the Eucharist. Worship on the Sabbath ceased with the passing of the old covenant… the weekly Eucharist is always the culmination of the Lord's Day assembly" (Acts 20:7–12); "For Orthodox Christians, Saturday is still the Sabbath, the day on which the Church especially remembers the departed" ("Sabbath Day, Sunday, and the Eighth Day," Deuteronomy 5); "Christian worship on earth, to be fully Christian, must mirror the worship of Christ in heaven" ("Liturgy in the New Testament Church," Revelation 4). | Volume's notes at Acts 20:7–12, Revelation 1:10, Colossians 2:16–23; articles "Sabbath Day, Sunday, and the Eighth Day," "Liturgy in the New Testament Church"; the lectionary and prayers |
| Church and state / political engagement | Obedience within God's limit, Church and state cooperating: "To defy the civil government is to defy God… This principle holds true so long as the civil ordinance is not in opposition to God" (Romans 13:1–3); "this is not a call to 'separate' Church from state, but rather a call to cooperate with and enhance the state — realizing that at times Church and state have been at great odds. When ultimate loyalty… to Christ and His gospel are at stake… we are to obey God rather than man, even to the point of prison or death" (1 Peter 2:13–17). | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–5, 1 Peter 2:13–17 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Equality of nature: "valuing people based on opinions and ethnicity (neither Jew nor Greek), pride and social status (neither slave nor free), or gender (neither male nor female) has no place in the Church. All are one in nature, and so all are equal in dignity" (Galatians 3:28–29); Pentecost reverses Babel — "the humble reception of the Holy Spirit is shown to have the overwhelming power to unite, even in diversity" (Acts 2:4–8). Race as a modern question is not treated. | Volume's notes at Galatians 3:28–29, Acts 2:4–8, 10:34–43 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Not dogmatized: "Regarding questions about the scientific accuracy of the Genesis account of creation, and about various viewpoints concerning evolution, the Orthodox Church has not dogmatized any particular view. What is dogmatically proclaimed is that the One Triune God created everything that exists, and that man was created in a unique way… The Church Fathers also consistently affirm that each species of the animate creation came into existence instantaneously, at the command of God" ("Creation," Genesis 1). The Genesis 1 notes read the days Trinitarianly (Father, Word, Spirit as co-creators) and say nothing of their length or of the earth's age. | Volume's article "Creation"; notes at Genesis 1:1–25, 2:7 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithing maintained: "Some modern Christians reject tithing, claiming it is a part of the Law not applicable today. But they forget that Abram (Abraham) tithed to Melchizedek five hundred years before the Law was given" (Malachi 3:8–12); "the Church has maintained the ancient practices of tithing (These you ought to have done, v. 23…)" (Matthew 23:23–33); 2 Corinthians 9 is "Paul's landmark teaching on Christian stewardship." Prosperity teaching is not named. | Volume's notes at Malachi 3:8–12, Matthew 23:23–33, 2 Corinthians 9:6–15 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | A Septuagint Old Testament and a Received-Text New Testament, both chosen for ecclesial reasons: the Church "has traditionally used the Greek Old Testament of the Septuagint (LXX)," and the NKJV New Testament is "a translation of the Received Text… with notes showing major variants in the Majority Text and the critical text" (introduction). KJV-onlyism has no standing; the Septuagint's priority over the Hebrew does. | Volume's "Introduction to The Orthodox Study Bible"; essay "The Bible: God's Revelation to Man" |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | "Both a fullness of equality… and a clarity of order": crowns "symbolic of their citizenship in the Kingdom of God, where 'there is neither male nor female' (Gal 3:28)," and "the husband is to serve God as head of his wife… There is nothing here to suggest the wife is oppressed in marriage, any more than one would call the Church oppressed in relationship to Christ… the husband as the icon of Christ and the wife as the icon of the Church" ("Marriage," Ephesians 5); "wives are called to submit to their husbands as equals" (Ephesians 5:22–33); "duties are reciprocal, everyone having the same standing before the same Master. All authority is for the sake of loving service" (Colossians 3:18–4:1). | Volume's article "Marriage"; notes at Ephesians 5:21–33, Colossians 3:18–4:1, 1 Peter 3:1–7, Genesis 2:18–25 |

## 20. NET Bible, Full-Notes Edition

**Translation used:** New English Translation (NET Bible 2.1)
**Publisher:** Thomas Nelson (text and notes © 1996, 2019 Biblical Studies Press).
**Scale:** "More than 60,000 translators' notes outlining every major translation decision, interpretive challenge, and textual variant" (preface), set beneath a single-column text in the manner of a glossed medieval manuscript — functioning, by volume and density of notes, as a de facto study Bible even though it is formally the translation's own annotated edition rather than a separate study-Bible product built atop it. The notes are typed *tn* (translator's note), *sn* (study note), *tc* (text-critical note) and *map*.

### General editor / project leadership
The preface says that "in 1995 a multi-denominational team of more than twenty-five of the world's foremost biblical scholars gathered" to produce the translation; the first edition names W. Hall Harris III as Project Director and Managing Editor and Michael H. Burer as Editor and Assistant Project Director, and the second edition (NET 2.1) James Davis as Managing Editor, with Brian Webster as Old Testament Editor. No single named general editor comparable to Grudem, Sproul or MacArthur is credited; the translators and editors listed are drawn largely from Dallas Theological Seminary and other evangelical faculties.

### Denominational leaning
Multi-denominational, broadly evangelical. The preface states the team's "deeply held belief that Scripture is inspired (2 Timothy 3:16), that every single detail matters (Matthew 5:18–19), and that faithfulness proves more important than tradition (Mark 7:8)," and describes the notes as highlighting "every major decision," outlining "alternative views," and explaining "difficult or nontraditional renderings." The notes are oriented toward transparency about translation and textual decisions rather than doctrinal or devotional application in the manner of the ESV Study Bible or MacArthur Study Bible; where a contested text is reached they usually set out the exegetical options and either rule on the Greek or Hebrew or leave the decision open, without naming the confessional positions at stake.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inspired and authoritative: the preface grounds the project in the "belief that Scripture is inspired (2 Timothy 3:16), that every single detail matters (Matthew 5:18–19), and that faithfulness proves more important than tradition (Mark 7:8)." No formal doctrine of inerrancy is stated; the notes flag where a translation choice bears on a doctrinal debate without deciding it. |
| Salvation | Not addressed as doctrine, but the Romans 3 notes rule on the text: "justified" may be "declared righteous" (3:24 tn); "faith in Jesus Christ" at 3:22 and 3:26 is read as a subjective genitive, "the faithfulness of Jesus Christ," the note calling the decision "difficult" and citing *ExSyn* that this "is not a denial of faith in Christ as a Pauline concept"; "mercy seat" at 3:25 is "the covering of the ark where the blood was sprinkled"; and "works of the law" (3:20) is taken, with Cranfield, as "obedience to the Mosaic law in general" rather than circumcision and food laws only. The notes at 10:9–10 are lexical. |
| Baptism | Not addressed as doctrine: at Matthew 28:19 the notes say only that "Go…baptize…teach" are "participles modifying the imperative verb 'make disciples'" and defend the Trinitarian formula textually ("there is no ms support for their contention" that it is a later addition); the Romans 6:3–4 notes are grammatical. Nothing on mode, subjects or efficacy. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not addressed as doctrine: the 1 Corinthians 11:23–30 notes gloss "careful regard" as "judging between, recognizing, distinguishing" (11:29) and "asleep" as "a euphemism for the death of a believer" (11:30, cross-referenced from Matthew 27:52), with nothing on "this is my body" or the manner of Christ's presence. |
| Church government | Not addressed as doctrine: at 1 Timothy 3:1 "overseer" "is naturally taken generically (referring to the class of leaders known as overseers)"; "husband of one wife" may be "a man married only once" or "devoted solely to his wife"; the "women" of 3:11 could be deaconesses, but the note concludes "a decision in this matter is difficult." No polity is endorsed. |
| Eschatology | Not addressed: the Revelation 20 notes contain no comment on millennial interpretations (the 20:5 note says the verse "appears to be a parenthetical comment by the author") and none on the second death or eternal punishment; the volume takes no confessional position. |
| Spiritual gifts | Not addressed as doctrine: "spiritual gifts" at 1 Corinthians 12:1 is glossed "Grk 'spiritual things,'" the plural at 14:1 "is probably a shorthand for the Spirit's gifts, especially in this context, tongues," "when what is perfect comes" (13:10) is glossed only "Or 'when completion,'" and nothing is said about whether the gifts cease; the questions of 12:29–30 "all expect a negative response." |
| Women's ordination | Not addressed as doctrine, but the notes rule on the contested texts: Phoebe was "a servant of the church, not a deaconess" (Romans 16:1, with the caveat that "this conclusion should be regarded as tentative"), and Junia "well known to the apostles" (16:7); the 1 Timothy 3:11 "women" are probably wives rather than deaconesses, "a decision in this matter is difficult"; at 1 Corinthians 14:34–35 the verses are judged original ("there are no mss that omit the verses") and "be silent" is related "to the preceding regulations about evaluating the prophets," the note observing that 11:2–16 permits women to pray or prophesy in the assembly; the 1 Timothy 2:12 note gives the BDAG gloss of *authentein*. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed as doctrine, but the Exodus 21:22 note rules on the text: the phrase "may indicate that the child was killed, as in a miscarriage; or it may mean that there was a premature birth. The latter view is taken here," the lex talionis of 21:23–25 being introduced "with cases that were not likely to have applied to the situation of the pregnant woman." No position on abortion is taken. | Volume's notes at Exodus 21:22–25 |
| Homosexuality | Not addressed as doctrine, but the Romans 1:26–27 notes render the Greek plainly: the women "exchanged the natural function for that which is contrary to nature," where *chrēsis* "has the force of 'sexual relations' here," and the men abandoned "the natural function of the female" and "burned with intense desire"; at 1 Corinthians 6:9 "practicing homosexuals" is explained by the distinction "in contemporary usage between sexual orientation and actual behavior." The notes are lexical and draw no ethical conclusion. | Volume's notes at Romans 1:26–27 and 1 Corinthians 6:9 |
| Alcohol | Not addressed: the Genesis 9:21 note says "Noah became overheated because of the wine and uncovered himself," the Ephesians 5:18 notes are grammatical ("be filled by means of the [Holy] Spirit," with "debauchery" glossed "Or 'dissipation'"), and the Romans 14:21 note is textual only; the volume takes no position on drinking. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:21, Ephesians 5:18 and Romans 14:21 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Not addressed as doctrine; the notes set out the options without ruling: the Matthew 5:32 note says the exception clause "has been the subject of much debate" and refers the reader to Instone-Brewer (Matthew 19:9 carries no note); at 1 Corinthians 7:15 the note gives both views of "not bound" — divorce "allowable under these circumstances, but not remarriage," or "free to remain single or to remarry" — and observes that if the parallel with 7:39–40 holds, "not bound in v. 15 also means 'free to marry another'"; the Romans 7:2 note says the illustration shows "that death frees a person from obligation to the law." | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:32, 1 Corinthians 7:12–15 and Romans 7:2 |
| Contraception | Not addressed as doctrine, but the Genesis 38:9 notes describe the act plainly — Onan "deliberately got rid of his semen on the ground so that his brother's widow would not become pregnant" — and locate the wrong in his refusal of the levirate duty: "He was willing to use the law to gratify his desires, but was not willing to fulfill his responsibilities." No conclusion about contraception is drawn. | Volume's notes at Genesis 38:8–10 |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the only occurrences of the word are translators' notes explaining why "cast lots" is rendered "throwing dice" at the crucifixion — "a modern equivalent…chosen here because of its association with gambling" (Matthew 27:35 and parallels) — and the Proverbs 16:33 study note treats casting lots as "the practice of seeking divine leading" through which "the Lord gives guidance in decisions that are submitted to him." No ethical position is taken. | Volume's notes at Matthew 27:35, Luke 23:34, Proverbs 16:33 |
| Dancing | Not addressed: the dancing of Exodus 15:20, Judges 11:34 and 2 Samuel 6:14 is glossed only with the Hebrew idiom or a bibliographic reference; at Exodus 32:6 the note rejects Kaiser's reading of "play" as "drunken immoral orgies and sexual play" ("quite an assumption for this word…the word does not mean it"), saying "the fact that they were festive and playing before an idol was sufficient." No position on dancing is taken. | Volume's notes at Exodus 15:20, 32:6, Judges 11:34, 2 Samuel 6:14 |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Not addressed as doctrine, but the notes render the texts plainly: the Exodus 20:14 study note distinguishes adultery, which "had a death penalty," from "sexual contact with a young woman (which carried a monetary fine and usually marriage if the father was willing)," adding that "both were sins"; at 1 Corinthians 7:1 "not to touch a woman" is "a euphemism for sexual relations," probably a Corinthian slogan Paul corrects "to show how the Corinthians misused the idea to justify abstinence within marriage"; at 7:2 "have a wife" means "have marital relations with"; at 6:18 the slogan "is a slogan used by the Corinthians to justify their immoral behavior" (a decision "regarded as tentative"); and at 1 Corinthians 5:1 the note observes it is "not clear…whether the man had actually married the woman or was merely cohabiting with her." No ruling on cohabitation is given. | Volume's notes at Exodus 20:14, 1 Corinthians 5:1, 6:18, 7:1–2 |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed: the Romans 13:4 "sword" carries no note; the volume takes no confessional position. | Volume's notes at Romans 13 (none on the sword) |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as doctrine: the Genesis 9:5 note says only that "God will exact punishment for the taking of a life" and refers the reader to a journal article on capital punishment in 9:6; the Romans 13:4 "sword" carries no note. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:5–6 and Romans 13:4 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture over tradition, stated once: the preface says the team holds "that faithfulness proves more important than tradition (Mark 7:8)." The notes themselves do not argue the point — at Mark 7:8 the note is text-critical, at 7:13 the note on "nullify" is grammatical ("this participle shows the results of the Pharisees' command"), and at 2 Thessalonians 2:15 and 1 Corinthians 11:2 the "traditions" Paul hands on receive only Greek glosses. No appeal is made to church tradition for doctrine anywhere in the notes. | Volume's preface; notes at Mark 7:8–13, 1 Corinthians 11:2, 2 Thessalonians 2:15 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Orthodox Trinitarian translators; the notes defend renderings that bear on Christ's deity on grammatical and textual grounds — at John 1:1 the anarthrous *theos* is read qualitatively ("the Word was fully God"), and at John 1:18 "the external evidence thus strongly supports *monogenēs theos*" — while drawing also on wider Johannine usage (John 8:58, 10:30). | Volume's translators' notes at John 1:1, 1:18 |
| Predestination and free will | Not addressed as doctrine; the Romans 9 notes gloss the Greek ("God's purpose according to election," 9:11; "he hardens whom he desires," 9:18) and at 9:22 offer "vessels destined for wrath" as an alternative rendering, and the Ephesians 1:4–5 notes weigh syntax ("by predestining" as one option for what "in love" modifies) — exegetical rulings on the text without naming Calvinist or Arminian positions. | Volume's translators' notes at Romans 9:11–22 and Ephesians 1:4–11 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Not addressed as doctrine: the Romans 6 notes on "holiness" (6:19, 6:22) are grammatical and lexical only. | Volume's notes at Romans 6:19, 6:22 (none doctrinal) |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Not addressed as doctrine; the Matthew 5:22 note explains Gehenna as the Greek transliteration of *ge hinnom*, the valley south of Jerusalem where "human sacrifices to the pagan god Molech" were offered, later a rubbish dump and, in the intertestamental period, a symbol of divine punishment; Hades and "eternal" are likewise explained lexically. | Volume's translators' note at Matthew 5:22 and parallels |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Not addressed as doctrine, but the Luke 1:28 note rules on the translation: "favored one" "points to Mary as the recipient of God's grace, not a bestower of it," and the Vulgate's "full of grace" "does not make sense here contextually." | Volume's translators' note at Luke 1:28 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Not addressed as doctrine; the notes record Sunday observance historically without legislating it: at Acts 20:7 "This is the first mention of a Sunday gathering (1 Cor 16:2)," and at Revelation 1:10 BDAG is quoted that "the Lord's day" is "certainly Sunday." Colossians 2:16 carries no note on the Sabbath, the Romans 14:5 note is a Greek gloss, and nothing is said about liturgy or music. | Volume's notes at Acts 20:7, Revelation 1:10, Romans 14:5 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Not addressed as doctrine: the Romans 13:1–7 notes are confined to Greek wording ("by God," the referent of "its wrath," "devoted to this very thing") and take no position on the state. | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–7 (none doctrinal) |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Not addressed as doctrine: the Genesis 9 notes read Ham's offense as disrespect ("Ham had no respect for his father") and insist "the curse is pronounced on Canaan, not Ham," leaving no room for the racial reading of the passage. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:20–25 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Not addressed as doctrine, but the Genesis 1 notes do rule on the Hebrew: the study note at 1:5 says "the exegetical evidence suggests the word 'day' in this chapter refers to a literal 24 hour day," adding that "the commandment to keep the sabbath clearly favors this interpretation," while the note at 1:1 leaves open whether verse 1 describes the original creation or summarizes what follows; no age of the earth is asserted and the gap theory is not named. | Volume's translators' notes at Genesis 1:1–5 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Not addressed as doctrine: the Malachi 3:8–10 notes say the tithes "are probably those used to sustain the Levites" and describe the storehouse as "a kind of temple warehouse," with no application to Christian giving. | Volume's notes at Malachi 3:8–10 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | An original translation: the Old Testament from *Biblia Hebraica Stuttgartensia* (corrected in places from other evidence) and the New Testament from "an eclectic text…differing in several hundred places from the standard critical text as represented by the Nestle-Aland 28th edition," each departure marked with a double dagger; the notes exist so that readers may "see for yourself" the decisions behind the translation, and the preface's "ministry first" copyright policy keeps the text and notes "freely available at netbible.org." KJV-onlyism is rejected by implication of the textual notes, which repeatedly judge Byzantine additions secondary (e.g., Mark 7:8). | Volume's preface and translators' notes |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Not addressed as doctrine; the notes present options without ruling: at 1 Corinthians 11:3 "man" and "woman" may mean "husband" and "wife"; at 11:10 the "symbol of authority" is either "a figure of speech that may substitute the result (the right to participate in worship) for the appropriate appearance" or "the outward symbol…representing the inward attitude the woman is to possess (deference to male leadership in the church)," and "Paul does not use a word specifying what type of 'covering' is meant"; the Ephesians 5:21–22 notes treat "submitting" as a result of being filled by the Spirit and discuss whether "submit" appears in 5:22; the 1 Timothy 2:12 note gives the BDAG gloss of *authentein*. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 11:3–10, Ephesians 5:21–22, 1 Timothy 2:12 |

## 21. The Complete Jewish Study Bible

**Translation used:** Complete Jewish Bible (CJB)
**Publisher:** Messianic Jewish Publishers / Hendrickson Publishers, first published 2016.
**Scale:** Combines the full CJB text (Tanakh and B'rit Hadashah/New Covenant together, without separation) with topical articles, extensive bottom-of-page notes, and Jewish-perspective book introductions.

### General editors
**Rabbi Barry Rubin**, General Editor — M.A. in Communications, Ohio University; rabbi of Emmanuel Messianic Jewish Congregation in Clarksville, Maryland (founded 1915, the oldest continuously operating Messianic congregation in the world); since 1988, director of Messianic Jewish Publishers and Resources (formerly The Lederer Foundation), a Baltimore-area organization with roots stretching back to the 1970s.
**Dr. John Fischer** served as Theological Editor, with additional notes and articles contributed by a team including Rabbi Dr. Glenn Blank.

The underlying CJB translation itself was produced by **Dr. David H. Stern** (1935–2022), a physicist-turned-theologian (Ph.D. in economics, Princeton University) who, after immigrating to Israel in 1979, produced both the Jewish New Testament (1989) and a Tanakh rendering drawing on the 1917 Jewish Publication Society version, combining both into the CJB in 1998.

### Denominational leaning
Messianic Judaism — a movement combining Jewish religious practice and identity with faith in Yeshua (Jesus) as the promised Messiah, distinct from both mainstream Judaism (which does not recognize Yeshua's messianic claims) and most mainstream Christian denominations (which typically do not retain the same degree of ongoing Torah observance and Jewish liturgical practice). This book's "Major U.S. Christian Denominations" section does not currently include Messianic Judaism as a separate entry, so no direct cross-reference exists elsewhere in this book for this movement's fuller doctrinal profile.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Fully inspired and authoritative, the Tanakh and the B'rit Hadashah (New Covenant) together; read with attention to Jewish interpretive tradition. |
| Salvation | By grace through trust in Yeshua as Messiah; broadly evangelical in soteriology, with the Torah retained as a way of life rather than a means of justification. |
| Baptism | Believer's immersion (mikveh), understood in continuity with Jewish ritual immersion. |
| The Lord's Supper | Observed within the Passover seder framework as a memorial of Yeshua's death; not sacramental in the Catholic or Lutheran sense. |
| Church government | Congregational, with rabbis or elders leading local Messianic congregations. |
| Eschatology | Premillennial, with a strong emphasis on the literal restoration of Israel and the return of Yeshua to Jerusalem. |
| Spiritual gifts | Generally continuationist; the movement overlaps with charismatic Christianity, though practice varies. |
| Women's ordination | Varies by congregation and umbrella body; not addressed as a doctrine in the notes. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not verified; Messianic Jewish congregations generally affirm the sanctity of human life consistent with broader conservative Jewish and Christian positions, but this volume's own specific verse-note wording on this question has not been directly verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | Not verified; Jewish tradition generally treats moderate wine use positively (wine is integral to Sabbath and festival observance), with drunkenness treated as a separate matter of self-control. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | Marriage intended to be permanent; divorce permitted on biblical grounds, with the Jewish get (bill of divorce) sometimes retained in practice. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; generally treated as a matter of conscience within marriage. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Dancing | Not addressed as a moral question; dance is an ordinary part of Messianic worship and celebration. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations reserved for marriage, following both Torah and New Covenant teaching. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the movement's strong identification with Israel generally implies support for national self-defense. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture (Tanakh and B'rit Hadashah) is the final authority, read with respect for Jewish tradition and rabbinic commentary as illuminating but not authoritative. | Volume's introduction and notes |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian in substance while preferring Hebrew categories; Yeshua is affirmed as fully divine Messiah and the notes avoid the term "Trinity" where possible. | Volume's notes and theme articles |
| Predestination and free will | Not confessionally fixed; the Messianic Jewish contributors range across the question. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Sanctification and holiness | Holiness pursued through Torah-shaped living in the Messiah; the notes commend observance of the commandments as response to grace. | Volume's notes and theme articles |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal judgment affirmed; the fate of the lost is not treated at length. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Mary (Miryam) is honored as the mother of Yeshua but not venerated; no prayer to or for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Saturday Shabbat observance and the biblical festivals are commended in the notes and articles as the Messianic Jewish pattern; worship follows synagogue forms with Messianic content. | Volume's notes and theme articles |
| Church and state / political engagement | Not addressed as doctrine; support for the Jewish people and Israel is a recurring theme of the articles. | Volume's theme articles |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The unity of Jew and Gentile in the Messiah without erasing Jewish identity (Romans 11; Ephesians 2) is a central theme of the notes. | Volume's notes and theme articles |
| Creation and the age of the earth | God as Creator affirmed; the age of the earth is not addressed. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Tithing is explained from Torah and commended as a principle; prosperity teaching is absent. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on David Stern's Complete Jewish Bible (1998), which restores Hebrew names and terms; the introduction explains the approach. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Traditional roles reflected in the notes on Ephesians 5; not treated at length. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

## 22. The Evidence Study Bible

**Translation used:** New King James Version
**Publisher:** Bridge-Logos / Living Waters; the official Bible of the *Way of the Master* evangelism ministry and television program.
**Scale:** Thousands of verse-related comments and over 130 informative articles, more than two dozen specifically addressing evolution; extensive material addressing other world religions (Mormonism, Hinduism, Islam, Jehovah's Witnesses, and others) from a comparative-apologetics standpoint; a finalist for the ECPA's 2012 Christian Book Award.

### General editor / commentator
**Ray Comfort** (b. 1949) — a New Zealand-born evangelist and author of more than 100 books, founder and CEO of Living Waters ministries and co-host, with actor Kirk Cameron, of the *Way of the Master* television and teaching ministry. Widely known for a confrontational, apologetics-driven approach to evangelism emphasizing the Ten Commandments as a tool for provoking moral self-examination prior to presenting the Gospel. Endorsements printed with the volume include the apologist Norman Geisler and evangelist Franklin Graham.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical, cross-denominational, with a specific and sustained focus on apologetics and evangelism training rather than a particular denomination's confessional distinctives.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed; the volume's apologetic material argues for the Bible's reliability throughout. |
| Salvation | Not confessionally fixed; contributors span Reformed and Arminian views, and the notes generally present both where a passage is contested. |
| Baptism | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe both believer's and infant baptism as practiced in the contributing traditions without mandating either. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Premillennial by tradition of the editor (Ray Comfort, Living Waters); not emphasized. |
| Spiritual gifts | Not confessionally fixed; the notes neither deny nor emphasize the continuation of the miraculous gifts. |
| Women's ordination | Complementarian by tradition of the editor; not emphasized. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Consistent with broad conservative evangelical Protestantism; Comfort's own ministry has addressed abortion directly in its broader apologetics and evangelism material, though this volume's own specific verse-note wording has not been independently confirmed. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Consistent with broad conservative evangelical Protestantism; the volume's own specific verse-note wording. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | Not verified. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture is the final authority and historic creeds are respected but not treated as a second source; the notes do not appeal to church tradition to settle readings. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and orthodox on the deity and humanity of Christ throughout the notes on the relevant passages (John 1, Philippians 2, Colossians 1). | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Predestination and free will | Not confessionally fixed; the editor's own emphasis on the Law as schoolmaster is compatible with both Reformed and Arminian readings and the notes do not rule. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification; the notes do not teach a distinct second work of grace. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment is central to the volume's evangelism method ("Hell's Best Kept Secret"); the notes press the reality of judgment at Matthew 25 and Revelation 20. | Volume's notes and articles; editor's own published works (Comfort) |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary is honored but not venerated, Jesus' "brothers" (Matthew 12:46) are read as literal siblings, and the notes give no basis for invoking saints or praying for the dead. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday worship assumed; the notes on Colossians 2:16 and Romans 14:5 treat the Sabbath command as fulfilled in Christ rather than binding, and no liturgical style is commended. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission to government (Romans 13); the editor's own public work includes pro-life advocacy (the film *180*, 2011). | Volume's notes; editor's own public work |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | The notes on Acts 10, Galatians 3:28 and Ephesians 2 affirm the unity of all peoples in Christ; racial questions are not otherwise treated as a doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Young-earth creationism and rejection of evolution, argued in more than two dozen articles and in the Genesis notes. | Volume's articles and notes at Genesis 1 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | The notes treat the tithe as Israel's law and New Testament giving as generous and proportionate (2 Corinthians 8–9) without mandating a tenth; prosperity teaching is not present. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the New King James Version; the preface commends a committee translation from the original languages. KJV-onlyism is not a position of the volume. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | The notes on Ephesians 5 present the husband's headship as loving, sacrificial leadership and the wife's submission within mutual submission (5:21); complementarian in tone without pressing the question. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
