## 23. Henry Morris KJV Study Bible

**Translation used:** King James Version
**Publisher:** Master Books (a division of New Leaf Publishing Group, the publishing arm associated with the Institute for Creation Research); the Master Books edition was first printed in March 2012 (the notes and appendices are copyright 1995, 2006 and 2012 by the Henry M. Morris Estate) and was in its sixth printing by July 2018.
**Scale:** Over 10,000 annotations across more than 2,200 pages, with 22 appendices (among them "Global Processes Indicating Recent Creation," "The Universal Flood," "Chronology of the Patriarchs in Genesis" and "A Creationist's Defense of the King James Bible"), an index to major topics in the annotations and a concordance. The Introduction states the volume's aim as grounding "all the great doctrines of the Christian faith" in the literal creation and Flood of Genesis, with notes that "explain the Bible's difficult passages, resolve its alleged contradictions … note its remarkable anticipations of modern science."

### General editor
**Dr. Henry M. Morris** (1918–2006) — Ph.D. in hydraulic engineering, University of Minnesota. Widely known as "the father of modern creation science," Morris co-authored the influential 1961 book *The Genesis Flood* with Dr. John C. Whitcomb, a work credited with substantially reviving young-earth creationism within 20th-century American evangelicalism. Morris founded the Institute for Creation Research (ICR) in 1972 and authored more than 60 apologetics-focused books over his career.

### Denominational leaning
Single-author and self-described: the Introduction says that, rather than take "neutral positions on such controversial doctrines," Morris chose "to express my own convictions," so that "the notes become what one might call Baptistic in ecclesiology, pre-millennial in eschatology, non-charismatic in pneumatology, and moderately Calvinistic in soteriology." The volume is thus conservative, independent-Baptist evangelicalism of the young-earth creationist kind, distinguished among the study Bibles in this section by its sustained scientific apologetics rather than by a denomination's confessional distinctives.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Verbal, plenary inspiration and inerrancy: at 2 Timothy 3:16 "the words, not just the 'thoughts,' are inspired of God," the verse "repudiates the idea of 'partial inspiration' and also that of so-called 'dynamic inspiration,'" and "since God is omniscient, the Scriptures are infallible and free from error of any kind"; the Introduction describes a "deep and settled conviction … that the Bible is indeed the very Word of the living God. Its histories are authentic, its science is accurate," and the notes exist to "remove any doubts about its inerrancy." Read literally throughout, with six-day creation and the global Flood as the foundation. |
| Salvation | By grace through faith alone, "moderately Calvinistic" (Introduction): "even the faith through which we receive God's salvation is the free gift of God" (Ephesians 2:8); we are "justified by his blood," "justified freely by his grace," "justified by faith" in relation to God and only then "justified by works" in relation to men (Romans 5:1); "it is faith in Christ that saves, not faith plus baptism" (Mark 16:16); and God "will ensure that" the work He began in us continues, since "it is neither our responsibility nor within our capability to maintain ourselves in a state of salvation" (Philippians 1:6). God "wants us to be saved" and "whosoever will may come" (1 Timothy 2:4). |
| Baptism | Believer's baptism by immersion, symbolic not saving: "the references to baptism in Romans 6:3-5 clearly imply immersion, as no other mode could picture the death, burial, and resurrection of Christ," and "there is nothing in the baptismal waters themselves … which produces this miracle" (Romans 6:4); "it is not that baptism is required for remission of sins (note, for example, the thief on the cross), but rather that baptism is always inseparably associated in Scripture with true repentance and faith" (Acts 2:38); baptism "normally is to follow immediately upon true repentance and faith," and "those who refuse or ignore this command should examine the reality of their professed faith" (Mark 16:16). Infant baptism is not described. |
| The Lord's Supper | Memorial and symbol: the cup "was not actual blood but 'the fruit of the vine.' Nevertheless, it became an unforgettable symbol of His shed blood, just as the bread represented His broken body" (Matthew 26:28); at John 6:55 "it should be obvious here that Christ is speaking of the symbolic food and drink … It would be cannibalistic, even if it were physically possible, to actually eat and drink His physical flesh and blood"; the cup "represents the New Covenant of God with His people" (1 Corinthians 11:25), and the ordinance's unbroken observance is itself "a perpetual testimony to the authenticity of the Christian faith." |
| Church government | "Baptistic in ecclesiology" (Introduction): bishop, elder, pastor and overseer are one office — "the office of 'bishop' … apparently refers to the same office as that of elder or pastor or overseer" (1 Timothy 3:1; likewise Acts 20:28 and Titus 1:5) — exercised in "their local church," with deacons as the second office; the Acts 14:23 note reads "ordained" as "'stretched out the hand,' thus suggesting an election by show of hands." Pastors must be men (1 Timothy 2:12 note). |
| Eschatology | Premillennial and pretribulational, literal: "the 'thousand years' or millennium … should be taken literally," and Satan "is certainly not 'bound' in this present age" (Revelation 20:2); the "first resurrection will be completed a thousand years before the second" (20:5); 1 Thessalonians 4:17 is "the classic defining passage on the great doctrine of the 'rapture of the saints,'" which occurs "shortly before the tribulation begins" (Revelation 14:4 note; the 2 Thessalonians 2:3 note reads the "falling away" as the departure of the church rather than a final apostasy). |
| Spiritual gifts | Cessationist ("non-charismatic in pneumatology," Introduction): "that which is perfect" "almost certainly refers to the completion of biblical revelation," so that "there is little need any more for miraculous signs, even though many still desire them" (1 Corinthians 13:10); prophecy "would definitely cease when no longer needed … and this would also be true of tongues and knowledge" (12:10); tongues are "the actual languages of the different nations" and "never … meaningless noises" (Acts 2:4; 1 Corinthians 12:10); "our present-day churches do have God's Word in its entirety, and this ought to be sufficient" (14:23). |
| Women's ordination | Men only: "there is no New Testament example of a woman serving as an elder or bishop or pastor of a local church," women "are not to take over the primary teaching ministry from the men (which would clearly include that of the pastor)," and "the intended leadership role for men in the basic institutions of the home and church dates from the creation itself" (1 Timothy 2:12–13), though Paul "is not saying that women should never teach" (Titus 2:4; Lois and Eunice); at 1 Corinthians 14:34 "women were permitted to pray (11:5) or even to prophesy in church, as long as the other rules were observed." |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Opposed: Jeremiah 1:5 illustrates "the truth that the human embryo is fully human from the very moment of conception," against the theory "promoted by modern abortionists"; the leaping of John in the womb "proves beyond question that a babe in a woman's womb is already a true human being … Thus abortion is nothing less than taking a human life" (Luke 1:41); Mark 7:7 names abortion among the "humanistic doctrine[s]" that displace Scripture. | Volume's notes at Jeremiah 1:5, Luke 1:41, Mark 7:7, 1 Corinthians 6:19 |
| Homosexuality | Condemned as sin, not an innate condition: God "calls it abomination. Despite modern theories, it is not a natural condition; it is a learned behavior" (Leviticus 18:22); Romans 1:26 ties "sexual perversion" to "the descent into evolutionary paganism" and notes that it "is quickly becoming accepted—even encouraged—here in America"; at 1 Corinthians 6:11 "any man or woman, no matter how wicked, can be saved and transformed," including from "those sins associated with homosexuality, whose modern practitioners attempt to justify with the pretense that they are born gay." | Volume's notes at Leviticus 18:22, Romans 1:26, 1 Corinthians 6:9–11, Hebrews 13:4 |
| Alcohol | Total abstinence: Ephesians 5:18 "in effect calls for total abstinence from alcohol," since "even a small amount of an intoxicant has a damaging effect on the brain"; "so-called social drinking by Christian believers is difficult to justify" (Proverbs 20:1); Timothy "was normally a total abstainer (and many other Scriptures indicate that this practice should be followed by all sincere Christians)" and 1 Timothy 5:23 at most justifies wine "as a medicine"; "intoxicating drink" is listed with drugs, smoking and gambling as things to "steer clear of" (1 Corinthians 6:12). | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:18, Proverbs 20:1, 23:31, 1 Timothy 5:23, 1 Corinthians 6:12 |
| Divorce and remarriage | Permitted only for sexual sin or an unbeliever's desertion: marriage is "a lifelong union between one man and woman" (Matthew 19:6); "the only allowable grounds for divorce according to Christ is adultery," and "even though adultery or fornication is permissible grounds for divorce and remarriage, it is still better to salvage the marriage if possible" (19:9); when an unbelieving spouse leaves and marries another, "the believer is free to remarry—but, of course, only 'in the Lord'" (1 Corinthians 7:15). "Remarriage after un-biblical divorce" is counted as fornication (6:18). | Volume's notes at Matthew 19:6–9, 1 Corinthians 6:18, 7:15 |
| Contraception | Not addressed: the Genesis 38 notes treat only the levirate custom (38:8), and the Genesis 1:28 note on "be fruitful" concerns filling the earth; the topic index has no entry. | Volume's notes at Genesis 38:8 and 1:28 (none on contraception); index to annotations |
| Gambling | Discouraged as potentially addictive: "the principle given here is to steer clear of any involvement with drugs, intoxicating drink, smoking, gambling, or any other behavior that might become addictive" (1 Corinthians 6:12); the casting of lots is "doubtful" as a practice today "now that we have the complete Bible" (Proverbs 16:33; Acts 1:26). | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:12, Proverbs 16:33, Acts 1:26 |
| Dancing | Not addressed: the notes at Exodus 15:20, 2 Samuel 6:14 and Matthew 14:6 do not comment on the dancing, and the topic index has no entry. | Volume's notes at Exodus 15:20, 2 Samuel 6:14, Matthew 14:6 (none on dancing); index to annotations |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Condemned: "'fornication' can mean any sexual contact outside monogamous marriage. It thus includes not only premarital sex, but also adultery, homosexual acts, incest, remarriage after un-biblical divorce, and sexual acts with animals" (1 Corinthians 6:18); "any other type of sexual relationship is wrong—whether premarital, extramarital, homosexual, incestuous, or anything else" (Hebrews 13:4); fornication "is always contrary to God's will" (1 Thessalonians 4:3). | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:18, Hebrews 13:4, 1 Thessalonians 4:3 |
| War/pacifism | Not pacifist; just war accepted: Romans 13:4 "supports the concept of using 'the sword' by government when necessary. This would confirm the principle of justified capital punishment first established in Genesis 9:6, as well as the concept of warfare when justified" (Romans 13:1); "Jesus did not say, 'Blessed are the pacifists,' but rather, 'Blessed are the peacemakers'" (Matthew 5:9; Colossians 1:20 adds "those who give in to evil just to avoid fighting for the right"). | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1, Matthew 5:9, Colossians 1:20 |
| Death penalty | Affirmed as a standing divine institution: "this establishment of capital punishment, administered judicially by man, has never been changed or withdrawn. It is still God's law today, and forms the basic authorization of the institution of human government" (Genesis 9:6); the Sixth Commandment "obviously refers to murder, not to judicial execution. God Himself had ordained the principle of capital punishment for the murderer" (Exodus 20:13); confirmed at Romans 13:1. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:5–6, Exodus 20:13, Romans 13:1 |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone, read literally: oral apostolic tradition served the churches only until "by the time the last apostle died, it would all have been written and circulated among the churches, and there would be no further need for them to be guided by the oral traditions" (2 Thessalonians 2:15); Mark 7:7–13 is applied to "any displacement of Scripture by some human precept," and Colossians 2:8 warns "against all philosophy." Figures of speech "will be explained in the biblical context itself, not requiring the professional help of specially educated priests or prophets" (Introduction). | Volume's notes at 2 Thessalonians 2:15, Mark 7:7–13, Colossians 2:8; Introduction |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and orthodox: Christ is "both eternal God—the second Person of the three-person Godhead—and perfect man" (Introduction); Matthew 28:19 "explicitly teaches the doctrine of the Trinity—one name, yet three names. The three Persons are each equally and fully God"; "our image" at Genesis 1:26 "implies human likeness to the triune Godhead," and Elohim is "the uni-plural Old Testament name for the divine 'Godhead'" (1:1). The Johannine Comma "could well have been in John's original autograph," though "the doctrine of the Trinity does not depend on this verse" (1 John 5:7). | Volume's notes at Matthew 28:19, Genesis 1:1, 1:26, John 1:1, 1 John 5:7; Introduction |
| Predestination and free will | Election by God's will held together with real human choice ("moderately Calvinistic"): "God chose us by His own will (1:11), not because He could foresee our choice of Him … Nevertheless, from our human perspective, every believer has also made his own willing decision to receive Christ" (Ephesians 1:4); foreknowledge "clearly suggests planning ahead of time, not just knowing ahead of time" (Romans 8:29); "in some mysterious way, our human decisions (for which we bear full responsibility) have also been God's decisions" (9:18); "hated" is "used here only in a relative sense" (9:13). | Volume's notes at Ephesians 1:4–5, Romans 8:29, 9:13–18, 1 Timothy 2:4 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive, with no sinless perfection: sanctification is "both separation to God … and the holy lifestyle of those so separated" (1 Thessalonians 4:3); Romans 7 describes "the conflict between the old and new natures after his conversion," a struggle "with which every believer must struggle" (7:15, 7:25); "the heresy of 'perfectionism'—that is, the claim that our sin-nature has been completely eradicated, so that we no longer commit sin—is self-deception" (1 John 1:8); believers are positionally "sanctified in Christ Jesus" and "still need to diligently follow after peace and holiness in a practical sense" (Hebrews 12:14). | Volume's notes at 1 Thessalonians 4:3, Romans 7:15–25, 1 John 1:8, Hebrews 12:14 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment: "it was Jesus Christ Himself who set forth this doctrine most emphatically of all," and the lost "will exist forever with Satan in the lake of fire" (Matthew 25:41); "the undying worm indicates neverending disintegration and the unquenchable fire indicates eternal suffering" (Mark 9:48); at the second death the lost "will die again physically (or possibly will continue in an eternal state of dying physically)" (Revelation 20:14), and the beast and false prophet are "still imprisoned in the lake of fire over a thousand years after first being cast into it" (20:10). | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:41, Mark 9:43–48, Revelation 20:10–15 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: Mary "recognized that she herself was not sinless and thus needed to obey the rules of purification" (Leviticus 12:2); she "remained a virgin after her marriage to Joseph until after the birth of Jesus. Later, however, she did have other sons" (Matthew 1:25), so "she could not have remained a perpetual virgin" (Mark 3:31), James being "one of the half-brothers of Jesus" (James introduction); at the cross "Jesus was providing here for His mother, not for John" (John 19:27). Prayer for the dead and the veneration of saints are not treated in the notes read. | Volume's notes at Leviticus 12:2, Matthew 1:25, Mark 3:31, John 19:27; introduction to James |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday as the Lord's Day: "'The Lord's Day' most likely refers to the first day of the week, our modern Sunday," on which believers commemorate "both Christ's finished work of creation (Ge 2:1-3) and His finished work of redemption" by "observing their Sabbath Day … on the day of His resurrection" (Revelation 1:10; Acts 20:7; 1 Corinthians 16:2); believers "should not allow ourselves to be intimidated by … legalistic stipulations (e.g., 'Sabbath Days')" (Colossians 2:16). Style and liturgy are not addressed. | Volume's notes at Revelation 1:10, Acts 20:7, 1 Corinthians 16:2, Colossians 2:16 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission to government, instituted by God at Genesis 9:6, except where "human laws demanded disobedience to God's laws" (Romans 13:1; Acts 5:29: "God's commands take priority, but they should be His commands, not our extensions of those commands"); "we must regard human ordinances as divine ordinances and submit to them as unto God, unless they contradict God's written Word" (1 Peter 2:13). Appendix 20, "The Creationist Faith of Our Founding Fathers," laments that "it is now considered unconstitutional to acknowledge God in any way at all in the public schools." | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1, Acts 5:29, 1 Peter 2:13; Appendix 20 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | One human race: "the concept of 'race' has no basis in Scripture; all men are descended from Adam, through Noah, and thus all are members of only one race—the human race. The term 'race,' as ordinarily used, is strictly an evolutionary concept" (Acts 17:26; likewise Genesis 10:32: "nations, tribes, tongues, peoples, and families, but these are not races"); the curse of Canaan is not a curse on Ham's descendants generally, for "neither the Hamitic nations in general nor the Canaanitic nations in particular have ever been" slaves of slaves (Genesis 9:25). | Volume's notes at Acts 17:26, Genesis 9:25, 10:32, Galatians 3:28 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Literal six-day creation, recent, with a global Flood — "an uncompromising commitment to literal biblical creationism" (Introduction): the first "day" is "specifically defined by God as 'the light' in the cyclical succession of light and darkness which has, ever since, constituted a solar day," so "the days of creation week were of the same duration as any natural solar day" (Genesis 1:5), a point Exodus 20:11 "settles once and for all"; "the Bible … indicates the universe to be only a few thousand years old" (Appendix 5), and the chronology of Genesis 5 and 11 is tabulated "assuming no gaps" (Appendix 10). The notes attack "evolutionism" at every turn. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1–31, Exodus 20:11; Appendices 5, 10 and 22; Introduction |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | No tithe mandate; prosperity teaching absent: "tithing as such is nowhere commanded in the New Testament, although this verse is often used as such a commandment" (Matthew 23:23); Malachi 3:10 "applies in context only to the one treasury in the one house of God in Jerusalem," and the New Testament "contains no references to this passage," though "God's people have the responsibility to support the work of God, rather than to heap luxuries on themselves"; giving is to be "regular and systematic" (1 Corinthians 16:2). | Volume's notes at Matthew 23:23, Malachi 3:8–10, 1 Corinthians 16:2 |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | KJV preferred, Textus Receptus favored, but not KJV-only: Appendix 21, "A Creationist's Defense of the King James Bible," calls the KJV "the most beautiful, the most powerful, and (I strongly believe) the most reliable of any that we have or probably ever will have, until Christ returns," while allowing that "its use is not a test of salvation or spirituality" and that "one can certainly find the way of salvation in the King James at least as easily as in any other"; the New Testament introduction sides with "many conservative scholars" who "still favor the Textus Receptus" over the Westcott–Hort and Nestle texts. | Appendix 21; Introduction to the New Testament |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Complementarian: "the man is not superior to the woman, as God is not superior to Christ, being of the same essence. However, there is divine order in the relative functions," and God "ordain[ed] a divine order in the functions of the family" (1 Corinthians 11:3), women having a "divinely established subordinate role (subordinate in role, not in importance or essence)" (11:7); the wife's submission and the husband's "deeply sacrificial love" follow "from the original created purpose for men and women" (Ephesians 5:22); child training is "primarily the responsibility of the father" (6:4). | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 11:3–7, Ephesians 5:22, 6:4, 1 Timothy 2:13 |

---

## 24. Jonathan Edwards Study Bible

**Translation used:** New King James Version
**Publisher:** Thomas Nelson, first published 2025.
**Scale:** Study notes, doctrinal insights, and personal applications drawn directly from Jonathan Edwards's own writings and sermons; full-page essays on Edwards's theology, life, and role in the First Great Awakening; an introduction to Edwards's own "Resolutions" and his "miscellanies" note-taking system, with space for readers to develop their own; and the complete texts of two of Edwards's sermons, including his most famous, "Sinners in the Hands of an Angry God."

### General editor
**Dr. Matthew Everhard**, a scholar of Jonathan Edwards's life and theology, served as General Editor. The volume draws its content from the writings of **Jonathan Edwards** (1703–1758), the colonial American theologian, philosopher, and Congregationalist pastor widely regarded as one of the most important and original theologians in American history, best known for his central role in the First Great Awakening and for sermons including "Sinners in the Hands of an Angry God."

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and Reformed, reflecting Edwards's own historic Congregationalist and broadly Calvinist theological commitments; the volume's stated organizing theme is Edwards's conviction that true and lasting joy is found only in God, rather than a systematic verse-by-verse doctrinal commentary in the manner of the MacArthur Study Bible or ESV Study Bible. As the newest entry in this section (2025), it has not yet had time to establish the kind of broad market presence several other entries in this list have achieved over decades.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Inerrancy affirmed explicitly, in the terms of the Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy (1978). |
| Salvation | Reformed/Calvinist: unconditional election, effectual grace, and perseverance of the saints, stated explicitly in the notes on Romans 9, Ephesians 1, and John 6. |
| Baptism | Edwards's own Congregationalist paedobaptist practice; the volume's thematic notes do not argue the question. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not confessionally fixed; the notes describe the ordinance as a memorial and means of fellowship with Christ, in line with the broad evangelical range rather than one sacramental theology. |
| Church government | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes describe New Testament church offices (elders, deacons) without endorsing a particular polity. |
| Eschatology | Edwards was postmillennial; the volume's notes are thematic (joy in God) and do not press an eschatological system. |
| Spiritual gifts | Edwards held the extraordinary gifts had ceased while defending genuine revival affections; the volume follows his writings. |
| Women's ordination | Not addressed; Edwards's own era assumed male ministry, and the volume's thematic notes do not engage the modern question. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture alone as the final authority, with Edwards's Reformed and Puritan heritage shaping the reading; the Westminster and Savoy confessions are the implicit standard. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Trinitarian and Chalcedonian; Edwards's *Discourse on the Trinity* and sermons on Christ's excellency are drawn on. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Predestination and free will | Confessionally Reformed: unconditional election and the bondage of the will, from Edwards's *Freedom of the Will* and sermons on Romans 9. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Sanctification and holiness | Progressive sanctification evidenced by "religious affections"; Edwards's *Religious Affections* is a principal source of the notes. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Eternal conscious punishment, in the vivid terms of Edwards's *Sinners in the Hands of an Angry God* and related sermons. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Protestant: no veneration of Mary or the saints and no prayer for the dead; Edwards regarded the papacy as antichrist, a view the volume reports as his. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Sunday as the Christian Sabbath, strictly kept, from Edwards's sermons on the Sabbath; Puritan plain worship. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Church and state / political engagement | Edwards's colonial context: civil government ordained by God, with the church's primary task spiritual; the volume does not apply this to modern politics. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Edwards held enslaved persons, a fact the volume's biographical material acknowledges; his theology of the unity of humanity and his mission to Native Americans are also presented. | Volume's biographical essays |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Edwards assumed a recent creation in the terms of his age; the volume presents his typological reading of creation rather than a position on the age of the earth. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Not addressed as doctrine beyond Edwards's sermons on charity; prosperity teaching absent. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Published in the NKJV; Edwards himself used the KJV. The volume makes no claim for one translation. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Edwards's own household and sermons reflect traditional male headship; the volume presents this historically rather than prescriptively. | Edwards's own writings as excerpted in the volume |
Given this volume's specific devotional and historical-theological orientation around Edwards's own writings, rather than a systematic modern doctrinal or pastoral commentary, direct notes on contemporary questions such as abortion, homosexuality, or alcohol comparable to the more directly application-focused study Bibles in this section have not been identified.

| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Opposed; the notes on Psalm 139 and Exodus 21 reflect the uniform evangelical view that human life bears God's image from conception. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Homosexuality | Same-sex sexual conduct treated as sinful in the notes on Romans 1 and 1 Corinthians 6, with pastoral care distinguished from approval. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Alcohol | Moderate use not treated as sinful; drunkenness is. The notes do not counsel abstinence. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Divorce and remarriage | The notes treat marriage as intended to be permanent and read Matthew 19:9 and 1 Corinthians 7:15 as permitting divorce for sexual immorality or abandonment, the majority evangelical view; remarriage after such a divorce is generally regarded as permissible. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Contraception | Not addressed; the evangelical tradition the volume reflects generally treats contraception within marriage as a matter of conscience. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Gambling | Not addressed directly; the tradition generally discourages gambling as poor stewardship and a form of covetousness, without treating it as a matter of doctrine. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Dancing | Not addressed; no position taken. | Not addressed by the volume; no confessional position taken |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Sexual relations are reserved for marriage; the notes on 1 Corinthians 6-7 and Hebrews 13:4 reflect the uniform evangelical position. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a doctrine; the tradition generally holds a just-war rather than pacifist position, and the notes on Romans 13 treat governmental use of force as legitimate. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a doctrine; the notes on Genesis 9:6 and Romans 13:4 generally treat capital punishment as within the state's legitimate authority, without advocating it. | General pattern of the editorial tradition, not independently documented in the notes |

## 25. Haydock's Catholic Bible and Commentary

**Translation used:** Douay-Rheims Bible
**Publisher:** Originally published by Thomas Haydock (publisher) beginning 1811-1814, in installments affordable to ordinary Catholic families under the era's Penal Laws restricting Catholic publishing in England; still in print today through several independent Catholic publishers, most commonly reproducing the expanded 1859 edition.
**Scale:** Extensive verse-by-verse commentary drawing on over 200 sources, chiefly the Church Fathers and Doctors; widely regarded as the most popular English Catholic Bible of the 19th century on both sides of the Atlantic, though its market presence today is considerably smaller and concentrated among traditionalist Catholics specifically, which is reflected in this entry's placement toward the end of this list despite its substantial historical significance.

### General editor / commentator
**Rev. George Leo Haydock** (1774–1849) — an English Catholic priest from an old Recusant family (a family that maintained Catholic practice through the era of English anti-Catholic Penal Laws), himself a descendant of the martyred 16th-century "seminary priest" Blessed George Haydock. Compiled the commentary that bears his name, drawing on patristic and later Catholic scholarship, for publication by his brother Thomas Haydock.

### Denominational leaning
Explicitly and traditionally Catholic — see the Catholic Church entry in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations" for the shared doctrinal framework, alongside the Ignatius Catholic Study Bible and Catholic Study Bible/NABRE entries elsewhere in this section for two more recent alternatives; this volume is distinguished from those by its use of the historic Douay-Rheims translation and its origin in 19th-century, rather than contemporary, Catholic scholarship, giving it particular ongoing appeal among traditionalist Catholics.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Scripture inspired and "profitable," but not sufficient alone: at 2 Timothy 3:14–16 the notes hold "with Catholics, that all Scripture is profitable; but not with Protestants, that Scripture alone is necessary and sufficient," the rule of faith requiring the New Testament "taking along with it the traditions of the apostles and the interpretation of the Church" (Challoner) — the Church's teaching long before *Dei Verbum* (1965) restated it. |
| Salvation | Grace and faith working through charity, never faith alone: "St. Paul does not pretend that the virtue of faith alone will justify and save a man" (Romans 3:20 note), which speaks rather of "a faith working by charity" (3:30); James "shew[s] … that faith alone will not save any one" (James 2:14), faith being shown "only by good works" (2:18); "that only is true faith, which does not contradict in works what is believed in words" (Mark 16:16, citing St. Gregory). Neither Calvinist nor Arminian categories apply. |
| Baptism | Regenerative and necessary, including for infants: at John 3:5 the Fathers, "especially St. Augustine," are said to "prove the necessity of giving baptism to infants" from Christ's words, "by Christ's adding water, is excluded a metaphorical baptism," and the new birth is "a spiritual regeneration in God" (citing St. John Chrysostom). The mode (pouring or immersion) is not discussed in the notes read. |
| The Lord's Supper | Transubstantiation; the Eucharist as the true Body and Blood of Christ and a sacrifice. The John 6 notes say Christ "promiseth to give his body and blood to be eaten" (v. 52) and repeated himself "to remove all metaphorical interpretation of his words" (v. 55), words that would make no sense if the sacrament were "nothing but a bit of bread" — the figurative reading being that of "sectarists." |
| Church government | Episcopal and papal: at Matthew 16:18–19 Peter is "constituted the rock upon which, as a foundation, I will build my Church," Christ promising "that he and his successors should be to the end"; the keys express "the supreme power and prerogative of the prince of the apostles," and "neither idolatry, heresy, nor any pernicious error whatsoever shall at any time prevail over the Church of Christ" (Challoner); at 1 Timothy 3 "priests were comprehended under the name of bishops" and the Church "can never uphold error, nor bring in corruptions, superstition, or idolatry" (3:15). |
| Eschatology | Amillennial in effect: the note at Apocalypse 20 takes the thousand years as "meaning a long time" that "may signify all the time from Christ's first coming" to the end of the world, makes the first resurrection the soul's, and calls a literal earthly reign "the opinion of those called Millenarians" — an "error, and heresy" born of a "false exposition of these thousand years"; purgatory, particular and general judgment are assumed throughout. |
| Spiritual gifts | Not cessationist: at 1 Corinthians 14:5 Paul "is far from condemning the gift of speaking tongues, in proper circumstances," objecting only to its "indiscreet use, or the abuse of it," and at 12:4–7 Calmet notes that "St. Justin Martyr, St. Irenæus, and Origen bear testimony" that "these special gifts of the Holy Ghost were not unusual in their time"; to prophesy is "to declare or expound the mysteries of faith" (14:1, Challoner). The notes read do not say the gifts ceased. |
| Women's ordination | Reserved to men: at 1 Timothy 2:11–12 the notes cite Tertullian, "We do not permit a woman to teach, to baptize," and Chrysostom, "Let her now be content to remain in silence, and subjection to man"; at 1 Corinthians 14:34 women are "not [to] speak at all in public Church-meetings" (Witham); the "women" of 1 Timothy 3:11 are deaconesses, "women charged with the assistance, and sometimes with the instruction, of persons of their own sex" — the Church's teaching later restated in *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis* (1994). |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Scripture and Tradition together: at 2 Timothy 3:14 the notes hold "with Catholics, that all Scripture is profitable; but not with Protestants, that Scripture alone is necessary and sufficient," Challoner adding that the rule of faith requires the New Testament "taking along with it the traditions of the apostles and the interpretation of the Church"; at 2 Thessalonians 2:14 "the apostle did not deliver all things that were to be believed, by writing," so the unwritten traditions are "no less than the writings of the apostles ... deserving of faith." The commentary is polemically Catholic against Protestant *sola scriptura*. | Volume's notes at 2 Timothy 3:14–17 and 2 Thessalonians 2:14 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Nicene and Chalcedonian: at John 1:1 "the eternity and the divinity of the second Person are incontrovertibly established" — "it was never true to say, he was not, as the Arians blasphemed"; all creatures are "equally produced, created, and preserved by the three divine Persons" (1:3); the Word was made flesh "by a true and physical union of his divine person … to our human nature" (1:14). | Volume's notes at John 1:1, 1:3, 1:14 |
| Predestination and free will | Grace primary and free will cooperating: the Romans 9 notes make election "not upon account of any works or merits in men, but only to be attributed to the mercy and goodness of God" (vv. 15–16), hold that God hardens "not by causing their malice, but by not giving them the free gift of his grace" (v. 18), and warn that reading the potter image to deny free will is contrary to Catholic doctrine (v. 21) — post-Tridentine teaching rejecting the Reformed doctrine of reprobation without naming Calvin. | Volume's notes at Romans 9:10–21 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Justification as inherent righteousness increased by good works and the sacraments; purgatory completes it. | Catholic teaching, stated in the notes |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Hell is real and eternal: the Matthew 25 notes distinguish "the pain of loss" ("depart from me") from "the pain of sense" ("eternal fire"), reject Origen's view that the torments end, and argue that doubting eternal punishment "must encourage vice and embolden the sinner"; the commentary holds a narrower view of salvation outside the Church than the modern Catechism. | Volume's notes at Matthew 25:41, 46 |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Marian doctrine, the intercession of the saints and prayer for the dead are affirmed and defended at length: at Luke 1:28 "full of grace" is kept against the Protestant "highly favoured" (and Erasmus's "gracious" and Beza's "freely beloved") as "the greatest share of divine graces granted to any creature"; at 2 Maccabees 12:46 "this text is so clear, that our adversaries judge it best to deny the book to be canonical"; and at 1 Corinthians 3:15 Calmet's note calls the fire "the fire of purgatory, according to the Fathers, and all Catholic divines." | Volume's notes at Luke 1:28, 2 Maccabees 12:42–46, 1 Corinthians 3:13–15 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | The Latin Mass as the Church's worship; the notes read John 6 and 1 Corinthians 11 eucharistically — at 1 Corinthians 11:27 Challoner says the verse "demonstrates the real presence of the body and blood of Christ," and at 11:20 he speaks of "the eucharistic sacrifice and sacrament" — and at 11:28 treat drinking from the chalice as said "not ... by way of command, but by way of allowance," depending on Church practice. | Volume's notes at John 6 and 1 Corinthians 11:20–28 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Submission to lawful authority: the Romans 13:1 note holds that "every one (even priests and bishops, says St. Chrysostom) must be subject and obedient to princes, even to heathens," so that "to resist them, is to resist God" — a commentary written by an English Catholic while the penal laws were being relaxed, though the notes themselves do not mention them. | Volume's note at Romans 13:1; historical context |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Not addressed; the Genesis 9:25–27 notes treat the curse as falling on Chanaan and the Chanaanites, who "perished for their own wickedness," and read Chanaan allegorically as a figure of heretics, without extending the curse to other peoples descended from Cham. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:25–27; no confessional position taken |
| Creation and the age of the earth | A literal reading of Genesis 1 with a recent creation, as was standard in 1811: the Genesis 1:1 note dates the verse "Year before Christ 4004" and speaks of the ordered world arising "in the space of six days," without discussing the length of the days; the Church had not yet addressed modern geology. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1, 1:11, 1:14 |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Not treated as a Christian obligation in the notes read: at Hebrews 7:4 Abraham "of his own accord, paid tithes to this priest of all the chief things he had," proving Melchisedech's superiority, and at Matthew 23:23 "the tithes of these small things are not found in the law"; the notes say nothing about tithes owed to the Church. Prosperity teaching is absent. | Volume's notes at Hebrews 7:4 and Matthew 23:23; the claim that tithes are owed to the Church under natural and divine law is not in the notes read |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the Douay-Rheims (Challoner revision), translated from the Latin Vulgate, which the notes defend as the Church's authentic text against the Protestant versions. KJV-onlyism has no standing. | Volume's own base translation and preface |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | Husband as head of the wife (Ephesians 5), in the terms of the period; the notes at Ephesians 5:23–24 dwell on Christ as head and saviour of his mystical body and on the Church's obedience to Christ rather than on the marital roles themselves. | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:23–24 |
Reflecting its early-19th-century original composition, Haydock's commentary does not address these specific contemporary questions the way modern study Bibles do; its underlying doctrinal framework is nonetheless consistent with the historic Catholic teaching described in the Catholic Church entry in "Major U.S. Christian Denominations," from which the Church's own modern-era statements on these specific questions (cited in the Ignatius Catholic Study Bible and Catholic Study Bible/NABRE entries above) themselves develop.

| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Treated as homicide: the Exodus 21:22–23 note holds that "to destroy the life of either" mother or formed child "was punished with death," that since "the precise time when the soul begins to animate the body" is uncertain whoever wilfully causes a miscarriage after conception "would expose himself to incur the guilt of murder," and quotes Tertullian, "Homicidii festinatio est prohibere nasci," adding that "the Romans punished it with death." | Volume's note at Exodus 21:22–23 |
| Homosexuality | Condemned: the sins of Romans 1:24–27 are "the foulest, most shameful, and unnatural sins of uncleanness" (Witham), into which God let the pagans fall "not by being author of their sins, but by withdrawing his grace" (Challoner); the sin of Sodom is "contrary to hospitality and the law of nature" (Genesis 19:7), the Sodomites boldly proclaiming "their infamous design" (19:5). | Volume's notes at Romans 1:24–27 and Genesis 19:4–7 |
| Alcohol | Wine permitted; drunkenness sinful: the Cana note calls Christ's first miracle "a kind of transubstantiation in changing water into wine" and, since Christ, his mother and the disciples were present, insists "when men have well drank" need not be rendered "drunk" (John 2); drunkards are among those who "shall not possess the kingdom of God" (1 Corinthians 6:12 note). | Volume's notes at John 2 and 1 Corinthians 6:12; Ephesians 5:18 has no note |
| Divorce and remarriage | A valid consummated sacramental marriage is indissoluble: the Matthew 19:9 note allows separation for adultery only ("divorce is in no case admitted but in that of adultery") and holds that "the separated parties cannot contract a second marriage without again committing adultery," a doctrine "universally received" and "confirmed in the general council of Trent"; remarriage while a spouse lives requires a declaration of nullity. | Volume's note at Matthew 19:9; Catholic teaching |
| Contraception | Onan's act condemned as "an abominable and unnatural vice": the Genesis 38 notes describe him as "sinning against nature" (38:7) and wish that "this abominable and unnatural vice would sooner perhaps be eradicated" if such offenders were punished as he was (38:10) — the traditional basis of the Church's teaching, though contraception as such is not discussed. | Volume's notes at Genesis 38:7, 38:10 |
| Gambling | Not in itself contrary to justice, but sinful when it deprives one of what is needed for one's own or others' needs, or becomes enslaving (Catechism 2413). | Catholic teaching |
| Dancing | Not addressed: the notes on the dance of Herodias' daughter (Matthew 14:6–8) concern Herod's oath ("wicked promises and wicked oaths are not binding"), not dancing; no position taken. | Volume's notes at Matthew 14 (none on dancing) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Fornication excludes from the kingdom: the 1 Corinthians 6 notes repeat that "unjust dealers, fornicators, drunkards, shall not possess the kingdom of God" and that "all things are lawful" cannot be taken "in the obvious sense" since Paul will not be "a slave to such passions" (6:8–12). | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:8–12 |
| War/pacifism | Just war, not pacifism: at Luke 3:14 the Baptist "knew that such as engage in war, are not murderers, but ministers of the law; not avengers of injuries, but defenders of the public weal" (citing St. Augustine, *Contra Faustum* 22) — had he thought soldiering wrong "he would have told the soldiers to abandon it"; what is condemned is "cruelty, revenge, implacable dispositions, and lust of power"; and at Romans 13 rulers are to be obeyed "even to heathens." | Volume's notes at Luke 3:14 and Romans 13:1 |
| Death penalty | Permitted: the Genesis 9:6 notes say "thou shalt not kill" "admits of exceptions, and forbids killing by private authority, or out of revenge," with Menochius calling it "just to inflict such a punishment on the offender" and Calmet concluding that judges may execute murderers — the Church's teaching of the commentary's era, not the 2018 revision of the Catechism. | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:6 (Haydock, Menochius, Calmet) |

---

## 26. New Oxford Annotated Bible with the Apocrypha (NRSV)

**Translation used:** New Revised Standard Version with the Apocrypha (1989), the National Council of Churches' revision of the RSV; the volume prints the NRSV committee's "To the Reader."
**Publisher:** Oxford University Press; the edition read here is the Fully Revised Fifth Edition (2018; study material © 1962, 1965, 1973, 1977, 1991, 2001, 2007, 2010, 2018), successor to the *Oxford Annotated Bible* (RSV, 1962; Herbert G. May and Bruce M. Metzger), the *New Oxford Annotated Bible with the Apocrypha, Expanded Edition* (RSV, 1977) and the NRSV editions of 1991, 2001 and 2010. Subtitled "An Ecumenical Study Bible."
**Scale:** Introductions and running annotations for every book of the Hebrew Bible, the Apocryphal/Deuterocanonical books (including 3 and 4 Maccabees, 1 and 2 Esdras, the Prayer of Manasseh and Psalm 151) and the New Testament; essays introducing each canonical division; some 380,000 words of end-matter essays ("The Canons of the Bible," "Textual Criticism," "Translations of the Bible into English," "Christian Interpretation in the Premodern Era," "Contemporary Methods in Biblical Study" and others), tables of rulers, weights, calendar and parallel texts, a glossary, an index to the study materials and fourteen colour maps. The preface says the annotations "are just that, notes rather than paraphrase or commentary."

### General editor
**Michael D. Coogan**, with associate editors **Marc Z. Brettler**, **Carol A. Newsom** and **Pheme Perkins**. Some sixty contributors are named by book — among them David Carr (Genesis), Carol Meyers (Exodus), Jeffrey Stackert (Leviticus), Bernard M. Levinson (Deuteronomy), Thomas Römer (Kings), Richard J. Clifford (Psalms), David J. A. Clines (Job), Marvin A. Sweeney (Isaiah), Amy-Jill Levine (Tobit), John J. Collins (3 Maccabees), J. R. C. Cousland (Matthew), Suzanne Watts Henderson (Mark), Marion L. Soards (Luke), Colleen Conway (John), Ann Jervis (Romans), Laurence L. Welborn (1 Corinthians), Margaret M. Mitchell (the Pastorals), Carolyn Osiek (Philippians), David A. deSilva (Hebrews, 4 Maccabees), M. Eugene Boring (1 Peter) and Jean-Pierre Ruiz (Revelation). The editors "have recruited contributors from a wide diversity of backgrounds and of scholarly approaches," Jewish and Christian; "each contribution was read in its entirety by at least three of the editors."

### Denominational leaning
Academic and ecumenical rather than confessional. The preface states the method: "The editors recognize that no single interpretation or approach is sufficient… On a great number of issues there is a consensus among scholars, and the contributors have been encouraged to present such consensus when it exists. Where it has broken down… alternatives are mentioned"; dates are given BCE/CE because BC/AD "imply a Christian view of the status of Jesus of Nazareth," and the first part is called "the Hebrew Bible." The notes describe what the text meant to its first writers and readers, name sources (P, H, the Yahwist), flag later additions (John 7:53–8:11, 1 Corinthians 14:33b–36) and distinguish Paul from "the author" of the Pastorals. They do not take doctrinal positions, and where a Christian doctrine is mentioned it is usually to note that the text does not teach it. Every cell below therefore records what the notes say about the text, and "not addressed" means the notes say nothing on the question.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Historical-critical, with no doctrine of Scripture stated: 2 Timothy 3:15–16's "sacred writings" are "the Jewish scriptures (the Hebrew Bible in the ancient Greek version, the Septuagint), which Christians will later call the Old Testament," with no comment on "inspired"; the words "inerrant" and "infallible" do not occur. The Pentateuch introduction and the Genesis notes assign the text to "a Priestly source" and "an earlier non-Priestly primeval history"; the Pastorals are by "the author" writing in Paul's name ("whether he is dead or alive, the letter takes Paul's place," 1 Timothy 3:14–15). |
| Salvation | Described, not adjudicated: *hilastērion* "can be translated as 'place of atonement,' 'atonement,' 'expiation,' 'propitiation,' 'sacrifice of reconciliation,' 'sacrifice'" (Romans 3:25); "the Gk can also be understood as referring to Christ's own faith… If this reading is chosen, it does not eliminate the need for human faith as well" (3:22); "by grace … saved, deliverance to a new life is due solely to God's mercy and benevolence" and "works, individual achievement, not 'works of the law'" (Ephesians 2:5–9); James's "works, or 'deeds,' a translation that avoids overstating differences between James and Paul," and "James rejects separating faith and works, insisting on a both/and relationship" (James 2:14–18). |
| Baptism | Reported as the early church's rite of entry, with its theology left to the text: "baptizing becomes the rite of entry" (Matthew 28:19); "here the gift of the Spirit follows baptism, but there is variety elsewhere" (Acts 2:38); "by baptism the believer is united with Christ… and so with the one death that ended in life" (Romans 6:3–5); "the significance of circumcision is here transferred to Christian baptism" (Colossians 2:11–12); at Acts 16:15 "she and her household were baptized, dependents followed the head of the household in religious matters." Infant baptism, mode and regeneration are not discussed as doctrines. |
| The Lord's Supper | Commemorative in the notes' description: "early Christians celebrated a communal meal commemorating Jesus's death" and "my body, the metaphor refers to both Jesus's own flesh and the 'body' of his followers" (Mark 14:22–25); "the action is commemorative" (Luke 22:19); Luke's two cups show "variation among early Christians in the way they observed the 'Lord's supper'"; John 6:51–58 is "a much debated passage that may have allusions to the Eucharist. Others read it in the context of ancient Greek hero cults"; "discerning the body? Probably the gathered church as Christ's body" (1 Corinthians 11:29). Real presence and transubstantiation are not mentioned. |
| Church government | Offices described as developing: Philippians 1:1's "bishops and deacons, the alternate translation overseers and helpers is preferable. These functions are not yet the church offices that they will later become"; by the Pastorals they are "already established ecclesial offices (contrast 1 Cor 12.28; Phil 1.1)" within "God's church… envisioned as an orderly patriarchal Greco-Roman household" (1 Timothy 3); "elders … bishop, apparently regarded as synonymous or at least overlapping" (Titus 1:5–7); the keys "bequeath Peter the authority to oversee admission to the church (cf. 18.18)" (Matthew 16:19). No polity is commended. |
| Eschatology | The millennium read as symbol, the Second Coming as the writers' expectation: "the period of a thousand years is symbolic… suggesting a temporary time of righteousness and tranquility before the final eschatological consummation"; "this passage, which gave rise to Christian millennialism, suggests an interim messianic kingdom" (Revelation 20:1–6); 144,000 "has been interpreted variously as… the faithful remnant of Israel; the church; the martyrs… all the redeemed" (7:4); "the purpose of this brief description is not to be predictive of the last days but to encourage the Thessalonians," and "the writers anticipate that these events will occur within their own lifetime" (1 Thessalonians 4:13–18); Matthew 24 "probably combine[s] both perspectives," AD 70 and the parousia. |
| Spiritual gifts | First-century practice described: "Paul's encomium of love serves to devalue the gift of tongues"; "tongues of angels refers to glossolalia" (1 Corinthians 13:1); "Paul shares with Plato, Philo, and others the belief in a higher form of Spirit-inspired speech directed to the divine, but incomprehensible to normal human intelligence," and "I would like all of you to speak in tongues, reveals the high value that was set upon this gift" (14:2, 5). Nothing on cessation or continuation today. |
| Women's ordination | The restrictive texts attributed to later hands, women's leadership affirmed as historical fact: 1 Timothy 2:9–15 — "the author wishes to show Paul unambiguously opposed to women holding leadership and teaching positions on the grounds that it threatens conventional domestic order… Paul had left an ambiguous legacy on women: while he upheld a gender distinction in worship (1 Cor 11.2–16), Rom 16.1–7 shows he respected women as deacons and apostles"; 2:15 is "a soteriology… at odds with Paul's justification by faith"; 1 Corinthians 14:33b–36 — "many scholars regard this passage as a later non-Pauline addition… it contradicts the assumption of 11.5 that women will pray and prophesy in the assembly"; "more interesting than what Paul argues for is what he does not argue against — the Spirit-filled leadership of women" (11:2–16); Phoebe "had a leadership role in the church at Cenchreae" (Romans 16:1); 1 Timothy 3:11 "women deacons… the short treatment may be due to the author's anxiety about female leadership." Ordination as a church question is not raised. |

### Ethical and lifestyle positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Abortion | Not addressed: Exodus 21:22–27 is explained as the *lex talionis*, "a principle of fair treatment of assailants and not necessarily a literal prescription," noting "compensation is sometimes acceptable (vv. 22,26,27)"; Jeremiah 1:5 is a call narrative; the word occurs only at 1 Corinthians 15:8 ("one untimely born refers to an abortion or miscarriage"). | Volume's notes at Exodus 21:12–32, Jeremiah 1:4–10, Luke 1:39–56, 1 Corinthians 15:8 (no position stated) |
| Homosexuality | Historical and lexical, with the modern question raised and left open: at Romans 1:26 "whether Paul is talking about heterosexual people engaging in homosexual acts (that is, acts that are 'unnatural' for heterosexuals) is unclear. It is uncertain whether Paul or his Jewish contemporaries ever considered the possibility of same-gender sexual orientation"; 1 Corinthians 6:9's *malakoi* "were boys and men sodomized by other males, while sodomites (Gk 'arsenokoitai'…) were men who exercised the dominant role, pederasts"; Leviticus 18:22 — "among the biblical legal corpora, only H contains a prohibition against same-sex intercourse. The prohibition may be due to its nonreproductive nature"; Genesis 19 — "the main issue here is hospitality… the men of the city who wish to rape (know) the guests." | Volume's notes at Romans 1:18–32, 1 Corinthians 6:9, Leviticus 18:1–30, Genesis 19:1–11, 1 Timothy 1:9–10 |
| Alcohol | Not addressed as a question; the one evaluative note is "Proverbs condemns drunkenness but not drinking alcohol" (Proverbs 20:1). | Volume's notes at Proverbs 20:1, John 2:1–12 (no position stated) |
| Divorce and remarriage | Text-critical, no counsel: "The Pharisaic schools of Hillel and Shammai both permitted divorce but differed over the appropriate grounds"; "except for unchastity, this exception clause has been added by Matthew; see also 5.32" (Matthew 19:3–9); Mark 10:9 — "the prohibition of divorce reflects the harmony commanded in 9.50. Elsewhere, the NT takes a more permissive stance on divorce"; 10:11–12 — "Jesus introduces parity between men and women in a world that traditionally restricted women's right to divorce"; in 1 Corinthians 7 "marriage as well as other marks of social status are relativized in importance by the eschatological 'calling.'" | Volume's notes at Matthew 5:21–48, 19:1–12, Mark 10:1–12, 1 Corinthians 7:1–40, Malachi 2:10–16 |
| Contraception | Not addressed: Onan's death "is attributed to his refusal to perform this duty of impregnating Er's widow, Tamar, probably by coitus interruptus (rather than 'onanism,' masturbation)" (Genesis 38:8–10). | Volume's note at Genesis 38:8–10 (no position stated) |
| Gambling | Not addressed: the word does not occur in the study materials. | Whole-text search (no entry) |
| Dancing | Not addressed as a question; Exodus 15:20's dancing is "a woman's performance genre… for victory celebrations." | Volume's note at Exodus 15:20–21 (no position stated) |
| Premarital sex/cohabitation | Paul's position described: "Against the temptation to immorality, Paul counsels monogamy and fidelity in marriage" (1 Corinthians 7:2); 6:12–20 is headed "Christians going to prostitutes," where Paul "counters by affirming God's power over the body" and "the fornicator sins against himself… is an idea with Stoic parallels"; 1 Thessalonians 4:4's "vessel" "could refer to a man's wife or his penis. In any case, the general sense is that the Thessalonians preserve sexual decorum." No contemporary application. | Volume's notes at 1 Corinthians 6:12–20, 7:1–9, 1 Thessalonians 4:1–8, Hebrews 13:1–21 |
| War/pacifism | Not addressed as a question: Deuteronomy 20 gives "rules for waging holy war… fought to create a covenantal community organized by divine law… not as a war of imperial gain," with the Moabite Stone showing "similar theologies of holy war were shared by some of Israel's neighbors"; Jericho's *ḥerem* is "a ritual act"; Luke 22:38 is "against armed resistance"; Romans 13:1 — "the character of the God revealed in Jesus Christ contextualizes subjection to the governing authorities." Just war and pacifism are not named. | Volume's notes at Deuteronomy 20:1–20, Joshua 6:6–27, Matthew 5:38–42, Luke 22:35–38, Romans 13:1–7 (no position stated) |
| Death penalty | Not addressed as a question: Exodus 21:12–17 gives "absolute rules for capital offenses," with the "important distinction… between deliberate and accidental homicide"; John 7:53–8:11 "is not part of the major manuscript tradition for the Gospel, and is likely a later addition"; Romans 13:4 is not glossed. | Volume's notes at Exodus 21:12–32, John 8:1–11, Romans 13:1–7 (no position stated) |

### Other divisive positions
| Category | Position | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Scripture and Tradition | Not adjudicated; the volume prints and annotates the Apocrypha with an introduction that explains the Reformation dispute historically ("disputes over the doctrine of purgatory and of the usefulness of prayers and masses for the dead involved the authority of 2 Maccabees"); at Mark 7:8–9 "for Jesus, the written law takes precedence over its interpretation, not the other way around"; 2 Thessalonians 2:15's traditions are "the things told to them by Paul and his companions verbally and in writing." The essay "The Canons of the Bible" sets out the Jewish, Catholic, Orthodox and Protestant canons side by side. | Volume's "Introduction to the Apocryphal/Deuterocanonical Books"; essay "The Canons of the Bible"; notes at Mark 7:1–23, 2 Thessalonians 2:13–17 |
| The Trinity and the person of Christ | Described as later development, not affirmed in the text: "the Gk pronouns translated he and him could also be rendered it, referring to the logos," and "was with God … was God, poetic expression of the relationship between the divine logos and God. It anticipates language of unity with distinction" (John 1:1–3); Colossians 1:15–20 is "an early Christian hymn, probably based on the Jewish figure Wisdom/Sophia"; 1 Peter 1:2 is "a proto-Trinitarian formula"; "Christian interpreters have often seen the 'Spirit' of the Trinity" in Genesis 1:2, but "wind of God fits the ancient context better"; Hebrews' "high Christology… contributed to the emergence of Trinitarian theology." | Volume's notes at John 1:1–18, Colossians 1:15–20, 1 Peter 1:2, Genesis 1:2; Hebrews introduction |
| Predestination and free will | Paul's language explained, the later debate not entered: "foreknew … predestined, see Eph 1.4. Though from a human perspective God's intention… can be conceived as being prior in time, Paul affirms that there was never a moment when this was not God's will" (Romans 8:29); "election, that is, God's freedom of choice"; "loved and hated, that is, chosen and not chosen" (9:11–13); "chose us, the language of Israel's election as God's people (Deut 7.6–8) is used here for followers of the Christian movement" (Ephesians 1:4). | Volume's notes at Romans 8:26–30, 9:6–18, Ephesians 1:3–14 |
| Sanctification and holiness | Not treated as doctrine: "sanctification, holiness, which is the goal of being made righteous through faith; the daily task of those who are justified/made righteous" (Romans 6:19); Philippians 2:12 and 1 Thessalonians 5:23 carry no doctrinal gloss. | Volume's notes at Romans 6:1–23, 1 Thessalonians 5:23–28 |
| Hell, judgment and the fate of the lost | Gehenna explained, the doctrine left to the reader: hell is "lit., 'Gehenna,' a valley in Jerusalem… used in Jesus's day as a trash heap. Metaphorically understood as a place of eternal punishment" (Mark 9:43); Matthew 25:46 receives no gloss, the note dwelling on the sheep and goats' fabrics; "the second death, see 2.11n.; Mt 25.41" (Revelation 20:14); 2 Thessalonians 1:8 "vengeance, better, 'justice.'" Universalism and annihilationism are not named. | Volume's notes at Mark 9:42–50, Matthew 5:22, 25:31–46, Luke 16:19–31, Revelation 20:11–15, 2 Thessalonians 1:3–12 (no position stated) |
| Mary, the saints and prayer for the dead | Historical reserve: Matthew 1:18–25 "indicates that Mary was a virgin and Joseph was not his father. This apparent difficulty is resolved by Joseph's formal adoption of Jesus"; at Mark 6:3 Jesus's brothers and sisters are cross-referenced without comment on their relation to Mary; at John 19:26–27 the beloved disciple "is given care over Jesus's mother and becomes a member of the family"; 2 Maccabees 12:43–45 — "the author expounds upon Judas's care for the dead as evidence of his belief that their lives did not end with death." Veneration, perpetual virginity and purgatory are not discussed as doctrines (purgatory is mentioned once, historically, in the Apocrypha introduction). | Volume's notes at Matthew 1:18–25, 12:46–50, Mark 6:1–6, Luke 1:26–38, John 19:25–27, 2 Maccabees 12:32–45 |
| Worship: day, style and liturgy | Early practice described: "the first day of the week, Sunday" (Acts 20:7); "the Lord's day, the weekly day of Christian worship, Sunday" (Revelation 1:10); "the breaking of bread… describes both a common Christian meal… and the Lord's Supper" (Acts 2:42); Colossians 2:16's "festivals, new moons, or sabbaths, a summary of the Jewish religious calendar"; Romans 14 — "from the perspective of 'the strong' it is weak to be scrupulous about diet and calendar observances." No position on Sabbath or liturgy. | Volume's notes at Acts 2:42–47, 20:7–38, Revelation 1:9–20, Colossians 2:16–23, Romans 14:1–12 |
| Church and state / political engagement | Reported with critical distance: "Instituted by God, Paul claims that God is the one who set up the governing authorities, an idea that would be surprising to those authorities who believed their powers were ordained by the Roman gods" (Romans 13:1); in 1 Peter "the Roman Empire, the institution of slavery, and the patriarchal family are accepted as the reality of the existing social order. They are given no divine legitimization, however, but are 'human institutions' that are soon to pass away." | Volume's notes at Romans 13:1–7, 1 Peter 2:11–3:12 |
| Race, ethnicity and the church | Racist readings repudiated: Genesis 9:18–29 "was once widely misread as describing a 'curse of Ham' justifying slavery of African peoples, Noah's curse here is actually directed at Canaan, a figure not seen as an ancestor of African peoples"; Galatians 3:28 "insists that participation 'in Christ' obviates" the old distinctions; Ephesians 2:14 — "Jews and Gentiles united in a new community." | Volume's notes at Genesis 9:18–29, Galatians 3:26–29, Ephesians 2:11–22, Acts 17:16–34 |
| Creation and the age of the earth | Ancient cosmogony, source-critical: "this Priestly account of creation presents God as a king, creating the universe by decree in six days and resting on the seventh"; "in either case, the text does not describe creation out of nothing (contrast 2 Macc 7.28)" (Genesis 1:1); "like Gen 2.4–6 and other ancient cosmogonies, Gen 1.2 begins with description of how things were before creation"; Genesis 2:4b–25 is "this non-Priestly Yahwistic tradition… different from 1.1–2.3"; the flood "is quite close in many respects to Mesopotamian flood stories found in Atrahasis and Gilgamesh"; Genesis 3 "is often taken by Christians as an account of 'original sin,' [but] the word 'sin' never occurs in it." The age of the earth and evolution are not mentioned. | Volume's notes at Genesis 1:1–2:3, 2:4b–25, 3:1–24, 5:1–32, 6:5–8:19; "Introduction to the Pentateuch" |
| Tithing and the prosperity gospel | Not addressed as a question: Malachi 3:8–10 is explained from Leviticus 27 and Numbers 18, "a matter of concern during the Persian period"; "tithe, see Lev 27.30; Deut 14.22–23" (Matthew 23:23); 2 Corinthians 9:7's "not under compulsion bears overtones of the Stoic doctrine of liberty." Prosperity teaching is not mentioned. | Volume's notes at Malachi 3:6–12, Matthew 23:23, 2 Corinthians 9:1–15, Genesis 14:20 (no position stated) |
| Bible translations and KJV-onlyism | Built on the NRSV, with the committee's own "To the Reader" and the editors' essay "Translations of the Bible into English" tracing the history from Wycliffe and the KJV through the RSV and NRSV; the essay "Textual Criticism" explains the manuscript basis. KJV-onlyism has no standing. | Volume's front matter ("To the Reader"); essays "Translations of the Bible into English," "Textual Criticism" |
| Marriage roles: complementarian and egalitarian | The household codes criticized as patriarchal: in Ephesians 5:22–33 "female subordination within marriage is legitimized by an extended theological analogy… This passage, by granting complete authority to the male and encouraging female passivity, has in the past been used to suggest that Christian women should remain in abusive marital relationships"; Colossians 3:18–4:1 "defends [the patriarchal family] as appropriate for a community dedicated to Christ… appeal to these rules throughout Christian history has tended to reinforce rather than challenge male authority"; Genesis 2:18's "helper need not imply a subordinate status"; 1 Corinthians 11:11–12 — "in the Lord there is mutuality and reciprocity between woman and man." | Volume's notes at Ephesians 5:22–6:9, Colossians 3:18–4:1, 1 Peter 3:1–7, 1 Corinthians 11:2–16, Genesis 2:4b–25 |

## 27. Logos Complete Study Bible

**Translation used:** American Standard Version (ASV), 1901
**Publisher:** Logos International, published 1972; based on the earlier "Cross-Reference Bible" (1910, edited by J.W. Monser). No second edition was ever produced, and the publisher itself ceased operations not long after this volume's release, making it very much a historical curiosity today rather than an actively marketed product -- reflected in its placement at the end of this list -- though it remains the one identifiable dedicated study Bible built specifically on the ASV among the sources checked.
**Scale:** Roughly 100,000 cross-references (more than many later study Bibles), topical articles and analyses distributed throughout the text, and variorum (alternative) readings drawn from the work of some 150 scholars.

### Editorial credit
Credited to Logos International as publisher rather than to a single named general editor; its content was substantially reprinted, with some material omitted, from the 1910 Cross-Reference Bible.

### Denominational leaning
Broadly evangelical and Protestant, cross-denominational so far as can be determined from available sources; given the volume's age, obscurity, and the publisher's own closure shortly after its release, this entry has not identified a specific confessional orientation beyond its general evangelical Protestant character.

### Doctrinal positions
| Category | Position |
|---|---|
| View of Scripture | Broadly evangelical Protestant, so far as available sources show; the volume's obscurity prevents a fuller characterization. |
| Salvation | Not addressed; no position could be identified from available sources. |
| Baptism | Not addressed; no position could be identified from available sources. |
| The Lord's Supper | Not addressed; no position could be identified from available sources. |
| Church government | Not addressed; no position could be identified from available sources. |
| Eschatology | Not addressed; no position could be identified from available sources. |
| Spiritual gifts | Not addressed; no position could be identified from available sources. |
| Women's ordination | Not addressed; no position could be identified from available sources. |
